# Daily Summary: 2025-06-03

### Measure gradients, not activations! Enhancing neuronal activity in deep reinforcement learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24061v1)
- **Authors**: Jiashun Liu, Zihao Wu, Johan Obando-Ceron, Pablo Samuel Castro, Aaron Courville, Ling Pan
- **Abstract**: Deep reinforcement learning (RL) agents frequently suffer from neuronal activity loss, which impairs their ability to adapt to new data and learn continually. A common method to quantify and address this issue is the tau-dormant neuron ratio, which uses activation statistics to measure the expressive ability of neurons. While effective for simple MLP-based agents, this approach loses statistical power in more complex architectures. To address this, we argue that in advanced RL agents, maintaining a neuron's learning capacity, its ability to adapt via gradient updates, is more critical than preserving its expressive ability. Based on this insight, we shift the statistical objective from activations to gradients, and introduce GraMa (Gradient Magnitude Neural Activity Metric), a lightweight, architecture-agnostic metric for quantifying neuron-level learning capacity. We show that GraMa effectively reveals persistent neuron inactivity across diverse architectures, including residual networks, diffusion models, and agents with varied activation functions. Moreover, resetting neurons guided by GraMa (ReGraMa) consistently improves learning performance across multiple deep RL algorithms and benchmarks, such as MuJoCo and the DeepMind Control Suite.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of neuronal activity loss in deep reinforcement learning (DRL) agents, which hinders their ability to adapt and learn continually. It argues that relying on activation-based metrics, like the τ-dormant neuron ratio (ReDo), becomes less effective in modern, complex DRL architectures (e.g., with residual connections, diffusion models) due to statistical mismatches. The authors propose GraMa (Gradient Magnitude Neural Activity Metric), a novel, architecture-agnostic metric that quantifies neuron-level learning capacity using gradient magnitudes instead of activations. They demonstrate that GraMa accurately identifies persistent neuron inactivity across diverse architectures and that resetting neurons guided by GraMa (ReGraMa) consistently improves learning performance on benchmarks like MuJoCo and the DeepMind Control Suite.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**  The paper's core novelty lies in shifting the focus from activation-based measures of neuron inactivity to gradient-based measures. The insight that gradient magnitude is a better indicator of learning capacity than activation magnitude in complex architectures is well-argued and supported by empirical evidence.  While neuron resetting isn't a new concept (ReDo already exists), the gradient-based approach and its application to complex DRL architectures are a significant step forward. Specifically, the limitations with activity regularization based on ReLU activations in complex networks (Residual, Normalization, MoE) and the introduction of a new method that is more network architecture agnostic is an incremental but relevant advancement for the field of DRL.

**Significance:** The problem of neuronal inactivity is a significant bottleneck in DRL, especially for continual and transfer learning. GraMa and ReGraMa offer a practical and lightweight solution to mitigate this issue, potentially leading to more robust and adaptable DRL agents. The paper's findings are likely to be of interest to researchers working on continual learning, transfer learning, and improving the stability and performance of DRL algorithms in complex environments. The fact that it imposes negligible computational and memory overhead during the optimization pipeline makes it more practical in real-world DRL training scenarios, that are resource-intensive.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies and articulates the limitations of existing activation-based metrics in modern DRL architectures.
*   **Well-Motivated Approach:** The gradient-based approach is intuitively justified and supported by both theoretical arguments and empirical evidence.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper provides a thorough evaluation of GraMa and ReGraMa across diverse architectures, benchmarks, and activation functions.
*   **Practicality:** GraMa is computationally lightweight and easy to implement, making it a valuable tool for practitioners.
*   **Solid Empirical Validation:** The experimental results convincingly demonstrate the effectiveness of ReGraMa in improving learning performance and stability.
*   **Reproducibility:** The authors provide detailed experimental setups and implementation details, enhancing the reproducibility of their work.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Theoretical Analysis:** While the paper provides an intuitive explanation for the effectiveness of GraMa, a more formal theoretical analysis could further strengthen its claims.
*   **Architecture focus**: Even though the authors suggest that the introduction of GraMa tackles the architectural limitations that ReDo presents in complex network settings, most of the architectures tested are based on the BRO-net architecture, which limits the conclusions of the work.
*   **Limited Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies could be more extensive to further understand the individual contributions of different components of ReGraMa.
*   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The robustness of ReGraMa to different hyperparameter settings (e.g., the threshold τ) could be explored more thoroughly. The experimental findings in the paper focus mainly on using similar hyperparameters that are used in ReDo, which is a limited scope that should be further validated.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to influence future research in DRL by:

*   Promoting the use of gradient-based metrics for assessing neuronal activity.
*   Facilitating the development of more robust and adaptable DRL algorithms.
*   Providing a practical tool for mitigating neuronal inactivity in complex DRL architectures.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field of DRL by addressing a significant problem with a novel and well-validated solution. While there are some limitations, the strengths of the paper outweigh the weaknesses, making it a valuable addition to the literature. It provides a new perspective and a practical tool that can be used to improve the performance and stability of DRL agents in complex environments. This warrants a score of 7, reflecting its meaningful novelty and potential impact.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### TCM-Ladder: A Benchmark for Multimodal Question Answering on Traditional Chinese Medicine
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24063v1)
- **Authors**: Jiacheng Xie, Yang Yu, Ziyang Zhang, Shuai Zeng, Jiaxuan He, Ayush Vasireddy, Xiaoting Tang, Congyu Guo, Lening Zhao, Congcong Jing, Guanghui An, Dong Xu
- **Abstract**: Traditional Chinese Medicine (TCM), as an effective alternative medicine, has been receiving increasing attention. In recent years, the rapid development of large language models (LLMs) tailored for TCM has underscored the need for an objective and comprehensive evaluation framework to assess their performance on real-world tasks. However, existing evaluation datasets are limited in scope and primarily text-based, lacking a unified and standardized multimodal question-answering (QA) benchmark. To address this issue, we introduce TCM-Ladder, the first multimodal QA dataset specifically designed for evaluating large TCM language models. The dataset spans multiple core disciplines of TCM, including fundamental theory, diagnostics, herbal formulas, internal medicine, surgery, pharmacognosy, and pediatrics. In addition to textual content, TCM-Ladder incorporates various modalities such as images and videos. The datasets were constructed using a combination of automated and manual filtering processes and comprise 52,000+ questions in total. These questions include single-choice, multiple-choice, fill-in-the-blank, diagnostic dialogue, and visual comprehension tasks. We trained a reasoning model on TCM-Ladder and conducted comparative experiments against 9 state-of-the-art general domain and 5 leading TCM-specific LLMs to evaluate their performance on the datasets. Moreover, we propose Ladder-Score, an evaluation method specifically designed for TCM question answering that effectively assesses answer quality regarding terminology usage and semantic expression. To our knowledge, this is the first work to evaluate mainstream general domain and TCM-specific LLMs on a unified multimodal benchmark. The datasets and leaderboard are publicly available at https://tcmladder.com or https://54.211.107.106 and will be continuously updated.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TCM-Ladder, a novel multimodal benchmark dataset for evaluating large language models (LLMs) in the domain of Traditional Chinese Medicine (TCM). The dataset is designed to address the limitations of existing evaluation datasets, which are often text-based and lack comprehensive coverage of TCM's core disciplines. TCM-Ladder incorporates text, images, and videos, spanning fundamental theory, diagnostics, herbal formulas, internal medicine, surgery, pharmacognosy, and pediatrics. The authors present a suite of tasks, including single-choice, multiple-choice, fill-in-the-blank, diagnostic dialogue, and visual comprehension. Additionally, the paper proposes a new evaluation metric, Ladder-Score, specifically designed for TCM question answering, which assesses the accuracy and completeness of TCM terminology usage and semantic accuracy.  The authors also train reasoning models on TCM-Ladder and compare the performance of state-of-the-art general and TCM-specific LLMs.  They make the dataset and leaderboard publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The creation of a multimodal benchmark explicitly focused on TCM is a significant step forward. While some prior datasets touched on TCM, they were often smaller, less comprehensive, lacked multimodality or had limitations in their construction or accessibility. TCM-Ladder appears to fill a crucial gap by offering a more robust and standardized evaluation framework. The Ladder-Score metric is also a novel contribution, specifically tailored to address the nuances of TCM terminology. The paper explicitly acknowledges existing datasets and how TCM-Ladder differs from them, highlighting the additions of visual elements and comprehensive task types.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical need for objective and comprehensive evaluation of LLMs in the TCM domain. As LLMs become increasingly prevalent in healthcare, it's essential to have reliable benchmarks to assess their performance in specialized areas like TCM. The inclusion of multimodal data is particularly important, given that TCM diagnosis often involves visual and auditory information. By providing a high-quality, publicly available dataset, the authors enable further research and development in this field. Furthermore, the benchmark allows for a comparative assessment between general-purpose and TCM-specific models, revealing the potential benefits of specialized training.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Coverage:**  The dataset covers multiple core disciplines within TCM.
    *   **Multimodality:**  The inclusion of images and videos reflects the real-world diagnostic practices in TCM.
    *   **Standardized Evaluation:** The creation of Ladder-Score provides a domain-specific metric for more accurate performance measurement.
    *   **Publicly Available:**  The availability of the dataset and leaderboard promotes open research and collaboration.
    *   **Rigorous Data Validation:** The involvement of certified TCM practitioners in reviewing and validating the data ensures accuracy and clinical relevance.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Human Evaluation:** The human evaluation was conducted on only 20% of the test dataset and relied on only two TCM physicians. A more extensive human evaluation with more experts could provide a more robust estimation of human performance, which is useful for calculating model performance relative to human performance.
    *   **Modality Bias:** The paper doesn't explicitly discuss any potential biases in the modality distribution (e.g., a disproportionate number of text-based questions compared to visual questions). The current modality distribution might result in bias towards text processing for the LLMs in the evaluation.
    *   **English translation of dataset:** While the authors state the language is Chinese & English, this may be a weakness as TCM has its own terminology in Chinese, making translation and/or interpretation of the underlying concepts more complex. The authors would have to be very explicit about how the English version was created, and verified.
*   **Potential Impact:** The TCM-Ladder benchmark has the potential to significantly impact the development of TCM-specific LLMs. It can serve as a valuable resource for researchers and practitioners looking to train and evaluate models for various TCM applications, such as diagnosis, treatment planning, and knowledge retrieval. Also, because it is publicly available it will serve as a standard dataset for people to compare their results to.

**Score:** 8

**Rationale:**

The paper makes a substantial contribution by introducing a novel and comprehensive multimodal benchmark for evaluating LLMs in TCM. This addresses a significant gap in the field and provides a much-needed resource for researchers. The Ladder-Score metric adds further value by offering a domain-specific evaluation approach. However, some limitations exist, particularly with respect to human evaluation. The limitations are not severe enough to prevent it from having a significant impact on TCM research. This results in an "8" representing a very significant contribution, but not quite an outstanding one with broad, transformative implications.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### DSR-Bench: Evaluating the Structural Reasoning Abilities of LLMs via Data Structures
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24069v1)
- **Authors**: Yu He, Yingxi Li, Colin White, Ellen Vitercik
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed for real-world tasks that fundamentally involve data manipulation. A core requirement across these tasks is the ability to perform structural reasoning--that is, to understand and reason about data relationships. For example, customer requests require a temporal ordering, which can be represented by data structures such as queues. However, existing benchmarks primarily focus on high-level, application-driven evaluations without isolating this fundamental capability. To address this gap, we introduce DSR-Bench, a novel benchmark evaluating LLMs' structural reasoning capabilities through data structures, which provide interpretable representations of data relationships. DSR-Bench includes 20 data structures, 35 operations, and 4,140 problem instances, organized hierarchically for fine-grained analysis of reasoning limitations. Our evaluation pipeline is fully automated and deterministic, eliminating subjective human or model-based judgments. Its synthetic nature also ensures scalability and minimizes data contamination risks. We benchmark nine state-of-the-art LLMs. Our analysis shows that instruction-tuned models struggle with basic multi-attribute and multi-hop reasoning. Furthermore, while reasoning-oriented models perform better, they remain fragile on complex and hybrid structures, with the best model achieving an average score of only 47% on the challenge subset. Crucially, models often perform poorly on multi-dimensional data and natural language task descriptions, highlighting a critical gap for real-world deployment.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the DSR-Bench paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DSR-Bench, a novel benchmark specifically designed to evaluate the structural reasoning abilities of Large Language Models (LLMs).  DSR-Bench focuses on how well LLMs can manipulate and reason about data relationships represented through data structures (arrays, queues, trees, graphs, etc.). It includes 20 different data structures, 35 operations, and 4,140 problem instances, organized in a hierarchical manner to enable fine-grained analysis. The benchmark uses a fully automated and deterministic evaluation pipeline, eliminating subjective judgments and minimizing contamination risk through synthetic data generation. The authors evaluate nine state-of-the-art LLMs and identify key limitations, including struggles with multi-attribute reasoning, complex hybrid structures, spatial data, and natural language task descriptions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Targeted Evaluation:** The paper directly addresses a critical gap in LLM evaluation by focusing specifically on structural reasoning, a fundamental capability often overlooked in existing benchmarks. The choice of data structures as a lens for evaluation is insightful, as they offer a clean and interpretable way to probe LLMs' understanding of data relationships.
    *   **Fine-Grained Analysis:** The hierarchical organization of the benchmark allows for a granular analysis of LLM reasoning limitations, isolating specific sources of complexity that models struggle with. This is more useful than simply observing overall performance on a complex task.
    *   **Objective Evaluation:**  The fully automated and deterministic evaluation pipeline ensures objectivity and reproducibility. The synthetic nature of the data further reduces contamination risks.
    *   **Comprehensive Scope:** The breadth of data structures and operations included in DSR-Bench provides a comprehensive assessment of structural reasoning abilities. The extensions to spatial reasoning and natural language description are particularly valuable for practical application.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Synthetic Data Bias:**  While synthetic data minimizes contamination, it may also introduce biases. The skills required to perform well on DSR-Bench might not perfectly align with the structural reasoning demands of real-world applications using unstructured or noisy data.  LLMs might exploit patterns specific to the synthetic data generation process.
    *   **Limited Task Complexity:** The tasks, while diverse, are still relatively constrained in terms of complexity compared to real-world problems. The focus is on direct data structure manipulation, but many applications involve reasoning about how to *choose* and *combine* appropriate data structures for a given problem, a higher-level cognitive skill that DSR-Bench does not directly assess.
    *   **Evaluation Metric Limitations:** The strict accuracy metric, while objective, might not fully capture the nuances of reasoning. A model might make a small error in constructing the data structure but still demonstrate a reasonable understanding of the underlying relationships. Levenshtein distance is suggested as an auxiliary metric, but that too has limitations.
    *   **Natural language gap is under explored**: While they introduce the DSR-Bench-natural benchmark, the gap between formal and natural languages is not explored in much depth.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    The paper's primary novelty lies in the *focused evaluation of structural reasoning via data structures* in LLMs. Existing benchmarks often test reasoning indirectly through application-level tasks, but DSR-Bench isolates this core ability. The benchmark's hierarchical design, objective evaluation, and broad scope contribute to its significance as a valuable tool for researchers seeking to understand and improve LLM reasoning capabilities. It establishes a valuable framework for benchmarking, allowing future researchers to directly assess and measure progress.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    DSR-Bench has the potential to significantly influence the field by:

    *   **Guiding Model Development:**  Providing insights into specific weaknesses of LLMs regarding structural reasoning, guiding future research efforts toward addressing these limitations.
    *   **Informing Application Design:** Helping practitioners to better understand the capabilities and limitations of LLMs when applying them to tasks involving data manipulation.
    *   **Inspiring New Benchmarks:** Serving as a model for the creation of other targeted benchmarks that focus on specific cognitive abilities.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a valuable and novel contribution by providing a targeted benchmark for evaluating structural reasoning in LLMs. The carefully designed benchmark and objective evaluation pipeline are strong assets. However, the inherent limitations of synthetic data and somewhat constrained task complexity prevent it from being a truly exceptional contribution.  Further research should focus on bridging the gap between the synthetic tasks in DSR-Bench and the structural reasoning challenges encountered in real-world applications.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Principal Context-aware Diffusion Guided Data Augmentation for Fault Localization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24079v1)
- **Authors**: Shihao Fu, Yan Lei
- **Abstract**: Test cases are indispensable for conducting effective fault localization (FL). However, test cases in practice are severely class imbalanced, i.e. the number of failing test cases (i.e. minority class) is much less than that of passing ones (i.e. majority class). The severe class imbalance between failing and passing test cases have hindered the FL effectiveness. To address this issue, we propose PCD-DAug: a Principal Context-aware Diffusion guided Data Augmentation approach that generate synthesized failing test cases for improving FL. PCD-DAug first combines program slicing with principal component analysis to construct a principal context that shows how a set of statements influences the faulty output via statistical program dependencies. Then, PCD-DAug devises a conditional diffusion model to learn from principle contexts for generating synthesized failing test cases and acquiring a class balanced dataset for FL. We conducted large-scale experiments on six state-of-the-art FL approaches and compare PCD-DAug with six data augmentation baselines. The results show that PCD-DAug significantly improves FL effectiveness, e.g. achieving average improvements of 383.83%, 227.08%, and 224.19% in six FL approaches under the metrics Top-1, Top-3, and Top-5, respectively.
- **Summary**: **Summary:**

The paper introduces PCD-DAug, a novel Principal Context-aware Diffusion guided Data Augmentation approach designed to address the class imbalance problem in fault localization (FL). PCD-DAug combines dynamic program slicing with principal component analysis (PCA) to construct a 'principal context,' representing the statements that influence faulty outputs. This context guides a conditional diffusion model to generate synthetic failing test cases, thereby balancing the dataset used for FL.  The authors conduct experiments on six state-of-the-art FL approaches and compare PCD-DAug against six data augmentation baselines across multiple benchmarks. The results demonstrate significant improvements in FL effectiveness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*Novelty:* The paper's novelty lies in its integration of several existing techniques in a new and potentially effective manner for fault localization. While program slicing, PCA, diffusion models, and data augmentation have all been used individually or in combination in software engineering contexts, the specific way PCD-DAug brings them together seems unique. Specifically, the combination of program semantics (via slicing) with statistical data properties (via revised PCA) to form a principal context is interesting. The adoption of diffusion models, especially with classifier-free guidance, offers a potential advantage over GANs and CVAEs in stability and training efficiency. The "revised PCA" warrants further scrutiny on whether it deviates significantly from standard PCA to justify the revised label.

*Significance:* The class imbalance problem is a well-recognized challenge in fault localization, and improved solutions are valuable. The experimental results suggest that PCD-DAug achieves substantial performance gains compared to existing data augmentation techniques, with improvements in Top-K metrics and reductions in statements requiring inspection (RImp). The experiments are comprehensive, covering multiple benchmarks and FL techniques, strengthening the credibility of the findings. The reported improvements justify the complexity of the approach. It's good that the code is released.

*Weaknesses:*

1.  *Revised PCA Justification:* The paper claims a revised PCA is used but does not provide sufficient detail about the revisions or motivate its use.  A clearer explanation of the "revised" aspects and how they improve upon standard PCA is needed.

2.  *Parameter Sensitivity:*  While the authors claim robustness by using the same hyper-parameters across all programs, the slight performance decrease on one dataset ("Mockito") suggests potential dataset-specific nuances. Further exploration of parameter sensitivity or adaptive parameter tuning could enhance the approach. Are all parameters default Diffusion Model parameters?

3.  *Scalability:* The experiments are conducted on relatively small to medium-sized programs. The scalability of PCD-DAug to very large, complex software systems needs to be investigated. The dynamic program slicing component, in particular, could become computationally expensive.

4.  *Threats to Internal Validity:*  While the benchmarks are standard, the implementation of the baselines should be double-checked for fairness. Small variations in implementation can significantly influence the results.

5.  *Theoretical Justification:* A deeper theoretical analysis of why PCD-DAug works could strengthen the paper.  Explaining the statistical properties learned by the diffusion model and how they relate to improved fault localization would be beneficial.

*Potential Influence:* If the approach scales well and proves robust across a wider range of programs, PCD-DAug could become a valuable tool for improving the effectiveness of fault localization techniques, particularly in scenarios with limited failing test cases. It would especially relevant in contexts where labelled test cases are expensive to acquire. The idea of combining semantic and statistical context for data augmentation is promising and could inspire future research in this area.

Score: 7.5

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and potentially significant approach to address a relevant problem in fault localization. The experimental results are compelling, demonstrating substantial performance improvements over several baselines. However, the lack of detailed explanation about the revised PCA, possible dataset-specific parameter choices, and scalability concerns hold it back from a higher score. The work demonstrates strong empirical improvements, but the contributions in theory and methodology, while present, need more development. Releasing the code is a plus, though. If the authors address these weaknesses in future work, the impact could be even greater.

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### ComposeAnything: Composite Object Priors for Text-to-Image Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24086v1)
- **Authors**: Zeeshan Khan, Shizhe Chen, Cordelia Schmid
- **Abstract**: Generating images from text involving complex and novel object arrangements remains a significant challenge for current text-to-image (T2I) models. Although prior layout-based methods improve object arrangements using spatial constraints with 2D layouts, they often struggle to capture 3D positioning and sacrifice quality and coherence. In this work, we introduce ComposeAnything, a novel framework for improving compositional image generation without retraining existing T2I models. Our approach first leverages the chain-of-thought reasoning abilities of LLMs to produce 2.5D semantic layouts from text, consisting of 2D object bounding boxes enriched with depth information and detailed captions. Based on this layout, we generate a spatial and depth aware coarse composite of objects that captures the intended composition, serving as a strong and interpretable prior that replaces stochastic noise initialization in diffusion-based T2I models. This prior guides the denoising process through object prior reinforcement and spatial-controlled denoising, enabling seamless generation of compositional objects and coherent backgrounds, while allowing refinement of inaccurate priors. ComposeAnything outperforms state-of-the-art methods on the T2I-CompBench and NSR-1K benchmarks for prompts with 2D/3D spatial arrangements, high object counts, and surreal compositions. Human evaluations further demonstrate that our model generates high-quality images with compositions that faithfully reflect the text.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "ComposeAnything: Composite Object Priors for Text-to-Image Generation" introduces a novel, training-free framework to enhance the compositional abilities of existing text-to-image (T2I) diffusion models. The core idea is to leverage Large Language Models (LLMs) with chain-of-thought reasoning to generate 2.5D semantic layouts from input text. These layouts consist of 2D object bounding boxes enriched with depth information and detailed captions. Based on the layout, a coarse composite image is created, which serves as a strong, interpretable prior, replacing stochastic noise initialization in diffusion models. A prior-guided diffusion module then utilizes object prior reinforcement and spatial-controlled denoising to guide the denoising process. The method achieves state-of-the-art results on compositional T2I benchmarks, demonstrating improvements in both visual quality and faithfulness to the input text, particularly for complex and surreal compositions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a well-integrated combination of techniques. The use of LLMs for generating structured 2.5D semantic layouts is not entirely novel, as previous works have utilized LLMs for layout generation. However, the enrichment of these layouts with depth information and their use as a direct prior for noise initialization within a diffusion model is a distinctive approach.  The prior-guided diffusion module, combining object prior reinforcement and spatially-controlled denoising, is also a novel component. The way the LLM and the diffusion model are integrated to guide the generation is also an important aspect of the novelty.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant challenge in T2I generation: effectively handling complex and novel object arrangements. The proposed framework offers a significant improvement over existing methods, especially those relying solely on 2D layouts, which often struggle with 3D positioning and coherence. By providing a more structured and interpretable prior, ComposeAnything facilitates the generation of higher-quality images that are more faithful to the input text. The improvements on existing benchmarks and positive human evaluations further strengthen the significance of the work. The training-free nature of the method is also a major advantage, as it allows it to be readily applied to existing T2I models without requiring extensive retraining.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Effective integration of LLMs and diffusion models.
    *   The 2.5D layout representation with depth information is beneficial.
    *   The prior-guided diffusion module is a well-designed component.
    *   Demonstrated state-of-the-art performance on challenging benchmarks.
    *   The training-free aspect makes it practical and adaptable.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The method's performance is dependent on the quality of the LLM-generated layouts. The paper acknowledges that errors in spatial layout generation can lead to failure, a weakness that future work could address by enhancing the robustness of the framework to such errors.
    *   Although prior information can improve image quality, it is stated in the paper that a fully incorrect prior leads to failure in image generation. This demonstrates that the prior information has to be at least roughly correct to ensure high-quality generation.
    *   As stated in the paper, one failure mode is the LLM for spatial layout generation. Thus, improving the LLM and generating more faithful layouts could further improve the generation quality.
    *   Computational overhead may be a concern. The need to first generate a composite prior and then use the diffusion model for generation may increase the overall computational cost. While the method is training-free, runtime efficiency could be a point to improve.

*   **Potential Influence:** This work is likely to influence future research in compositional T2I generation. It provides a strong foundation for developing more controllable and interpretable image generation systems. The idea of using LLMs to generate structured scene representations as priors for diffusion models is a promising direction that can be explored further. Other applications that can benefit from this architecture are synthetic data generation and robot vision.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of T2I generation. It addresses a key challenge and offers a practical, training-free solution that achieves state-of-the-art results. While the method is dependent on LLM-generated layouts and there are challenges in scaling prior information to image generation, it is a well-executed piece of research with clear strengths and demonstrated impact. It provides a strong basis for future work in this area, addressing limitations, and improving even further the quality of generated images.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### SkyLB: A Locality-Aware Cross-Region Load Balancer for LLM Inference
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24095v1)
- **Authors**: Tian Xia, Ziming Mao, Jamison Kerney, Ethan J. Jackson, Zhifei Li, Jiarong Xing, Scott Shenker, Ion Stoica
- **Abstract**: Serving Large Language Models (LLMs) efficiently in multi-region setups remains a challenge. Due to cost and GPU availability concerns, providers typically deploy LLMs in multiple regions using instance with long-term commitments, like reserved instances or on-premise clusters, which are often underutilized due to their region-local traffic handling and diurnal traffic variance. In this paper, we introduce SkyLB, a locality-aware multi-region load balancer for LLM inference that aggregates regional diurnal patterns through cross-region traffic handling. By doing so, SkyLB enables providers to reserve instances based on expected global demand, rather than peak demand in each individual region. Meanwhile, SkyLB preserves KV-Cache locality and a balanced load, ensuring cost efficiency without sacrificing performance. SkyLB achieves this with a cache-aware cross-region traffic handler and a selective pushing load balancing mechanism based on checking pending requests. Our evaluation on real-world workloads shows that it achieves 1.12-2.06x higher throughput and 1.74-6.30x lower latency compared to existing load balancers, while reducing total serving cost by 25%.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the "SkyLB: A Locality-Aware Cross-Region Load Balancer for LLM Inference" paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SkyLB, a locality-aware cross-region load balancer designed to improve the efficiency and reduce the cost of serving Large Language Models (LLMs) in multi-region deployments. It addresses the issue of underutilized GPU resources due to region-specific peak demand provisioning and diurnal traffic patterns. SkyLB achieves this by: (1) aggregating regional demand through cross-region traffic handling, (2) preserving KV-Cache locality using prefix-aware routing, and (3) mitigating load imbalances using a selective pushing mechanism based on pending requests. The evaluation on real-world workloads demonstrates higher throughput, lower latency, and reduced serving costs compared to existing load balancing approaches.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a practical system to tackle LLM inference challenges in multi-region environments. The main novelty lies in the *combination* of several techniques and their adaptation to the specific characteristics of LLM inference. Specifically, the synthesis of cross-region load balancing, KV-cache aware routing and pending request-aware selective pushing differentiates SkyLB from existing solutions. However, none of these elements are entirely novel in isolation. Cross-region routing in general is well-studied but hasn't seen significant use cases with traditional workloads because of latency cost (which this paper handles). KV-cache routing is present in existing load balancers for single regions, and selective pushing isn't entirely new, however there is novelty in how this is implemented. A central point is the system implementation is a solution to be used by the engineering community rather than a novel algorithm or theory.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper lies in its potential to reduce the operational costs and improve the performance of LLM inference at scale. By enabling providers to reserve instances based on global demand rather than regional peaks, SkyLB offers a substantial cost-saving opportunity. The techniques it employs balance KV Cache locality with overall load across regions without a centralized bottleneck. The empirical evaluation further strengthens the claims, showcasing significant improvements in throughput and latency.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Practical Focus:** The paper addresses a real-world problem faced by LLM providers with a practical, implementable solution.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The evaluation uses real-world workloads and benchmarks SkyLB against relevant baselines, strengthening the validity of the results.
    *   **Clear Design Rationale:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges, design choices, and trade-offs involved in building SkyLB.
    *   **Open Source Release:** Providing SkyLB to allow for future work and deployment of the software.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Incremental Novelty:** As mentioned above, the individual components of SkyLB are not entirely novel. The strength of the paper is in combining these elements effectively in the context of LLM inference.
    *   **Complexity:** SkyLB introduces a degree of complexity in terms of deployment and management compared to simpler load balancing approaches. The paper could provide more guidance on the operational aspects of SkyLB.
    *   **Limited Scope of Workloads:** The evaluation primarily focuses on multi-turn conversation and tree-of-thoughts workloads. Evaluating SkyLB with a broader set of LLM applications would further strengthen the results.
    *   **Cost Model Assumptions**: The paper assumes that users can easily migrate workloads across regions without impact on cost. The economic impact on the end user for multi-region routing should also be considered.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the design and deployment of LLM serving infrastructure. Its focus on cost efficiency and performance optimization is particularly relevant in the current landscape of resource-intensive LLMs. The cross-regional traffic handling approach could be adopted in other distributed AI systems beyond LLM inference.
    *   Additionally, as LLM compute becomes further commoditized, systems like SkyLB will become increasingly necessary to reduce the cost of operation.

**Justification for Score:**

While SkyLB's individual components may not be ground-breaking, their effective integration to address a crucial problem in LLM inference warrants a high score. The practical focus, comprehensive evaluation, and significant performance gains demonstrate the value of the work. The primary drawback is the incremental nature of the novelty. Thus a high but not exceptional score is appropriate.

### Training LLMs for EHR-Based Reasoning Tasks via Reinforcement Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24105v1)
- **Authors**: Jiacheng Lin, Zhenbang Wu, Jimeng Sun
- **Abstract**: We present EHRMIND, a practical recipe for adapting large language models (LLMs) to complex clinical reasoning tasks using reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR). While RLVR has succeeded in mathematics and coding, its application to healthcare contexts presents unique challenges due to the specialized knowledge and reasoning required for electronic health record (EHR) interpretation. Our pilot study on the MEDCALC benchmark reveals two key failure modes: (1) misapplied knowledge, where models possess relevant medical knowledge but apply it incorrectly, and (2) missing knowledge, where models lack essential domain knowledge. To address these cases, EHRMIND applies a two-stage solution: a lightweight supervised fine-tuning (SFT) warm-up that injects missing domain knowledge, stabilizes subsequent training, and encourages structured, interpretable outputs; followed by RLVR, which reinforces outcome correctness and refines the model's decision-making. We demonstrate the effectiveness of our method across diverse clinical applications, including medical calculations (MEDCALC), patient-trial matching (TREC CLINICAL TRIALS), and disease diagnosis (EHRSHOT). EHRMIND delivers consistent gains in accuracy, interpretability, and cross-task generalization. These findings offer practical guidance for applying RLVR to enhance LLM capabilities in healthcare settings.
- **Summary**: Okay, I can provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Training LLMs for EHR-Based Reasoning Tasks via Reinforcement Learning".

**Summary:**

The paper introduces EHRMIND, a method for adapting large language models (LLMs) to complex clinical reasoning tasks using reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR). Recognizing that applying RLVR to healthcare faces unique challenges (specialized knowledge, EHR interpretation), the authors address two key failure modes: misapplied knowledge and missing knowledge. EHRMIND employs a two-stage approach: (1) supervised fine-tuning (SFT) to inject missing domain knowledge and (2) RLVR to reinforce outcome correctness and refine decision-making.  The method is evaluated on medical calculations (MEDCALC), patient-trial matching (TREC CLINICAL TRIALS), and disease diagnosis (EHRSHOT), demonstrating improved accuracy, interpretability, and cross-task generalization.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its practical adaptation of RLVR for EHR-based reasoning. While RLVR is established in areas like mathematics and coding, its direct application to healthcare is less explored. The identification and targeted mitigation of "misapplied" and "missing" knowledge are valuable contributions. The use of a simple SFT warm-up to bootstrap RLVR is also a practical and effective technique. However, the core RLVR concept is not new, and the SFT warm-up is a relatively straightforward extension.
*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its potential to enhance LLM capabilities in healthcare, a high-stakes domain requiring accuracy, transparency, and trust. The results on MEDCALC are particularly striking, demonstrating that even a relatively small (3B) model can outperform significantly larger proprietary models with the proposed method. The focus on interpretable reasoning paths is also highly relevant to clinical applications.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Practicality:** EHRMIND offers a concrete recipe for applying RLVR in a challenging domain.
    *   **Problem Formulation:** The identification of "misapplied" and "missing" knowledge is insightful and relevant.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The method is evaluated across diverse clinical applications, demonstrating its effectiveness.
    *   **Interpretability:** The emphasis on generating interpretable reasoning paths is a significant strength.
    *   The introduction of Pass@k as a proxy to decide SFT warm-up effectiveness is quite innovative.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited novelty of core RLVR:** The core RLVR component is well-established; the contribution is primarily in adaptation and application.
    *   **Dependency on rule-based rewards:**  The reliance on rule-based reward functions limits the applicability of the method to tasks where such rewards are readily available. Real-world clinical tasks may require more complex reward structures.
    *   **Limited discussion of data biases:** The paper acknowledges the importance of diverse training data, but doesn't deeply explore the potential impact of biases present in EHR datasets.
    *   The model selection strategy for choosing the checkpoints (based on the best validation performance) may need to be more rigorous. A proper hyperparameter tuning strategy (with suitable regularisation) can improve the model's performance.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's findings offer practical guidance for applying RLVR to enhance LLM capabilities in healthcare. The method could potentially improve the accuracy, interpretability, and reliability of LLM-based clinical decision support systems. The cross-task generalization results suggest that the learned reasoning patterns can be transferred to new clinical tasks.

**Justification of Score:**

EHRMIND provides a practical and effective method for adapting LLMs to EHR-based reasoning tasks, with promising results across several benchmarks. I have assigned a score of **7** because of the method's strengths (practicality, insights into failure modes, diverse evaluations, contribution to interpretability). Even though the core concept of RLVR isn't entirely new, the adaptation and empirical validation in a challenging clinical setting are significant. The limitations related to rule-based rewards, potential data biases, and absence of a good hyperparameter tuning strategy are important considerations to have in mind. Overall, the work offers a valuable contribution to the field, albeit with certain limitations that need to be further addressed in future research.

### R-KV: Redundancy-aware KV Cache Compression for Training-Free Reasoning Models Acceleration
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24133v1)
- **Authors**: Zefan Cai, Wen Xiao, Hanshi Sun, Cheng Luo, Yikai Zhang, Ke Wan, Yucheng Li, Yeyang Zhou, Li-Wen Chang, Jiuxiang Gu, Zhen Dong, Anima Anandkumar, Abedelkadir Asi, Junjie Hu
- **Abstract**: Reasoning models have demonstrated impressive performance in self-reflection and chain-of-thought reasoning. However, they often produce excessively long outputs, leading to prohibitively large key-value (KV) caches during inference. While chain-of-thought inference significantly improves performance on complex reasoning tasks, it can also lead to reasoning failures when deployed with existing KV cache compression approaches. To address this, we propose Redundancy-aware KV Cache Compression for Reasoning models (R-KV), a novel method specifically targeting redundant tokens in reasoning models. Our method preserves nearly 100% of the full KV cache performance using only 10% of the KV cache, substantially outperforming existing KV cache baselines, which reach only 60% of the performance. Remarkably, R-KV even achieves 105% of full KV cache performance with 16% of the KV cache. This KV-cache reduction also leads to a 90% memory saving and a 6.6X throughput over standard chain-of-thought reasoning inference. Experimental results show that R-KV consistently outperforms existing KV cache compression baselines across two mathematical reasoning datasets.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces R-KV, a novel redundancy-aware KV cache compression method designed to accelerate reasoning models (specifically, Chain-of-Thought models) without significant performance degradation. The core idea is to identify and selectively compress redundant tokens within the KV cache during inference, thereby reducing memory footprint and improving throughput. R-KV employs three key components: (1) importance scoring based on attention weights, (2) redundancy estimation via semantic similarity of key vectors, and (3) a joint selection strategy balancing importance and non-redundancy for eviction.  Experimental results on mathematical reasoning datasets (MATH-500 and AIME) demonstrate that R-KV can achieve near-lossless performance with significantly reduced KV cache size (10-34% of the original), outperforming existing KV cache compression baselines like SnapKV.  The paper also presents real-time throughput analysis demonstrating significant gains in batch size and throughput compared to FullKV and SnapKV, especially at longer sequence lengths. The method is training-free and model-agnostic, potentially making it suitable for various applications like reinforcement learning and LLM serving.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates a clear, well-motivated innovation by focusing on redundancy-aware KV cache compression specifically tailored for reasoning models. While KV cache compression is not entirely new, the focus on *reasoning models' inherent redundancy* and the combined approach of attention-based importance *and* semantic redundancy estimation makes it novel. Prior work primarily focuses on long input prompts but does not explore extensively for long generation outputs. Further, most approaches rely on the attention mechanism for importance and fail in reasoning models where the self-verification steps also generate high attention signals.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant challenge in deploying large reasoning models: the growing memory demands due to long, often redundant, CoT outputs. Successfully reducing memory footprint and improving inference speed without sacrificing accuracy has significant practical implications. LLM serving and RL rollout processes could benefit considerably from such an optimization. The method's training-free and model-agnostic nature further enhances its significance, providing a versatile solution readily applicable to existing models. The results are solid, and the method allows for larger batches to be processed than previously.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Motivation:** The paper provides a strong rationale for addressing redundancy in reasoning models.
    *   **Technically Sound:** The proposed R-KV method seems technically solid, integrating attention-based importance, semantic redundancy estimation, and joint selection in a reasonable way.
    *   **Empirically Validated:** The paper presents comprehensive experimental results on well-known benchmark datasets, demonstrating the effectiveness of R-KV compared to baselines.
    *   **Thorough Analysis:** The paper includes detailed analysis of memory savings, throughput improvements, and parameter sensitivity.
    *   **Well-Written and Organized:** The paper is generally well-written and organized, making it easy to follow the proposed method and experimental results.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Dataset Diversity:** While the mathematical reasoning datasets are appropriate, evaluating R-KV on other reasoning tasks (e.g., commonsense reasoning, logical inference) could further strengthen the paper.
    *   **Comparative Analysis:** The baselines employed include a well-known method. However, more recent works in KV cache compression could provide a more comprehensive comparison.
    *   **Scalability:** The current evaluation is on moderately-sized models (Llama3-8B, and Qwen-14B). Evaluating R-KV on much larger LLMs (e.g., 70B+ parameter models) and more complex reasoning tasks would demonstrate its scalability.
    *   **Complexity Concerns:** While the paper mentions complexity, a deeper dive into the computational overhead of importance and redundancy scoring, especially at very large sequence lengths, would be beneficial.

*   **Potential Influence:** R-KV has the potential to influence the development of more efficient reasoning models and facilitate their deployment in resource-constrained environments. The focus on redundancy could inspire other researchers to explore similar techniques in different contexts. The training-free and model-agnostic nature of R-KV makes it highly accessible and potentially widely adopted.

**Justification for Score:**

Despite some minor weaknesses, the paper presents a novel, well-executed, and empirically validated approach to addressing a critical challenge in deploying reasoning models. The focus on redundancy and the resulting performance improvements, particularly in throughput, are significant. While further evaluation on more diverse datasets and larger models would be beneficial, the current results strongly support the claims made in the paper.

- **Score**: 7/10

### AMSbench: A Comprehensive Benchmark for Evaluating MLLM Capabilities in AMS Circuits
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24138v1)
- **Authors**: Yichen Shi, Ze Zhang, Hongyang Wang, Zhuofu Tao, Zhongyi Li, Bingyu Chen, Yaxin Wang, Zhiping Yu, Ting-Jung Lin, Lei He
- **Abstract**: Analog/Mixed-Signal (AMS) circuits play a critical role in the integrated circuit (IC) industry. However, automating Analog/Mixed-Signal (AMS) circuit design has remained a longstanding challenge due to its difficulty and complexity. Recent advances in Multi-modal Large Language Models (MLLMs) offer promising potential for supporting AMS circuit analysis and design. However, current research typically evaluates MLLMs on isolated tasks within the domain, lacking a comprehensive benchmark that systematically assesses model capabilities across diverse AMS-related challenges. To address this gap, we introduce AMSbench, a benchmark suite designed to evaluate MLLM performance across critical tasks including circuit schematic perception, circuit analysis, and circuit design. AMSbench comprises approximately 8000 test questions spanning multiple difficulty levels and assesses eight prominent models, encompassing both open-source and proprietary solutions such as Qwen 2.5-VL and Gemini 2.5 Pro. Our evaluation highlights significant limitations in current MLLMs, particularly in complex multi-modal reasoning and sophisticated circuit design tasks. These results underscore the necessity of advancing MLLMs' understanding and effective application of circuit-specific knowledge, thereby narrowing the existing performance gap relative to human expertise and moving toward fully automated AMS circuit design workflows. Our data is released at https://huggingface.co/datasets/wwhhyy/AMSBench
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "AMSbench: A Comprehensive Benchmark for Evaluating MLLM Capabilities in AMS Circuits":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces AMSbench, a new benchmark suite specifically designed to evaluate the performance of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) in the domain of Analog/Mixed-Signal (AMS) circuit design. The benchmark covers three key areas: schematic perception, circuit analysis, and circuit design, encompassing a diverse set of tasks and difficulty levels. The authors evaluate several prominent MLLMs, both open-source and proprietary, using AMSbench and demonstrate the limitations of current MLLMs, especially in complex reasoning and circuit design tasks, highlighting the need for further research to improve their circuit understanding and application. The dataset is publicly released to foster research in this area.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the creation of a comprehensive, multimodal benchmark tailored specifically for evaluating MLLMs in the AMS circuit domain. While individual tasks related to circuit design have been explored previously, AMSbench's holistic approach, encompassing perception, analysis, and design, is a significant contribution. The emphasis on multimodal data (schematics, text, charts) is also a key aspect of its novelty.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial gap in the application of MLLMs to AMS circuit design, a field where automation has remained a persistent challenge due to the complexity and multi-modal nature of the data. By providing a standardized benchmark, AMSbench facilitates more systematic and rigorous evaluation of MLLM capabilities, allowing researchers to track progress and identify areas for improvement. The results are informative, identifying specific weaknesses of existing models such as inaccurate netlist generation and limited understanding of circuit trade-offs. This clear identification of limitations is crucial for guiding future research directions. The open release of the dataset further enhances the paper's significance by enabling reproducibility and wider community involvement.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Scope:** Covers three major aspects of AMS circuit design: perception, analysis, and design.
    *   **Multimodal Data:** Integrates diverse data modalities (schematics, text, tables).
    *   **Systematic Evaluation:** Evaluates a range of state-of-the-art MLLMs.
    *   **Clear Identification of Limitations:** Highlights specific areas where MLLMs struggle.
    *   **Publicly Released Dataset:** Fosters reproducibility and community contribution.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Design Task Complexity:** While the inclusion of design tasks is commendable, the paper acknowledges that even the best models struggled significantly.  The complexity of these tasks might currently be beyond the reach of most MLLMs, potentially limiting the benchmark's immediate usefulness for evaluating design capabilities.  It may be beneficial to consider more intermediate level design tasks.
    *   **Limited Depth of Analysis:** While the paper provides a good overview of the model's performance, a deeper qualitative analysis of *why* certain models fail on specific tasks could strengthen the findings. Case studies help, but more detailed error analysis would improve the insights gained.
    *   **Metric Limitations**: The "Syntax@5" and "Metric@5" metrics used to evaluate the testbench design might be insufficient or incomplete. More specific, nuanced metrics related to power consumption, area efficiency, etc., might be needed to evaluate the effectiveness of the generated testbenches.

*   **Potential Influence:** AMSbench has the potential to significantly influence the direction of MLLM research in EDA, specifically for AMS circuits. It will likely become a standard benchmark for evaluating new models and techniques. It can also inspire the creation of more targeted training datasets and architectures for MLLMs designed for circuit design applications.

*   **Rigorous Rationale:** The scoring has taken into account the specific strengths and weaknesses of the work. Although there is still room to enhance design task difficulty and provide deeper qualitative insights, AMSbench provides an excellent foundation for evaluating MLLMs in the AMS field. Its influence on the community will likely be considerable, providing a solid benchmark for future development.

### S4-Driver: Scalable Self-Supervised Driving Multimodal Large Language Modelwith Spatio-Temporal Visual Representation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24139v1)
- **Authors**: Yichen Xie, Runsheng Xu, Tong He, Jyh-Jing Hwang, Katie Luo, Jingwei Ji, Hubert Lin, Letian Chen, Yiren Lu, Zhaoqi Leng, Dragomir Anguelov, Mingxing Tan
- **Abstract**: The latest advancements in multi-modal large language models (MLLMs) have spurred a strong renewed interest in end-to-end motion planning approaches for autonomous driving. Many end-to-end approaches rely on human annotations to learn intermediate perception and prediction tasks, while purely self-supervised approaches--which directly learn from sensor inputs to generate planning trajectories without human annotations often underperform the state of the art. We observe a key gap in the input representation space: end-to-end approaches built on MLLMs are often pretrained with reasoning tasks in 2D image space rather than the native 3D space in which autonomous vehicles plan. To this end, we propose S4-Driver, a scalable self-supervised motion planning algorithm with spatio-temporal visual representation, based on the popular PaLI multimodal large language model. S4-Driver uses a novel sparse volume strategy to seamlessly transform the strong visual representation of MLLMs from perspective view to 3D space without the need to finetune the vision encoder. This representation aggregates multi-view and multi-frame visual inputs and enables better prediction of planning trajectories in 3D space. To validate our method, we run experiments on both nuScenes and Waymo Open Motion Dataset (with in-house camera data). Results show that S4-Driver performs favorably against existing supervised multi-task approaches while requiring no human annotations. It also demonstrates great scalability when pretrained on large volumes of unannotated driving logs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces S4-Driver, a self-supervised end-to-end motion planning algorithm for autonomous driving using multimodal large language models (MLLMs). S4-Driver addresses a key limitation in previous MLLM-based approaches: the mismatch between 2D image-based pretraining and the 3D space of autonomous driving.  It proposes a novel sparse volume representation to transform visual features from perspective views to a 3D space, enabling better prediction of planning trajectories. The method is trained using only raw sensor data and ego-vehicle trajectories, without human annotations. The approach is evaluated on nuScenes and a larger, in-house Waymo Open Motion Dataset (WOMD), showing favorable performance compared to supervised multi-task approaches.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the sparse volume representation used to bridge the gap between 2D pretrained MLLMs and 3D autonomous driving environments. Transforming perspective-view features to a 3D representation is not entirely new; however, the specific sparse volume strategy coupled with a frozen vision encoder from an existing MLLM represents a significant engineering contribution.  It avoids the need for retraining the entire vision encoder, making it scalable. The integration of this representation into a self-supervised MLLM framework is also a novel combination.
* **Significance:** The significance stems from several factors. First, it explores self-supervised learning for end-to-end autonomous driving, which has the potential to alleviate the dependence on expensive human annotations. Second, the demonstrated scalability with large volumes of driving data (WOMD) shows promise for real-world deployment. Third, achieving state-of-the-art performance on standard benchmarks without human annotations is a significant accomplishment. While some existing works employ supervised methods for perception pretraining and rely on object tracks as inputs, S4-Driver only uses camera images as inputs, which is compelling.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Scalability:** The paper convincingly demonstrates the scalability of the approach by leveraging a significantly larger (WOMD) dataset for pretraining.
    * **Self-Supervision:** The end-to-end self-supervised approach simplifies the system architecture and reduces the need for human-annotated data.
    * **Performance:** The algorithm achieves compelling performance on both nuScenes and WOMD, demonstrating its effectiveness.
    * **Clear Explanation and Ablation Studies:** The method and design choices are well explained, and thorough ablation studies are presented to justify each component of S4-Driver.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Dependency on pretrained MLLMs:** While leveraging pretrained MLLMs is a strength, the performance is still inherently limited by the capabilities of the base model (PaLI).  Future performance relies on continued advancement in the pretraining of foundation models.
    * **3D Scene Understanding:** It is worth noting that recent results show the limitations of MLLMs in 3D spatial reasoning, which is also an issue for this paper. S4-driver addresses the challenge of 3D reasoning by lifting pretrained 2D MLLMs to a 3D representation, however, it will still lack a more native 3D foundation.
    * **Heuristic high-level behaviors:** The high-level behavior command is determined by heuristic rules, which could limit the model's ability to learn more complex behaviors that are not easily categorized.
    * **Limited evaluation on safety-critical behavior:** Safety-critical behavior like collision avoidance or handling rare but dangerous edge cases is not specifically addressed, which is a crucial consideration for autonomous driving.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper can influence the field by providing a new direction for MLLM-based autonomous driving – one that emphasizes self-supervision, 3D representation, and scalability. The sparse volume representation could be adopted or adapted by other researchers. The use of the WOMD for evaluation is also a welcome addition, as it provides a more realistic and challenging benchmark.
The method's clear design and convincing experimental validation provide a solid foundation for future research.

**Justification:**

While the core idea of using MLLMs for autonomous driving isn't brand new, the specific way the authors tackled the issue of 2D vs. 3D representation, combined with the focus on self-supervision and the compelling experimental results, constitutes a valuable contribution. It addresses a significant bottleneck (the need for human annotations) and shows a promising path forward.  However, some limitations remain regarding high-level behavior recognition, dependency on pretrained MLLMs, and a lack of concrete safety-critical scenario evaluation.

### CrossICL: Cross-Task In-Context Learning via Unsupervised Demonstration Transfer
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24143v1)
- **Authors**: Jinglong Gao, Xiao Ding, Lingxiao Zou, Bing Qin, Ting Liu
- **Abstract**: In-Context Learning (ICL) enhances the performance of large language models (LLMs) with demonstrations. However, obtaining these demonstrations primarily relies on manual effort. In most real-world scenarios, users are often unwilling or unable to provide such demonstrations. Inspired by the human analogy, we explore a new ICL paradigm CrossICL to study how to utilize existing source task demonstrations in the ICL for target tasks, thereby obtaining reliable guidance without any additional manual effort. To explore this, we first design a two-stage alignment strategy to mitigate the interference caused by gaps across tasks, as the foundation for our experimental exploration. Based on it, we conduct comprehensive exploration of CrossICL, with 875 NLP tasks from the Super-NI benchmark and six types of LLMs, including GPT-4o. Experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of CrossICL and provide valuable insights on questions like the criteria for selecting cross-task demonstrations, as well as the types of task-gap-induced interference in CrossICL.
- **Summary**: Here is a concise summary and a critical evaluation of the paper "CrossICL: Cross-Task In-Context Learning via Unsupervised Demonstration Transfer":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a new paradigm called CrossICL for in-context learning (ICL). Instead of requiring task-specific demonstrations, CrossICL leverages demonstrations from different, existing tasks to improve the performance of large language models (LLMs). The approach is based on the idea that human analogy can benefit from different tasks, unlike current ICL methods that are limited to analogies within the same task. The authors propose a two-stage alignment strategy: (1) minimum gap selection to choose source task demonstrations with the smallest gap to the target task and (2) progressive task adaptation to transform the selected demonstrations further. They empirically evaluate CrossICL on a large set of NLP tasks from the Super-NI benchmark using various LLMs, including GPT-4o, and provide insights on demonstration selection criteria and interference types.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper addresses a significant practical limitation of ICL: the need for manually-provided demonstrations, which can be a barrier to adoption in many real-world scenarios. The idea of leveraging demonstrations from *different* tasks is a novel and potentially important direction for ICL research. It moves beyond simply automating or generating same-task demonstrations.

**Strengths:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of cross-task demonstration transfer for ICL is a compelling new approach.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The authors conduct extensive experiments on a large benchmark (Super-NI) and various LLMs.
*   **Practical Value:** The approach eliminates the need for manual demonstrations, making ICL more accessible and practical.
*   **Detailed Analysis:** The paper includes in-depth analysis of factors affecting performance, including demonstration selection criteria and types of cross-task interference.
*   **Well-Defined Methodology:** The proposed two-stage alignment strategy provides a clear framework for cross-task transfer.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Complexity of Implementation:** The two-stage alignment strategy, while providing a clear framework, might add to the complexity of the implementation compared to standard ICL. The adaptation step relies on LLMs to transform demonstrations, which introduces an added layer of inference and therefore a possibility of error.
*   **Reliance on LLM Capabilities:** The success of the approach relies heavily on the ability of LLMs to perform well on both tasks involved and to effectively transform and adapt existing demonstrations. LLMs that are not able to perform well on the target task will limit the applicability of the method.
*   **Potential for Negative Transfer:** Despite the authors' efforts, there's a potential risk of "negative transfer" if the source task demonstrations are too dissimilar or if the adaptation process is not effective. However, the quantitative impact of this is minimized by minimum gap selection and adaptation.
*   **Incremental Improvement:** While CrossICL consistently outperforms the baselines, the improvement observed on powerful LLMs like GPT-4o is not as large. This suggests that these more advanced models may already be capturing some of the benefits of cross-task transfer implicitly.

**Significance:**

The paper is significant because it opens up new avenues for applying ICL in scenarios where manual demonstrations are not feasible. By successfully demonstrating cross-task transfer, the authors have shown the potential of LLMs to generalize and learn from diverse sources of information. This can lead to more efficient and adaptable ICL systems. The detailed analysis of interference and selection criteria provides valuable insights for future research in this area.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel approach to ICL with practical implications. The extensive evaluation, detailed analysis, and insights on factors affecting performance make it a solid contribution. Although there are certain limitations associated with complexity and model reliance, the overall concept presents a major improvement over manual demonstrations in ICL and opens up a promising research direction. For its impact on improving the applicability of ICL, its thorough evaluation, and analysis, I assign this paper a score of 7.
