Further experiments on a wider range of tasks (e.g., style transfer, creative writing) would strengthen the claims of generalizability.
    *   **Annotator Expertise:** The study uses professional annotators, and while that assures quality, it may also limit the generalizability to tasks where crowd-sourcing is used. The impact of different annotator skills on annotation quality using IFS needs more exploration.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by providing a more principled and effective approach to preference annotation. The open-source release of tools and algorithms will likely facilitate adoption and further research. The demonstrated benefits in downstream model performance suggest that the IFS framework could contribute to the development of better-aligned and more reliable LLMs.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the above analysis, I'm assigning a score of **7**. Here's why:

*   The paper tackles a *highly relevant* and *significant* problem in LLM training.
*   The use of IFS is a *novel* application of a well-established mathematical framework.
*   The *experimental validation* provides solid evidence of the benefits of the proposed approach.
*   The *practical considerations* and implementation details enhance the paper's potential impact.
*   However, the *novelty is somewhat incremental*, and the paper could benefit from *more theoretical depth* and *broader experimental validation*.

Therefore, while the paper does not present a completely revolutionary idea, it provides a valuable and well-executed contribution to the field of LLM training by addressing a critical bottleneck with a novel and practical approach. The solid empirical results and practical considerations warrant a score of 7.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Aligning Protein Conformation Ensemble Generation with Physical Feedback
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24203v1)
- **Authors**: Jiarui Lu, Xiaoyin Chen, Stephen Zhewen Lu, Aurélie Lozano, Vijil Chenthamarakshan, Payel Das, Jian Tang
- **Abstract**: Protein dynamics play a crucial role in protein biological functions and properties, and their traditional study typically relies on time-consuming molecular dynamics (MD) simulations conducted in silico. Recent advances in generative modeling, particularly denoising diffusion models, have enabled efficient accurate protein structure prediction and conformation sampling by learning distributions over crystallographic structures. However, effectively integrating physical supervision into these data-driven approaches remains challenging, as standard energy-based objectives often lead to intractable optimization. In this paper, we introduce Energy-based Alignment (EBA), a method that aligns generative models with feedback from physical models, efficiently calibrating them to appropriately balance conformational states based on their energy differences. Experimental results on the MD ensemble benchmark demonstrate that EBA achieves state-of-the-art performance in generating high-quality protein ensembles. By improving the physical plausibility of generated structures, our approach enhances model predictions and holds promise for applications in structural biology and drug discovery.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Energy-based Alignment (EBA), a novel framework for improving protein conformation ensemble generation using diffusion models. EBA integrates physical feedback by aligning generated conformations with the underlying physical energy landscapes, effectively calibrating the model to balance conformational states based on their energy differences. The method fine-tunes a pre-trained all-atom denoising diffusion model to achieve this alignment. The authors validate EBA on the ATLAS MD ensemble benchmark, demonstrating state-of-the-art performance in generating high-quality protein ensembles compared to existing generative models that incorporate physical feedback.  The core idea is to implicitly learn an energy function by aligning the model's learned distribution with ratios derived from a classical force field, avoiding the computational complexities of directly sampling from a Boltzmann distribution.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of aligning generative models with physical energy feedback using an energy-weighted classification objective is novel. While existing methods explore similar themes, EBA's approach to incorporating Boltzmann factors without explicitly calculating the partition function is a significant contribution.  The specific implementation using AlphaFold3 and diffusion models builds upon existing work but the alignment objective itself is a key differentiator. The derivation connecting it to DPO is also a plus.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical challenge in protein dynamics modeling: generating physically plausible and thermodynamically accurate conformation ensembles.  By improving the physical plausibility of generated structures, EBA has the potential to enhance model predictions and benefit applications in structural biology and drug discovery. Specifically, the improvements in capturing long-range interactions by exposing buried residues and improvements to solvent are promising.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Principled approach:** The EBA objective is well-motivated and derived from first principles, demonstrating a clear connection between generative modeling and physical energy landscapes. The method also provides theoretical connections between DPO (Direct Preference Optimization) with this model.
    *   **Strong experimental results:** The experiments on the ATLAS MD ensemble benchmark convincingly demonstrate the effectiveness of EBA, achieving state-of-the-art performance compared to existing generative models with physical feedback. Ablation studies further support the contribution of the different components of the method. Specifically, the consistent improvements over a pre-trained diffusion module, and over EBA-DPO is great.
    *   **Scalability:** The stochastic mini-batch approximation of the partition function makes EBA scalable to large protein structures, overcoming limitations of existing amortized sampling methods. Specifically, the relatively small runtime per sample is notable.
    *   **Clear and well-written:** The paper is well-structured, clearly explains the methodology, and provides a thorough discussion of the results and limitations.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependence on force fields:**  The accuracy of EBA depends on the quality of the force field used for calculating the potential energy. The results could be sensitive to the choice of force field, and the current work does not extensively explore this dependency. Using *better* force fields can directly impact the results.
    *   **Limited Scope:** The study is limited to generating single-chain protein ensembles. Extending EBA to multi-chain complexes and other biomolecular systems would further increase its impact.
    *   **Theoretical complexity:** While the underlying idea is intuitive, the derivation can be dense, which might hinder accessibility for some readers. Some parts of the appendix can benefit from further simplification.

*   **Potential influence:** EBA has the potential to influence the field by providing a more effective way to incorporate physical feedback into generative models for protein dynamics. This could lead to more accurate and realistic simulations of protein behavior, benefiting applications in drug discovery, protein engineering, and basic biological research. The approach can also be used more generally, for example when some part of the generative model is physically plausible (such as electrostatics), whereas other parts may not be, such as how it models covalent bonds.

**Justification for Score:**

Overall, the paper presents a significant contribution to the field of protein dynamics modeling. The EBA framework is novel, well-motivated, and demonstrated to be effective through rigorous experimental validation. While the method has some limitations, its strengths outweigh its weaknesses, and it has the potential to influence future research in this area. The scalability and results are promising.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: q-bio.BM
- **Score**: 8/10

### STORK: Improving the Fidelity of Mid-NFE Sampling for Diffusion and Flow Matching Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24210v1)
- **Authors**: Zheng Tan, Weizhen Wang, Andrea L. Bertozzi, Ernest K. Ryu
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models (DMs) have demonstrated remarkable performance in high-fidelity image and video generation. Because high-quality generations with DMs typically require a large number of function evaluations (NFEs), resulting in slow sampling, there has been extensive research successfully reducing the NFE to a small range (<10) while maintaining acceptable image quality. However, many practical applications, such as those involving Stable Diffusion 3.5, FLUX, and SANA, commonly operate in the mid-NFE regime (20-50 NFE) to achieve superior results, and, despite the practical relevance, research on the effective sampling within this mid-NFE regime remains underexplored. In this work, we propose a novel, training-free, and structure-independent DM ODE solver called the Stabilized Taylor Orthogonal Runge--Kutta (STORK) method, based on a class of stiff ODE solvers with a Taylor expansion adaptation. Unlike prior work such as DPM-Solver, which is dependent on the semi-linear structure of the DM ODE, STORK is applicable to any DM sampling, including noise-based and flow matching-based models. Within the 20-50 NFE range, STORK achieves improved generation quality, as measured by FID scores, across unconditional pixel-level generation and conditional latent-space generation tasks using models like Stable Diffusion 3.5 and SANA. Code is available at https://github.com/ZT220501/STORK.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces STORK (Stabilized Taylor Orthogonal Runge-Kutta), a novel training-free ODE solver designed to improve the fidelity of diffusion and flow matching models within the mid-NFE (Number of Function Evaluations) regime (20-50 NFEs). Unlike previous fast sampling methods like DPM-Solver which rely on the semi-linear structure of the DM ODE, STORK leverages a class of stiff ODE solvers with Taylor expansion adaptation, making it applicable to both noise-based and flow matching-based models.  The authors demonstrate improved generation quality (FID scores) on unconditional pixel-level and conditional latent-space generation tasks using models such as Stable Diffusion 3.5 and SANA, particularly within the 20-50 NFE range.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the specific application of stabilized Runge-Kutta methods with Taylor expansion adaptations to diffusion model sampling, particularly targeting the under-explored mid-NFE range. While SRK methods themselves aren't brand new in numerical analysis, their application to diffusion models and adaptation for efficiency (reducing the required NFEs) is a contribution. The Taylor expansion strategy to approximate derivatives to decrease NFE count is also a significant practical contribution. The structure-independent nature of the solver, applicable to both noise-based and flow-based models, sets it apart from methods like DPM-Solver that rely on a semi-linear ODE structure.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the work stems from the practical observation that many high-performing generative models operate in the mid-NFE regime for a balance of speed and quality, and the existing fast sampling techniques are primarily optimized for extremely low NFE.  STORK's improvement in image quality within this mid-NFE range has real-world implications for applications where speed and fidelity both matter. The experiments demonstrate tangible improvements in FID scores compared to existing methods, particularly for flow-based models where DPM-solver might not be directly applicable without approximations. The experiments involving Stable Diffusion and SANA, commonly used models, makes the finding relevant and practically impactful.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem statement: Identifies the gap in the mid-NFE sampling regime.
    *   Technically sound:  Well-motivated method grounded in numerical analysis.
    *   Empirically validated: Demonstrates improvements in FID scores across various models and datasets.
    *   Structure-independent: Applicable to a broader range of diffusion/flow models.
    *   Training-free: Does not require retraining the diffusion model.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The paper could provide more theoretical analysis of the stability and convergence properties of STORK, especially regarding the interplay between the SRK method and the Taylor approximation.  While Theorem 1 provides order of convergence, a tighter bound on the error or a more detailed analysis of the effect of the Taylor expansion on the overall convergence would be more insightful.
    *   The parameter tuning of the sub-step count 's' is presented as empirically determined. A more systematic approach to selecting 's' or guidelines based on problem characteristics would enhance the method's usability.
    *   The experiments, while comprehensive, primarily rely on FID scores.  Including perceptual metrics or human evaluation studies could strengthen the case for improved perceptual quality.

*   **Potential Influence:** STORK has the potential to influence the design and application of diffusion models by offering a more efficient sampling method for the mid-NFE regime. It also encourages exploration of numerical methods beyond those solely optimized for extremely low NFE counts. Its structure-independent nature could also make it a valuable tool for researchers working with flow-matching models.

**Score:** 7.5

**Rationale:** The paper offers a significant, albeit incremental, advance in diffusion model sampling. It addresses a practical need by improving the fidelity of generation in the mid-NFE range, where many real-world models operate. The novelty lies in the specific combination of stabilized Runge-Kutta methods with Taylor approximations adapted for this domain. The gains, as demonstrated through FID scores, are significant enough to warrant attention. However, the paper could benefit from a more in-depth theoretical analysis, a more systematic approach to parameter tuning, and additional perceptual evaluations. The score reflects a solid contribution with clear practical value and potential for further research, but falling short of a truly groundbreaking impact.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Benchmarking Foundation Models for Zero-Shot Biometric Tasks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24214v1)
- **Authors**: Redwan Sony, Parisa Farmanifard, Hamzeh Alzwairy, Nitish Shukla, Arun Ross
- **Abstract**: The advent of foundation models, particularly Vision-Language Models (VLMs) and Multi-modal Large Language Models (MLLMs), has redefined the frontiers of artificial intelligence, enabling remarkable generalization across diverse tasks with minimal or no supervision. Yet, their potential in biometric recognition and analysis remains relatively underexplored. In this work, we introduce a comprehensive benchmark that evaluates the zero-shot and few-shot performance of state-of-the-art publicly available VLMs and MLLMs across six biometric tasks spanning the face and iris modalities: face verification, soft biometric attribute prediction (gender and race), iris recognition, presentation attack detection (PAD), and face manipulation detection (morphs and deepfakes). A total of 41 VLMs were used in this evaluation. Experiments show that embeddings from these foundation models can be used for diverse biometric tasks with varying degrees of success. For example, in the case of face verification, a True Match Rate (TMR) of 96.77 percent was obtained at a False Match Rate (FMR) of 1 percent on the Labeled Face in the Wild (LFW) dataset, without any fine-tuning. In the case of iris recognition, the TMR at 1 percent FMR on the IITD-R-Full dataset was 97.55 percent without any fine-tuning. Further, we show that applying a simple classifier head to these embeddings can help perform DeepFake detection for faces, Presentation Attack Detection (PAD) for irides, and extract soft biometric attributes like gender and ethnicity from faces with reasonably high accuracy. This work reiterates the potential of pretrained models in achieving the long-term vision of Artificial General Intelligence.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a benchmark evaluation of zero-shot and few-shot performance of various publicly available Vision-Language Models (VLMs) and Multi-modal Large Language Models (MLLMs) across six biometric tasks: face verification, soft biometric attribute prediction (gender and race), iris recognition, presentation attack detection (PAD), and face manipulation detection (morphs and deepfakes). The authors evaluate 41 VLMs and show their potential in biometric tasks. The results show varying degrees of success, with some VLMs achieving impressive performance without fine-tuning (e.g., high TMR for face and iris verification). The paper explores how simple classifiers can enhance the performance of these models on tasks like deepfake detection, PAD, and soft biometric extraction. The work highlights the potential of pre-trained models for Artificial General Intelligence in biometrics.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its comprehensive, systematic evaluation of the zero-shot capabilities of a wide array of recent VLMs and MLLMs on a diverse set of biometric tasks. While prior work has explored the application of foundation models in narrower biometric settings (iris segmentation, face recognition with fine-tuning, etc.), this study provides a broad benchmark that allows for a direct comparison of different models' capabilities. The study's focus on *zero-shot* performance is a key aspect, as it investigates the inherent generalization abilities of these models, without task-specific training. The idea of using the raw outputs of these models directly for tasks like face recognition has been shown previously, but this paper is unique in its comparative analysis of multiple modern architectures across modalities.

*   **Significance:** The work is significant because it provides a baseline understanding of how well VLMs can generalize to biometric recognition and analysis tasks. This is crucial for several reasons: (1) Biometric data is often scarce and labeled data is expensive to obtain. VLMs can offer a way to circumvent the need for extensive task-specific training. (2) It helps identify which models are more suitable for specific biometric tasks without fine-tuning, saving computational resources. (3)The work can guide the development of more adaptable and scalable biometric systems. (4) The study also uncovers limitations of certain models, informing future research on architecture design and training strategies tailored to biometric-related challenges.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The benchmark covers a wide range of VLMs and biometric tasks, providing a thorough overview of the field.
    *   **Focus on Zero-Shot Performance:** The emphasis on zero-shot performance highlights the models' intrinsic generalization capabilities.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The findings offer practical insights for building scalable and adaptable biometric systems.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper presents the methodology, results, and analysis clearly.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Task Complexity:** The tasks are primarily recognition or classification-based. VLMs might have greater potential for more complex biometric tasks (e.g., age estimation with regression).
    *   **Classifier Head Simplicity:** The use of simple classifier heads might not fully exploit the representational power of the VLMs. More sophisticated classification techniques could further improve performance.
    *   **Bias and Fairness Not Addressed:** The study doesn't explicitly address potential biases in the VLMs or the impact on different demographic groups, which is a major concern in biometrics.
    *   **Dataset Limitations:** The results are dataset dependent. Generalizing these finding to new datasets needs to be validated.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper can serve as a starting point for future research on applying foundation models to biometrics, inspiring the development of specialized architectures, training strategies, and domain adaptation techniques. It highlights the promise and limitations of the current VLMs, encouraging researchers to address the identified gaps (bias, complex tasks, classifier design). The benchmark will be useful for comparing new models and approaches against existing baselines.

*   **Overall Assessment:** While the paper doesn't introduce a fundamentally new method or architecture, its systematic evaluation of a critical, evolving field (VLMs in biometrics) adds substantial value. The comprehensive analysis and the focus on zero-shot capabilities make it a significant contribution.

Score: 7.  The paper offers a well-executed empirical study that provides important insights into the capabilities and limitations of existing foundation models in biometrics. It has significant implications for future research and system design, and establishes valuable baselines. While it doesn't propose new models or solutions, the breadth and rigor of the evaluation make it a solid and significant contribution to the community. A more thorough discussion of bias and fairness and evaluation of more complex biometric tasks would have increased the score.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Semi-structured LLM Reasoners Can Be Rigorously Audited
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24217v1)
- **Authors**: Jixuan Leng, Cassandra A. Cohen, Zhixian Zhang, Chenyan Xiong, William W. Cohen
- **Abstract**: As Large Language Models (LLMs) become increasingly capable at reasoning, the problem of "faithfulness" persists: LLM "reasoning traces" can contain errors and omissions that are difficult to detect, and may obscure biases in model outputs. To address these limitations, we introduce Semi-Structured Reasoning Models (SSRMs), which internalize a semi-structured Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning format within the model. Our SSRMs generate reasoning traces in a Pythonic syntax. While SSRM traces are not executable, they adopt a restricted, task-specific vocabulary to name distinct reasoning steps, and to mark each step's inputs and outputs. Through extensive evaluation on ten benchmarks, SSRMs demonstrate strong performance and generality: they outperform comparably sized baselines by nearly ten percentage points on in-domain tasks while remaining competitive with specialized models on out-of-domain medical benchmarks. Furthermore, we show that semi-structured reasoning is more amenable to analysis: in particular, they can be automatically audited to identify reasoning flaws. We explore both hand-crafted structured audits, which detect task-specific problematic reasoning patterns, and learned typicality audits, which apply probabilistic models over reasoning patterns, and show that both audits can be used to effectively flag probable reasoning errors.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper.

**Summary**

The paper introduces Semi-Structured Reasoning Models (SSRMs) to address the "faithfulness" problem in large language models (LLMs). SSRMs are designed to produce reasoning traces in a Pythonic syntax, using a restricted vocabulary to name reasoning steps and mark inputs/outputs.  The key ideas are: 1) to train LLMs to generate these semi-structured traces, 2) to demonstrate their strong performance and generalization across multiple tasks, and 3) to develop both structured and typicality audits to automatically identify reasoning flaws in the generated traces.  The structured audits use task-specific rules, while typicality audits use probabilistic models of reasoning patterns to flag potentially erroneous reasoning steps. Experiments show SSRMs outperform comparable baselines and that the audit methods can effectively detect reasoning flaws and potentially improve performance. The code and data are publicly released.

**Critical Evaluation**

*Novelty:*

The idea of using semi-structured reasoning is not entirely new, as the authors acknowledge related work in Program Trace Prompting (PTP) and the broader area of program-aided language models. However, this paper presents a more comprehensive approach by *internalizing* the semi-structured format directly into the model training process (SFT and RL), rather than relying solely on few-shot prompting. The introduction of both structured and typicality audits is also a significant contribution, providing concrete methods for verifying and analyzing LLM reasoning.  The probabilistic approach to typicality audits, providing a quantifiable measure of a trace's similarity to expected patterns, is a particularly novel angle.

*Significance:*

The significance of the work lies in its focus on *auditing* LLM reasoning, which is a crucial step toward building trustworthy and reliable AI systems. The "faithfulness" problem is a major bottleneck in deploying LLMs in high-stakes applications. By enabling automated detection of reasoning flaws, this research offers a promising avenue for improving LLM reliability and reducing biases. While other work explores faithfulness through metrics or causality, this paper contributes a pragmatic approach using symbolic audits and probabilistic models applied to traces. The demonstration of strong performance across diverse tasks, including medical QA, further increases the practical significance of the work.

*Strengths:*

*   **Clear problem statement and well-defined approach:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of unfaithful reasoning and presents a structured methodology for addressing it.
*   **Comprehensive experimental evaluation:**  The experiments are thorough, covering a wide range of benchmarks and comparing against multiple baselines.  The inclusion of both in-domain and out-of-domain tasks strengthens the generality of the findings.
*   **Detailed analysis of results:** The paper provides insightful analysis of the different audit methods and their effectiveness in detecting reasoning flaws.  The correlation between audit failures and accuracy losses is a strong indicator of the practical value of the approach.
*   **Public release of code and data:** This greatly enhances the reproducibility and impact of the work, allowing other researchers to build upon the findings.

*Weaknesses:*

*   **Limited scale:** The study is limited to a 7B model size, which is relatively small compared to state-of-the-art LLMs. The generalizability of the results to larger models needs to be further investigated.
*   **Manual audit creation:** The structured audits are manually crafted, which limits their scalability and requires task-specific expertise. The authors acknowledge this and propose automatic audit generation as future work, but the current reliance on manual audits is a significant limitation.
*   **Typicality audit limitations:** While typicality audits are promising, they are essentially flagging *outliers* in reasoning patterns.  The authors note that, in some cases, highly typical reasoning traces can *still* be incorrect, particularly in tasks with a high error rate overall. This highlights the need for more sophisticated audit methods that go beyond simple pattern matching.
*   **Limited test-time integration:** The test-time-scaling with audits is still preliminary. The gains are not significant over standard greedy decoding which indicates some further refinement might be needed for that aspect of the method.

*Overall Assessment:*

Despite the limitations, this paper makes a valuable contribution to the field of LLM reasoning and trustworthiness. The idea of SSRMs and associated auditing methods represents a solid advancement. The comprehensive evaluation and detailed analysis provide strong evidence for the effectiveness of the proposed approach.  The focus on auditing is highly relevant and addresses a critical need in the development of reliable AI systems. The public release of code and data will further facilitate progress in this area.
Score: 7.5

*Rationale:*

A score of 7.5 reflects the significance and novelty of the work while acknowledging its limitations. The strengths of clear problem definition, thorough evaluation, and the introduction of structured and probabilistic auditing are strong positives. The limitations of model scale, manual audit creation, and preliminary test-time integration prevent a higher score, however, the potential for this work is significant, laying a foundation for more automated and scalable methods for auditing LLM reasoning.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Unleashing High-Quality Image Generation in Diffusion Sampling Using Second-Order Levenberg-Marquardt-Langevin
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24222v1)
- **Authors**: Fangyikang Wang, Hubery Yin, Lei Qian, Yinan Li, Shaobin Zhuang, Huminhao Zhu, Yilin Zhang, Yanlong Tang, Chao Zhang, Hanbin Zhao, Hui Qian, Chen Li
- **Abstract**: The diffusion models (DMs) have demonstrated the remarkable capability of generating images via learning the noised score function of data distribution. Current DM sampling techniques typically rely on first-order Langevin dynamics at each noise level, with efforts concentrated on refining inter-level denoising strategies. While leveraging additional second-order Hessian geometry to enhance the sampling quality of Langevin is a common practice in Markov chain Monte Carlo (MCMC), the naive attempts to utilize Hessian geometry in high-dimensional DMs lead to quadratic-complexity computational costs, rendering them non-scalable. In this work, we introduce a novel Levenberg-Marquardt-Langevin (LML) method that approximates the diffusion Hessian geometry in a training-free manner, drawing inspiration from the celebrated Levenberg-Marquardt optimization algorithm. Our approach introduces two key innovations: (1) A low-rank approximation of the diffusion Hessian, leveraging the DMs' inherent structure and circumventing explicit quadratic-complexity computations; (2) A damping mechanism to stabilize the approximated Hessian. This LML approximated Hessian geometry enables the diffusion sampling to execute more accurate steps and improve the image generation quality. We further conduct a theoretical analysis to substantiate the approximation error bound of low-rank approximation and the convergence property of the damping mechanism. Extensive experiments across multiple pretrained DMs validate that the LML method significantly improves image generation quality, with negligible computational overhead.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Levenberg-Marquardt-Langevin (LML), a novel training-free method to enhance diffusion model sampling. LML leverages a low-rank approximation of the diffusion Hessian geometry, inspired by the Levenberg-Marquardt optimization algorithm, to guide the Langevin update. This approach involves two key innovations: (1) a computationally tractable low-rank Hessian approximation that avoids quadratic complexity computations by capitalizing on the inherent structure of Diffusion Models (DMs); (2) a damping mechanism to stabilize the approximated Hessian for more accurate steps. Theoretical analyses support the approximation error bound and convergence properties. Experiments across multiple pretrained DMs demonstrate that LML improves image generation quality with negligible computational overhead.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its training-free approximation of the diffusion Hessian, making it computationally feasible to enhance DM sampling with second-order geometry. Drawing inspiration from the Levenberg-Marquardt optimization method and adapting it to the DM sampling context is clever. While Newton-Langevin methods exist, their direct application to high-dimensional DMs is impractical due to the computational costs. LML addresses this gap by providing a scalable approximation. The low-rank approximation is a reasonable approach to reducing the computational burden.

*   **Significance:** By making Hessian-guided sampling computationally viable, LML offers a practical way to improve the sample quality of pretrained diffusion models without retraining or extensive computational resources. The method is also very efficient. The experiments demonstrate tangible improvements across various datasets and models, indicating a broad applicability.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Practical Scalability:** The method's key strength is its ability to approximate the Hessian geometry in a computationally efficient and training-free manner, crucial for high-dimensional diffusion models.
    *   **Theoretical Justification:** Providing both an approximation error bound and a convergence analysis for the damping mechanism strengthens the method's validity.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** Experiments across diverse pretrained models demonstrate the effectiveness and generalizability of LML.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited exploration of Adaptive Parameters:** The paper does mention in the final section the possibility of adapting the damping coefficient over time, and acknowledges that more effective strategies for this may exist. Furthermore, the paper states in Section 5.5. Hyperparameters, "our LML introduces two robust hyperparameters" and in the second half of section 5.5 the paper suggests that the model will degenerate into the standard Langevin model. This would suggest that there is a limited amount of control in LML model to improve DMs.

*   **Potential Impact:** LML could influence future research by encouraging the exploration of second-order methods in diffusion model sampling. Its training-free nature makes it readily adoptable by practitioners working with existing pretrained models.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a clever adaptation of an existing optimization technique to a new problem domain (diffusion model sampling). It successfully addresses a key challenge (computational cost) that has hindered the use of second-order methods. The theoretical analysis and empirical validation are solid. However, the method's reliance on hyperparameter tuning and unexplored potential for more sophisticated rank approximations prevent it from achieving a higher score. It lacks a breakthrough advance but still offers a practically valuable contribution.

Score: 7.0

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Automated Structured Radiology Report Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24223v2)
- **Authors**: Jean-Benoit Delbrouck, Justin Xu, Johannes Moll, Alois Thomas, Zhihong Chen, Sophie Ostmeier, Asfandyar Azhar, Kelvin Zhenghao Li, Andrew Johnston, Christian Bluethgen, Eduardo Reis, Mohamed Muneer, Maya Varma, Curtis Langlotz
- **Abstract**: Automated radiology report generation from chest X-ray (CXR) images has the potential to improve clinical efficiency and reduce radiologists' workload. However, most datasets, including the publicly available MIMIC-CXR and CheXpert Plus, consist entirely of free-form reports, which are inherently variable and unstructured. This variability poses challenges for both generation and evaluation: existing models struggle to produce consistent, clinically meaningful reports, and standard evaluation metrics fail to capture the nuances of radiological interpretation. To address this, we introduce Structured Radiology Report Generation (SRRG), a new task that reformulates free-text radiology reports into a standardized format, ensuring clarity, consistency, and structured clinical reporting. We create a novel dataset by restructuring reports using large language models (LLMs) following strict structured reporting desiderata. Additionally, we introduce SRR-BERT, a fine-grained disease classification model trained on 55 labels, enabling more precise and clinically informed evaluation of structured reports. To assess report quality, we propose F1-SRR-BERT, a metric that leverages SRR-BERT's hierarchical disease taxonomy to bridge the gap between free-text variability and structured clinical reporting. We validate our dataset through a reader study conducted by five board-certified radiologists and extensive benchmarking experiments.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Structured Radiology Report Generation (SRRG), a new task that aims to convert free-text chest X-ray (CXR) reports into a standardized, structured format. The authors create a novel dataset from MIMIC-CXR and CheXpert Plus by using large language models (LLMs) to reformulate existing free-text reports following specific structural and content guidelines. They also introduce SRR-BERT, a fine-grained disease classification model with 55 labels, which enables more precise evaluation. Finally, they propose F1-SRR-BERT, a new metric leveraging SRR-BERT to better evaluate the generated structured reports. The paper validates the dataset and approach through a reader study by board-certified radiologists and extensive benchmarking experiments, showing improved consistency compared to existing free-form report generation methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the problem formulation itself: transforming free-text radiology reports into a structured format for both generation and evaluation. This is a significant shift from the standard approach. The dataset creation process, using LLMs to restructure existing data, is novel in this specific context, though LLM-based data augmentation or transformation is becoming more common. SRR-BERT, while building upon existing architectures, offers a more granular classification schema, which is a valuable contribution. F1-SRR-BERT is a natural extension given the SRRG task, offering a task-specific evaluation metric.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical limitation in the automated radiology report generation field: the lack of standardized reporting and evaluation. The structured format and the new metrics promise to enable more consistent and clinically meaningful report generation. The creation of a large-scale structured dataset will be beneficial for the community. The benchmarking demonstrates the challenges involved in this new task and provides a solid foundation for future research. The reader study adds clinical validation, strengthening the results.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear Problem Definition: The paper clearly defines the problems with free-text radiology reporting and presents a well-motivated solution.
    *   Novel Dataset: The SRRG dataset is a significant contribution to the field, filling a gap in structured radiology report data. The use of LLMs for data restructuring, while not completely groundbreaking, is a practical and effective approach.
    *   Fine-Grained Evaluation: The introduction of SRR-BERT and F1-SRR-BERT addresses the limitations of existing evaluation metrics, providing a more nuanced assessment of report quality. The hierarchical disease representation and the classification granularity of the model are a major strength.
    *   Clinical Validation: The reader study provides valuable clinical validation, increasing confidence in the quality of the structured reports and the annotations.
    *   Extensive Benchmarking: The paper presents comprehensive experimental results, comparing several existing models on the new task. This allows for a solid evaluation of the approach and provides benchmarks for future work.
    *  OOD Evaluation: Showing the performance of all methods on the HOPPR set (out-of-distribution) is a significant strength, offering insight into the generalizability of the proposed method.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   LLM-based Data Generation: Reliance on LLMs for dataset creation introduces a potential for biases and errors. While the reader study mitigates this, there's still a dependence on the LLM's performance and training data. The process is very expensive (in terms of both time and computational resources).
    *   Limited Scope of Evaluation: While F1-SRR-BERT is a valuable metric, it is still based on automated classification. The agreement of radiologists and the validity of findings can be further strengthened with a more comprehensive reader study involving a more nuanced set of tasks.
    *   Difficulty of One-to-One Label Mapping: The direct comparison of CheXbert and SRR-BERT is hampered by the difficulty in directly mapping the labels; this limitation is clearly acknowledged in the paper, however.
    *   In the end, although there is a structured format, there is a certain degree of freedom remaining. How to improve clarity further remains an open question.

*   **Potential Influence:** The SRRG task, dataset, and evaluation metrics have the potential to significantly impact the radiology report generation field. By promoting structured reporting, this work can lead to improved consistency, accuracy, and clinical communication, potentially reducing radiologists' workload and improving patient care. The increased ease in comparing different models is also valuable.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper is not revolutionary in terms of fundamental algorithmic breakthroughs, its practical contribution and influence on the field are substantial. The creation of a structured dataset and a clinically informed evaluation metric address critical needs in radiology report generation. The reader study, though limited in scope, is a valuable validation step. The systematic benchmarking is rigorous. The limitations are clearly acknowledged. Given the practical significance, the valuable resources contributed to the field, the potential impact, and clear problem formulation, I would assign this paper a score of **8**. The novelty is primarily in the task definition and dataset creation, rather than core technical innovations, and the paper has a very good potential influence on the field.

**Score: 8**
- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Reasoning Can Hurt the Inductive Abilities of Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24225v1)
- **Authors**: Haibo Jin, Peiyan Zhang, Man Luo, Haohan Wang
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown remarkable progress across domains, yet their ability to perform inductive reasoning - inferring latent rules from sparse examples - remains limited. It is often assumed that chain-of-thought (CoT) prompting, as used in Large Reasoning Models (LRMs), enhances such reasoning. We investigate this assumption with creating four controlled, diagnostic game-based tasks - chess, Texas Hold'em, dice games, and blackjack - with hidden human-defined rules. We find that CoT reasoning can degrade inductive performance, with LRMs often underperforming their non-reasoning counterparts. To explain this, we present a theoretical framework that reveals how reasoning steps can amplify error through three failure modes: incorrect sub-task decomposition, incorrect sub-task solving, and incorrect final answer summarization. Based on our theoretical and empirical analysis, we introduce structured interventions that adapt CoT generation according to our identified failure types. These interventions improve inductive accuracy without retraining. Our findings suggest that effective (CoT) reasoning depends not only on taking more steps but also on ensuring those steps are well-structured.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Reasoning Can Hurt the Inductive Abilities of Large Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper challenges the assumption that chain-of-thought (CoT) prompting, commonly used in Large Reasoning Models (LRMs), necessarily enhances inductive reasoning in Large Language Models (LLMs).  The authors create four controlled, diagnostic game-based tasks (chess, Texas Hold'em, dice games, blackjack) with hidden, human-defined rules. Surprisingly, they find that CoT reasoning can *degrade* inductive performance, with LRMs often underperforming their non-reasoning counterparts. They propose a theoretical framework explaining this degradation through three failure modes: incorrect sub-task decomposition, incorrect sub-task solving, and incorrect final answer summarization.  Based on this framework, they introduce structured interventions that adapt CoT generation to mitigate these failure types, improving inductive accuracy without retraining. The core message is that effective CoT reasoning depends not just on taking more steps, but on ensuring those steps are well-structured and avoid propagating errors.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The central finding that CoT can *hinder* inductive reasoning is novel and counter-intuitive. While previous works have observed limitations to reasoning depth, this paper goes further by demonstrating a performance *decrease* compared to models lacking reasoning capabilities. The formalization of reasoning failures into the three modes (decomposition, solving, summarization) offers a structured way to understand this phenomenon, going beyond simple empirical observations. This is relatively novel within the existing literature.

*   **Significance:** The significance lies in prompting a re-evaluation of how CoT and similar reasoning techniques are used. It suggests that blind application of CoT might be detrimental in certain contexts, particularly when dealing with inductive tasks where understanding latent rules is crucial. The interventions proposed, based on the identified failure modes, offer practical solutions for improving inductive reasoning in LRMs.
    *  The paper could have provided more concrete guidelines regarding the types of task (e.g. complexity, problem structure) for which guided/restricted reasoning approaches are likely to provide the most value.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Rigorous Experiment Design:** The creation of controlled game-based tasks is a strength, enabling isolation and evaluation of inductive reasoning abilities. The use of hidden rules adds complexity and mirrors real-world scenarios where underlying principles must be inferred.
    *   **Theoretical Foundation:** The theoretical framework provides a strong foundation for understanding the observed results and designing targeted interventions. The decomposition of reasoning into sub-tasks allows for a granular analysis of potential failure points.
    *   **Practical Interventions:** The proposed interventions are valuable because they offer practical solutions that can be implemented without retraining models. This makes the findings readily applicable to existing LRMs.
    *  **Strong Analysis of Solving Error subtype** - the detailed taxonomy of errors, such as *math overuse* and *hallucinated rules*, is extremely valuable in that it gives more granular insight as to how LLMs are reasoning improperly.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Task Domain:**  The evaluation is primarily focused on game-based tasks. While well-controlled, the generalizability of the findings to other domains (e.g., scientific discovery, financial analysis) could be further explored.
    *   **Simplifications in Theoretical Model:** The theoretical model makes certain simplifications (e.g., scalar alignment factor, independent noise) to maintain analytical tractability. While this is a common approach, it might limit the model's ability to capture all nuances of real-world LRM reasoning.
    *  **Evaluation Limitations** The prompts provided in Appendix are a useful start, but more detail and variety in the prompts used may have been necessary to rigorously assess and address the identified issues, such as a more robust approach to mitigating math overuse.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development and application of LRMs. It highlights the importance of carefully designing reasoning strategies to avoid introducing errors and suggests that structured, error-guided interventions can lead to more robust and accurate inductive reasoning. More work is needed to extend this analysis and intervention strategy into different domains.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**
The paper offers a novel and important insight: that CoT reasoning is not a universal solution and can, in fact, hinder inductive reasoning in LLMs. The rigorous experimental setup, coupled with a well-defined theoretical framework and practical interventions, strengthens the paper's contribution. However, the limited task domain and the simplifications in the theoretical model prevent it from achieving a higher score. It introduces useful structured decompositions of reasoning failures, but could have extended the robustness of its evaluations further. The paper is a solid contribution deserving wider circulation and further exploration within the field.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### E^2GraphRAG: Streamlining Graph-based RAG for High Efficiency and Effectiveness
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24226v2)
- **Authors**: Yibo Zhao, Jiapeng Zhu, Ye Guo, Kangkang He, Xiang Li
- **Abstract**: Graph-based RAG methods like GraphRAG have shown promising global understanding of the knowledge base by constructing hierarchical entity graphs. However, they often suffer from inefficiency and rely on manually pre-defined query modes, limiting practical use. In this paper, we propose E^2GraphRAG, a streamlined graph-based RAG framework that improves both Efficiency and Effectiveness. During the indexing stage, E^2GraphRAG constructs a summary tree with large language models and an entity graph with SpaCy based on document chunks. We then construct bidirectional indexes between entities and chunks to capture their many-to-many relationships, enabling fast lookup during both local and global retrieval. For the retrieval stage, we design an adaptive retrieval strategy that leverages the graph structure to retrieve and select between local and global modes. Experiments show that E^2GraphRAG achieves up to 10 times faster indexing than GraphRAG and 100 times speedup over LightRAG in retrieval while maintaining competitive QA performance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces E2GraphRAG, a streamlined graph-based retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) framework designed to improve both the efficiency and effectiveness of knowledge retrieval from long documents. E2GraphRAG combines aspects of both tree and graph-based structures.  In the indexing stage, it constructs a summary tree using an LLM for multi-granularity understanding and an entity graph using SpaCy for efficient fine-grained knowledge extraction.  Bidirectional indexes are created between entities and chunks to enable efficient lookup during retrieval. In retrieval, E2GraphRAG uses an adaptive strategy to choose between local (entity-focused) and global (summary tree) retrieval modes based on the density of entity connections within the graph.  Experiments demonstrate significant speedups in both indexing and retrieval compared to existing graph-based RAG methods, while maintaining competitive question answering performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the integration of several existing ideas into a more efficient and adaptable system. The key innovations are:
    *   **Hybrid Tree/Graph Structure:**  While other works have used trees or graphs individually, E2GraphRAG combines a summary tree for global context and an entity graph for fine-grained knowledge. This isn't entirely new, but the specific combination and interaction are a contribution.
    *   **SpaCy for Entity Extraction:**  The use of SpaCy for entity extraction, rather than relying solely on LLMs, significantly improves indexing efficiency. This is a good practical choice, but isn't a deep conceptual breakthrough.
    *   **Adaptive Retrieval Strategy:**  The dynamic selection between local and global retrieval modes based on entity graph structure is a valuable contribution, increasing flexibility and potentially improving retrieval quality. This reduces reliance on predetermined query modes.
    *   **Bidirectional Indexing:**  Building both entity-to-chunk and chunk-to-entity indexes enhance lookup during both local and global retrieval.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant problem in RAG: the high computational cost of graph-based methods, particularly for long documents. By significantly improving efficiency without sacrificing performance, E2GraphRAG makes graph-based RAG more practical for real-world applications. The focus on open-source models also broadens the accessibility and applicability.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Strong Experimental Results:** The paper presents compelling experimental results demonstrating substantial speedups compared to GraphRAG and LightRAG, while maintaining competitive or even superior accuracy.
    *   **Clear and Well-Structured:** The paper is generally well-written and clearly explains the E2GraphRAG framework and its components.
    *   **Focus on Efficiency:** The paper prioritizes and delivers on efficiency, which is a critical factor for the adoption of RAG systems.
    * **Adaptive retrieval:** The framework is able to make smarter decisions about retrieval using an automated approach.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Incremental Improvement:** While the combination of techniques is effective, individual components aren't radically novel.  The paper builds upon existing ideas rather than introducing fundamentally new concepts.
    *   **Limited Theoretical Analysis:**  The paper provides a cost analysis but lacks a deeper theoretical justification for the effectiveness of the adaptive retrieval strategy. More formal analysis of its decision-making process would be beneficial.
    *   **Dependency on SpaCy:** While SpaCy improves efficiency, the system is tied to its performance. SpaCy may not perform as well as LLMs in certain domains.
    *   **Parameter Sensitivity:** The system still involves several parameters (hop threshold *h*, the number of retrieved chunks *k*, and the grouping factor *g*) which require tuning and experimentation.

*   **Impact:** The paper's impact is likely to be moderate. It offers a practical and efficient improvement to graph-based RAG, making it more accessible.  However, it lacks the groundbreaking nature of a completely new RAG paradigm. The hybrid design and use of SpaCy is a great decision but is not fundamentally a groundbreaking concept.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a solid engineering contribution with a practical and efficient solution to the problem of efficient graph-based RAG.  While the individual components aren't revolutionary, their combination and the adaptive retrieval strategy provide a significant improvement in terms of speed and maintainability. It improves on existing research by providing a more realistic solution. Therefore, it warrants a score of 7, indicating a valuable contribution that advances the state-of-the-art in a meaningful way, though it doesn't represent a fundamental paradigm shift.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### ProofNet++: A Neuro-Symbolic System for Formal Proof Verification with Self-Correction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24230v1)
- **Authors**: Murari Ambati
- **Abstract**: We propose ProofNet++, a neuro-symbolic framework that enhances automated theorem proving by combining large language models (LLMs) with formal proof verification and self-correction mechanisms. Current LLM-based systems suffer from hallucinated logical steps and unverifiable reasoning. ProofNet++ mitigates these limitations by integrating symbolic proof tree supervision, a reinforcement learning loop using verifiers as reward functions, and an iterative self-correction module. Our experiments on miniF2F, Lean's mathlib, and HOL Light show that ProofNet++ significantly improves proof accuracy, correctness, and formal verifiability over prior models. We provide theoretical analysis of the convergence and stability of the verifier-guided RL framework and release our datasets and codebase for future research.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary of the paper followed by a critical evaluation and a score.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ProofNet++, a neuro-symbolic framework designed to improve automated theorem proving by integrating large language models (LLMs) with formal proof verification and self-correction mechanisms. The system addresses the limitations of current LLM-based theorem provers, which often generate hallucinated logical steps and unverifiable reasoning. ProofNet++ uses a verifier-in-the-loop reinforcement learning approach, curriculum learning over structured proof trees, and a self-correction loop to improve proof accuracy and formal verifiability. Experiments on miniF2F, Lean's mathlib, and HOL Light datasets demonstrate significant improvements over prior models. The paper provides both empirical results and theoretical analysis on the convergence and stability of the verifier-guided RL framework.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The core novelty lies in the tight integration of several techniques to address a specific problem in a high-stakes domain.  Prior works have explored LLMs for theorem proving, RL for guiding proof search, and self-correction.  However, ProofNet++ distinguishes itself by:

*   **Verifier-in-the-loop RL:**  The explicit use of a formal verifier as the environment in an RL setting is a good design choice that enables strong supervision during training. This significantly constraints the LLM to only generate valid proofs.
*   **Curriculum Learning on Proof Trees:**  Structuring the training data based on proof complexity is a sensible approach to help the LLM learn from simpler proofs before tackling more complex ones.
*   **Self-Correction Loop:**  The incorporation of an automatic error correction mechanism, leveraging the verifier's feedback, is a crucial component for improving the robustness of the system.

While individual components might not be entirely novel, their specific combination and application to the formal theorem proving domain, guided by the need for strict logical correctness, constitutes a significant advance.

**Significance:**

The significance of this work rests on its ability to bridge the gap between the flexible expressiveness of LLMs and the rigorous correctness requirements of formal verification systems. Potential impacts include:

*   **Improved Automated Theorem Proving:** Enhancing the capabilities of automated theorem provers could lead to progress in various areas of mathematics, computer science, and formal methods.
*   **Verified Program Synthesis:**  The ability to generate machine-checkable proofs has implications for creating verified software and hardware systems.
*   **AI Safety and Alignment:**  Applying formal methods to AI systems could improve their safety and trustworthiness, especially in critical domains.

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The experiments on diverse datasets (miniF2F, Lean's mathlib, HOL Light) provide a strong basis for evaluating the system's performance and generalization ability.
*   **Theoretical Analysis:**  The inclusion of theoretical analysis on the convergence and stability of the verifier-guided RL framework adds depth to the paper and supports the claims made.
*   **Clear Architecture and Methodology:** The paper clearly describes the architecture of ProofNet++ and the methodology used for training and evaluation.
*   **Error Analysis and Mitigation Strategies:** The detailed analysis of error modes (hallucinated lemmas, invalid topological order, etc.) and the implemented mitigation strategies demonstrates a thorough understanding of the challenges involved.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Computational Cost:** The verifier-guided RL approach is computationally expensive, limiting the scalability of the system. While the authors mention some optimizations, further improvements are needed.
*   **Loose Symbolic-Neural Coupling:** The current architecture parses LLM outputs into symbolic trees post-hoc, which results in a relatively loose coupling between the neural and symbolic components. Tighter integration could lead to further performance gains. The authors do suggest directions for tighter integration in future work.
*   **Limited Scope of Theoretical Analysis:** The theoretical analysis focuses primarily on the RL component. Further analysis of the end-to-end system's convergence and stability would strengthen the paper.
*   **Lack of Comparison to Llemma:** It would be very useful to see how ProofNet++ compares to Meta's Llemma model in terms of performance on the same benchmarks.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a well-designed and thoroughly evaluated neuro-symbolic system for formal proof verification. The integration of verifier-in-the-loop RL, curriculum learning, and a self-correction loop is a significant contribution.  The detailed error analysis and mitigation strategies further demonstrate the robustness of the approach. While the computational cost and relatively loose symbolic-neural coupling are weaknesses, the paper offers a solid foundation for future research in this area. The novelty is good, and the potential impact on automated theorem proving, verified program synthesis, and AI safety is significant.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 8/10

### MIRAGE: Assessing Hallucination in Multimodal Reasoning Chains of MLLM
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24238v2)
- **Authors**: Bowen Dong, Minheng Ni, Zitong Huang, Guanglei Yang, Wangmeng Zuo, Lei Zhang
- **Abstract**: Multimodal hallucination in multimodal large language models (MLLMs) restricts the correctness of MLLMs. However, multimodal hallucinations are multi-sourced and arise from diverse causes. Existing benchmarks fail to adequately distinguish between perception-induced hallucinations and reasoning-induced hallucinations. This failure constitutes a significant issue and hinders the diagnosis of multimodal reasoning failures within MLLMs. To address this, we propose the {\dataset} benchmark, which isolates reasoning hallucinations by constructing questions where input images are correctly perceived by MLLMs yet reasoning errors persist. {\dataset} introduces multi-granular evaluation metrics: accuracy, factuality, and LLMs hallucination score for hallucination quantification. Our analysis reveals that (1) the model scale, data scale, and training stages significantly affect the degree of logical, fabrication, and factual hallucinations; (2) current MLLMs show no effective improvement on spatial hallucinations caused by misinterpreted spatial relationships, indicating their limited visual reasoning capabilities; and (3) question types correlate with distinct hallucination patterns, highlighting targeted challenges and potential mitigation strategies. To address these challenges, we propose {\method}, a method that combines curriculum reinforcement fine-tuning to encourage models to generate logic-consistent reasoning chains by stepwise reducing learning difficulty, and collaborative hint inference to reduce reasoning complexity. {\method} establishes a baseline on {\dataset}, and reduces the logical hallucinations in original base models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MIRAGE, a new benchmark specifically designed to assess and isolate reasoning-induced hallucinations in multimodal large language models (MLLMs). It addresses the shortcomings of existing benchmarks that often conflate perception-induced and reasoning-induced hallucinations, hindering a thorough diagnosis of MLLM failures. MIRAGE constructs questions where input images are correctly perceived, allowing the focus to be solely on reasoning errors. The benchmark provides multi-granular evaluation metrics (accuracy, factuality, and an LLM hallucination score) to quantify hallucination levels.  The paper presents experimental results revealing that model scale, data scale, and training stages affect hallucination types.  Furthermore, spatial reasoning hallucinations remain a challenge.  To mitigate these issues, the authors propose Logos, a method combining curriculum reinforcement fine-tuning (CRFT) and collaborative hint inference (CHI) to encourage logic-consistent reasoning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in isolating reasoning-induced hallucinations, a significant improvement over existing benchmarks that often blend perceptual and reasoning errors. This allows for more targeted diagnosis and mitigation strategies. MIRAGE’s multi-granular evaluation is also a valuable contribution, providing a more comprehensive assessment than simple accuracy metrics. The proposed Logos method, leveraging CRFT and CHI, introduces a practical approach towards improving logical consistency. The combination of curriculum learning and collaborative hint inference isn't entirely new in the general machine learning landscape, but its specific application to multimodal reasoning hallucination is innovative.

*   **Significance:** Addressing hallucination in MLLMs is crucial for building trustworthy and reliable AI systems, especially in applications where accuracy is paramount (e.g., medical diagnosis, autonomous systems). By focusing on reasoning errors, MIRAGE offers a path to improve MLLM reasoning capabilities. The empirical analysis provides valuable insights into the factors influencing hallucination, offering guidance for future model development. The success of the Logos method, even as a baseline, indicates the potential for mitigation strategies that target logical consistency. However, the paper acknowledges limitations in handling temporal aspects or inter-image relations and the lack of theoretical insights. The paper only looks into MLLMs, it has no implication for visual-only AI model since MLLMs are used for the downstream task that requires the capability of reasoning.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Well-defined problem and clear motivation.
    *   Novel benchmark design with targeted hallucination isolation.
    *   Multi-granular evaluation metrics.
    *   Comprehensive experimental analysis with insightful findings.
    *   Practical mitigation method (Logos) showing promise.
    *   Publicly available benchmark enhances reproducibility and future research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limitations in scope (temporal aspects and inter-image relations).
    *   Lack of a theoretical understanding of reasoning hallucination.
    *   Baseline Mitigation method Logos.
    *   The experiments heavily rely on Qwen models, limiting generalizability. More comprehensive study on different models is encouraged.

*   **Potential Influence:** MIRAGE has the potential to become a widely used benchmark for evaluating and improving the reasoning capabilities of MLLMs.  The insights from the experiments could guide the development of more robust and trustworthy multimodal AI systems. The Logos method provides a solid starting point for research into mitigation strategies.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:** MIRAGE represents a significant and novel advancement in the evaluation of MLLMs by disentangling reasoning-induced hallucinations. The comprehensive benchmark design, multi-granular metrics, and empirical analysis provide valuable tools and insights for the community. While the paper acknowledges limitations in scope and theoretical understanding, its practical contributions and potential impact on the field justify a score of 8. The baseline mitigation method, while valuable, prevents an even higher score, indicating that the paper still offers space for follow-up research.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Advantageous Parameter Expansion Training Makes Better Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24241v1)
- **Authors**: Naibin Gu, Yilong Chen, Zhenyu Zhang, Peng Fu, Zheng Lin, Shuohuan Wang, Yu Sun, Hua Wu, Weiping Wang, Haifeng Wang
- **Abstract**: Although scaling up the number of trainable parameters in both pre-training and fine-tuning can effectively improve the performance of large language models, it also leads to increased computational overhead. When delving into the parameter difference, we find that a subset of parameters, termed advantageous parameters, plays a crucial role in determining model performance. Further analysis reveals that stronger models tend to possess more such parameters. In this paper, we propose Advantageous Parameter EXpansion Training (APEX), a method that progressively expands advantageous parameters into the space of disadvantageous ones, thereby increasing their proportion and enhancing training effectiveness. Further theoretical analysis from the perspective of matrix effective rank explains the performance gains of APEX. Extensive experiments on both instruction tuning and continued pre-training demonstrate that, in instruction tuning, APEX outperforms full-parameter tuning while using only 52% of the trainable parameters. In continued pre-training, APEX achieves the same perplexity level as conventional training with just 33% of the training data, and yields significant improvements on downstream tasks.
- **Summary**: This paper introduces Advantageous Parameter EXpansion Training (APEX), a novel training strategy for large language models (LLMs) that aims to improve performance without increasing the overall number of parameters.  The core idea is to identify a subset of parameters deemed "advantageous" based on their activation magnitudes, and then progressively expand these parameters into the space of "disadvantageous" ones during training. The authors claim this increases the proportion of impactful parameters, leading to enhanced training effectiveness.  They support their approach with theoretical analysis based on matrix effective rank, and demonstrate its efficacy through experiments on instruction tuning and continued pre-training, showing improved performance compared to various baselines, including parameter-efficient and full-parameter tuning methods.  Ablation studies are conducted to validate the importance of both the advantageous parameter expansion and the assessment method used to identify them.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Novel Idea:** The core concept of APEX – expanding a subset of advantageous parameters into a disadvantageous space – is interesting and intuitively appealing. It offers a fresh perspective on parameter allocation during LLM training, moving beyond simply scaling up the model size.
*   **Theoretical Justification:**  The paper attempts to provide a theoretical basis for APEX using the concept of matrix effective rank. While not deeply rigorous, it offers a plausible explanation for why expanding advantageous parameters could improve overall matrix utilization and model performance.
*   **Comprehensive Experiments:**  The experimental setup covers instruction tuning and continued pre-training, providing a decent evaluation of APEX's applicability across different training scenarios. The comparison against multiple baselines, including parameter-efficient and full-parameter tuning methods, strengthens the empirical validation.
*   **Ablation Studies:** The inclusion of ablation studies, specifically those removing the expansion and the assessment stages, is valuable.  These studies provide evidence that both components of APEX are necessary for its performance gains.
* **Clear writing**: The writing of the paper is clear and easy to follow, enabling the reader to easily understand the content.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Theoretical Depth:** The theoretical analysis is somewhat superficial.  While the effective rank argument is plausible, it lacks the depth and rigor to fully explain the observed performance gains. It's more of a high-level intuition than a solid mathematical proof. The assumptions for the analysis lack strong justifications.
*   **Reliance on Activation Magnitudes:** The entire method hinges on using activation magnitudes to identify "advantageous" parameters. This is a heuristic that, while commonly used, might not be the most accurate or robust measure of a parameter's importance.  There's a risk of being misled by parameters that have high activations but contribute less to the overall learning objective.
*   **Parameter Selection Sensitivity:** The method's performance depends on the selection thresholds ($K_{MHA}$ and $K_{FFN}$), and the number of stages ($T$).  While the authors present some analysis on the impact of these parameters, more comprehensive hyperparameter tuning and sensitivity analysis would be beneficial. It is unclear how these parameters might need to be adjusted for different models or datasets.
*   **Modest Performance Gains:** While APEX shows improvements over baselines, the performance gains are often incremental. The percentage increases in average scores on various benchmarks are not dramatically significant, especially considering the added complexity of the APEX training process.
* **Not easily applicable**: Because APEX requires custom implementation in the training loop, the algorithm is not easy to apply to existing models.
* **Scope of Experiment**: The experiments of APEX mainly focus on open-source LLMs such as TinyLLaMA and LLaMA, but it is not clear whether APEX can also be applicable to large LLMs or closed-source LLMs.

**Significance:**

The paper contributes a potentially useful technique for improving the efficiency of LLM training. However, the method's reliance on heuristics and the relatively modest performance gains limit its overall impact. The paper's most significant contribution may be in inspiring further research into alternative methods for parameter allocation and dynamic expansion during training.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the novelty of the idea, the attempt at theoretical justification, and the comprehensive experimental evaluation, but acknowledging the limited theoretical depth, heuristic nature, and modest performance gains, the assigned score is a 6.  The core idea has merit and warrants further exploration, but the current implementation and results don't represent a groundbreaking advancement in the field.

Score: 6
- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Mamba Knockout for Unraveling Factual Information Flow
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24244v1)
- **Authors**: Nir Endy, Idan Daniel Grosbard, Yuval Ran-Milo, Yonatan Slutzky, Itay Tshuva, Raja Giryes
- **Abstract**: This paper investigates the flow of factual information in Mamba State-Space Model (SSM)-based language models. We rely on theoretical and empirical connections to Transformer-based architectures and their attention mechanisms. Exploiting this relationship, we adapt attentional interpretability techniques originally developed for Transformers--specifically, the Attention Knockout methodology--to both Mamba-1 and Mamba-2. Using them we trace how information is transmitted and localized across tokens and layers, revealing patterns of subject-token information emergence and layer-wise dynamics. Notably, some phenomena vary between mamba models and Transformer based models, while others appear universally across all models inspected--hinting that these may be inherent to LLMs in general. By further leveraging Mamba's structured factorization, we disentangle how distinct "features" either enable token-to-token information exchange or enrich individual tokens, thus offering a unified lens to understand Mamba internal operations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the flow of factual information within Mamba-based language models (Mamba-1 and Mamba-2). It adapts the "Attention Knockout" technique, originally developed for Transformers, to analyze information transfer and localization within Mamba architectures. The study reveals both shared patterns and distinct differences in information processing between Mamba models and Transformers. A novel "feature knockout" mechanism is introduced, leveraging Mamba's factorized structure to disentangle the roles of different feature types in information exchange and token enrichment. Ultimately, the work aims to extend interpretability techniques beyond the Transformer paradigm, providing a more unified perspective on how language models organize factual information.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in extending interpretability techniques from Transformers to Mamba architectures. Adapting the "Attention Knockout" method is a significant step, given the structural differences between attention mechanisms and SSMs. The "feature knockout" mechanism is also a novel contribution, exploiting the SSM's structure for more nuanced interventions. However, the core idea of attention knockout itself is not new, which somewhat limits the overall novelty.

*   **Significance:** The work has the potential to significantly impact the field of language model interpretability. By providing tools to understand the internal workings of Mamba models, it helps bridge the gap in understanding between attention-based and SSM-based architectures. This can lead to more unified and generalizable theories of language understanding in neural networks. The identified shared patterns and distinct differences in information flow could inform future architecture design and optimization strategies. However, the immediate impact is somewhat limited by the current focus on factual information, and it remains to be seen how these findings will translate to other aspects of language modeling.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear adaptation of an existing technique to a new architecture.
    *   Introduction of a novel "feature knockout" mechanism.
    *   Empirical validation on both Mamba-1 and Mamba-2, with comparisons to Transformer models.
    *   Identification of both shared and distinct characteristics across architectures.
    *   Well-documented methodology and open-source code.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Relies heavily on an existing interpretability technique (Attention Knockout); the paper demonstrates *application* of said technique to Mamba.
    *   The analysis is primarily focused on factual information, leaving other aspects of language modeling unexplored.
