### LLMs Are Globally Multilingual Yet Locally Monolingual: Exploring Knowledge Transfer via Language and Thought Theory
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24409v1)
- **Authors**: Eojin Kang, Juae Kim
- **Abstract**: Multilingual large language models (LLMs) open up new possibilities for leveraging information across languages, but their factual knowledge recall remains inconsistent depending on the input language. While previous studies have attempted to address this issue through English-based prompting and evaluation, we explore non-English to English transfer via Language and Thought Theory. This perspective allows us to examine language-thought binding in LLMs and uncover why factual knowledge often fails to transfer effectively. We propose the Language-to-Thought (L2T) prompting strategy, which analyzes the relationship between input language, internal cognitive processes, and knowledge. Experimental results challenge the assumption that English-based approaches consistently outperform other languages and offer a novel insight that aligning the model's internal thought with the knowledge required for the task is critical for successful cross-lingual transfer. Furthermore, we show that applying L2T during training can alleviate LLMs' reliance on the input language and facilitate cross-linguistic knowledge integration without translation-based learning. Code and datasets will be available.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "LLMs Are Globally Multilingual Yet Locally Monolingual: Exploring Knowledge Transfer via Language and Thought Theory":

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the inconsistent factual knowledge transfer capabilities of multilingual large language models (LLMs) across different input languages. It challenges the assumption that English-based prompting and evaluation are universally superior.  The authors introduce the concept of Language and Thought Theory to examine how LLMs' internal "thought" processes are bound to the input language, hindering effective cross-lingual factual knowledge transfer. They propose a Language-to-Thought (L2T) prompting strategy to decouple the input language from the model's internal reasoning process. Through experiments using Chinese, Korean, and Arabic to English transfer, the paper demonstrates that aligning the model's internal thought with the knowledge required for the task is crucial.  Furthermore, they show that applying L2T during training can improve cross-linguistic knowledge integration without relying solely on translation-based learning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper introduces a novel perspective by focusing on the "local monolingualism" of LLMs, even when they exhibit global multilingual abilities. The L2T prompting strategy is a practical way to manipulate and investigate the language-thought binding within LLMs, going beyond simply translating inputs.  The training strategy using L2T during pretraining to directly impact knowledge representation is less novel, but the specific implementation and the focus on factual knowledge are valuable contributions.

**Significance:**

The paper has the potential to significantly impact how we understand and improve cross-lingual knowledge transfer in LLMs. It offers a nuanced view that moves beyond simply increasing training data or relying solely on English as the intermediary language. By highlighting the importance of aligning internal thought processes with the required knowledge, the paper provides a new avenue for research. The findings could lead to better prompting strategies, training techniques, and model architectures that enable more effective and consistent factual knowledge retrieval across languages. Furthermore, the study highlights the fact that reliance on only using English prompts to evaluate LLMs is problematic, as results do not generalize across languages.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Statement:** The paper identifies a crucial challenge in multilingual LLMs: the inconsistent recall of factual knowledge depending on the input language.
*   **Theoretical Foundation:** The use of Language and Thought Theory provides a strong conceptual framework for the investigation.
*   **Methodological Innovation:** The L2T prompting strategy is a well-designed and easily implementable technique for exploring language-thought binding.
*   **Empirical Validation:**  The experiments are well-structured, using diverse languages and models.  The analysis of PPL adds further support to the claims.
*   **Practical Implications:**  The paper offers actionable insights for improving cross-lingual knowledge transfer.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope of Datasets:**  The reliance on multiple-choice QA datasets, while common, could limit the generalizability of the findings.  Open-ended QA or generation tasks might provide a more comprehensive evaluation.
*   **The "Thought Language" Manipulation Is Indirect:** While L2T prompts are a clever technique, the manipulation of the LLM's "thought language" is indirect. It's difficult to know precisely how the prompt alters the model's internal representation. Future studies could use probing methods to better understand the impact of the prompt.
*   **L2T During Training Still Needs Further Exploration:** It would be more interesting to observe performance across various language pairings. Since the authors demonstrated the success of fine-tuning in L2T, it would be useful to see if the performance scales linearly with more aligned L2T data, or if there are other underlying features that contribute to successful alignments.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper is likely to inspire further research on language-thought binding in LLMs and the development of more effective cross-lingual knowledge transfer techniques. It could also lead to a re-evaluation of current benchmarks and evaluation practices, encouraging a more nuanced and multilingual approach.

**Score:** 7

**Rationale:** While the paper doesn't present a radical paradigm shift, it provides a valuable and well-executed investigation into an important problem. The introduction of L2T prompting is methodologically significant. The paper's theoretical grounding, clear empirical evidence, and practical implications contribute to a solid advancement of the field. However, the indirect nature of the "thought language" manipulation and the narrow scope of the training experiment limit its score from being higher.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### EasyText: Controllable Diffusion Transformer for Multilingual Text Rendering
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24417v1)
- **Authors**: Runnan Lu, Yuxuan Zhang, Jiaming Liu, Haofan Wang, Yiren Song
- **Abstract**: Generating accurate multilingual text with diffusion models has long been desired but remains challenging. Recent methods have made progress in rendering text in a single language, but rendering arbitrary languages is still an unexplored area. This paper introduces EasyText, a text rendering framework based on DiT (Diffusion Transformer), which connects denoising latents with multilingual character tokens encoded as character tokens. We propose character positioning encoding and position encoding interpolation techniques to achieve controllable and precise text rendering. Additionally, we construct a large-scale synthetic text image dataset with 1 million multilingual image-text annotations as well as a high-quality dataset of 20K annotated images, which are used for pretraining and fine-tuning respectively. Extensive experiments and evaluations demonstrate the effectiveness and advancement of our approach in multilingual text rendering, visual quality, and layout-aware text integration.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the EasyText paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces EasyText, a novel framework for controllable and high-quality multilingual text rendering within text-to-image diffusion models. Addressing the limitations of existing methods primarily focused on English text, EasyText leverages the in-context learning capabilities of Diffusion Transformers (DiT) to render text in over ten languages. The method encodes text using a VAE-based font tokenization and employs a novel "Implicit Character Position Alignment" technique for precise text placement, enabling both position-aware and layout-free rendering. A two-stage training strategy is adopted, involving large-scale synthetic pretraining for glyph generation and spatial mapping, followed by fine-tuning on a high-quality annotated dataset for visual-text integration and aesthetic refinement. The paper demonstrates the effectiveness of EasyText through quantitative and qualitative experiments, highlighting its ability to handle complex layouts, irregular regions, and unseen characters.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces several innovative components. The use of DiT architecture for multilingual text rendering is a solid direction. The proposed Implicit Character Position Alignment (ICPA) is a novel approach to controlling text placement, and the two-stage training strategy tackles the challenges of balancing multilingual glyph generation with visual coherence. Using image patches derived from a VAE for character representation is novel, as opposed to symbolic representations.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical gap in the field of text-to-image generation, specifically the lack of robust multilingual text rendering capabilities. EasyText's ability to handle diverse languages, complex layouts, and unseen characters has the potential to significantly impact various applications, including content creation, graphic design, and visual communication. Providing controllable and precise rendering is a significant achievement. The authors claim improved visual quality, layout control, and handling of unseen characters which, if validated, would represent advancements. However, the paper must rigorously support such claims.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Effective Architecture:** The DiT-based architecture and ICPA mechanism demonstrate strong potential for precise and controllable text rendering.
    *   **Two-Stage Training:** The pretraining/fine-tuning approach is well-suited for learning multilingual glyph features and integrating them into visual scenes.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper includes a mix of quantitative metrics (character/sentence precision, CLIPScore, OCR accuracy) and qualitative evaluations (user studies, GPT-40 assessments), providing a relatively thorough assessment of EasyText's performance.
    *   **Addresses a Clear Problem:** Multilingual text rendering is a practical need not sufficiently addressed by previous methods.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Clarity of ICPA:**  The exact implementation details and mathematical foundations of the "Implicit Character Position Alignment" could be clarified further. More ablation studies focusing on varying positional encoding interpolation strategies may support the advantage of such technique.
    *   **Limited Comparison:** The experimental comparison is solid, but further comparisons to concurrent works in multilingual and controllable text generation may improve the assessment of the method's novelty.
    *   **Trade-Offs:** The paper mentions a trade-off between rendering accuracy and flexible positioning/artistic font usage. This aspect should be further investigated and quantified, as it affects the usability of the framework.
    *   **Dataset Dependency:**  Although the two-stage training approach reduces the reliance on large annotated datasets, the synthetic pretraining dataset still requires careful construction and balancing across languages. The generalizability of the learned glyph features to truly unseen fonts and styles should be critically examined.
    *   **Limitations acknowledgment:** The paper only briefly acknowledges the limitations.  A deeper dive is needed into the failure cases (occluded text, highly stylized fonts, extreme perspective distortion, script confusions) that may limit the applicability of the approach.

*   **Potential Influence:** If the approach shows strong generalization ability in real-world scenarios, EasyText has the potential to become a valuable tool for multilingual content generation and image editing. Its layout control features may enable new applications in graphic design and visual communication.

**Justification for the Score:**

Considering the novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses, a score of 7 is appropriate. The core ideas – DiT architecture, ICPA, and the training strategy – show promise. The thorough experimentation strengthens the validity of its claims. However, the clarity and trade-offs must be investigated further. Furthermore, comparing to concurrent works may raise concerns regarding its novelty.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### MMAFFBen: A Multilingual and Multimodal Affective Analysis Benchmark for Evaluating LLMs and VLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24423v1)
- **Authors**: Zhiwei Liu, Lingfei Qian, Qianqian Xie, Jimin Huang, Kailai Yang, Sophia Ananiadou
- **Abstract**: Large language models and vision-language models (which we jointly call LMs) have transformed NLP and CV, demonstrating remarkable potential across various fields. However, their capabilities in affective analysis (i.e. sentiment analysis and emotion detection) remain underexplored. This gap is largely due to the absence of comprehensive evaluation benchmarks, and the inherent complexity of affective analysis tasks. In this paper, we introduce MMAFFBen, the first extensive open-source benchmark for multilingual multimodal affective analysis. MMAFFBen encompasses text, image, and video modalities across 35 languages, covering four key affective analysis tasks: sentiment polarity, sentiment intensity, emotion classification, and emotion intensity. Moreover, we construct the MMAFFIn dataset for fine-tuning LMs on affective analysis tasks, and further develop MMAFFLM-3b and MMAFFLM-7b based on it. We evaluate various representative LMs, including GPT-4o-mini, providing a systematic comparison of their affective understanding capabilities. This project is available at https://github.com/lzw108/MMAFFBen.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "MMAFFBen: A Multilingual and Multimodal Affective Analysis Benchmark for Evaluating LLMs and VLMs":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MMAFFBen, a new open-source benchmark designed to evaluate the affective analysis capabilities (sentiment analysis and emotion detection) of Large Language Models (LLMs) and Vision-Language Models (VLMs). The benchmark is comprehensive, encompassing text, image, and video modalities across 35 languages. It covers four affective analysis tasks: sentiment polarity, sentiment intensity, emotion classification, and emotion intensity. Furthermore, the authors create MMAFFIn, an instruction-tuning dataset for fine-tuning LMs on these tasks, and develop MMAFFLM-3B and MMAFFLM-7B models based on it. The paper concludes with a systematic evaluation of 20 representative LMs using MMAFFBen, comparing their affective understanding capabilities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the creation of a *multilingual* and *multimodal* benchmark specifically designed for evaluating affective analysis in LLMs and VLMs. Existing benchmarks often focus on a single modality (usually text) or a limited set of languages. MMAFFBen's comprehensive approach is a significant step forward. The introduction of the instruction-tuning dataset, MMAFFIn, is also a novel contribution. The development of two custom MMAFFLM is interesting, but there is limited details on the architecture differences from Qwen2.5-VL.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical gap in the understanding of LLM/VLM capabilities. While LLMs have demonstrated impressive performance across various tasks, their proficiency in affective analysis remains underexplored. Affective analysis is crucial for a wide range of applications, including social media monitoring, customer service, and mental health support. A robust benchmark like MMAFFBen enables researchers to systematically evaluate and improve the affective understanding abilities of these models, paving the way for more nuanced and human-aware AI systems. The public release of the benchmark and the developed models promote transparent, reproducible, and inclusive progress.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Scope:** The inclusion of multiple modalities (text, image, video) and languages (35) makes MMAFFBen a truly comprehensive benchmark.
    *   **Well-Defined Tasks:** The benchmark covers a diverse set of affective analysis tasks, allowing for a thorough evaluation of different aspects of affective understanding.
    *   **Instruction-Tuning Dataset:** MMAFFIn facilitates fine-tuning LLMs specifically for affective analysis, which can lead to significant performance improvements.
    *   **Systematic Evaluation:** The paper presents a detailed evaluation of 20 LMs, providing valuable insights into their strengths and weaknesses.
    *   **Open-Source Release:** The public availability of MMAFFBen, MMAFFIn, and the MMAFFLM models ensures transparency and reproducibility, fostering collaborative research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dataset Bias:** While the benchmark includes data from multiple languages and sources, there's a risk of bias in the underlying datasets. The paper acknowledges the potential bias toward Eastern image data due to the use of the QwenVL architecture in MMAFFLM, which could influence performance on specific demographics or cultural contexts. More in-depth discussion of dataset diversity and potential demographic/cultural biases would strengthen the paper.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Model Architectures:** The paper focuses primarily on evaluating existing models rather than exploring novel architectural approaches for affective analysis.
    *   **Computational Cost:** The comprehensive nature of the benchmark may require significant computational resources for evaluation, potentially limiting its accessibility for some researchers.
    *   **Evaluation Metrics:** The paper primarily uses Pearson Correlation Coefficient (PCC) and Macro F1 for evaluation. While these are standard metrics, some might benefit from a more nuanced analysis of the confusion matrices, especially for emotion classification tasks.

*   **Potential Influence:** MMAFFBen has the potential to significantly influence the field of affective analysis by providing a much-needed benchmark for evaluating LLMs and VLMs. It can drive research into more sophisticated and culturally sensitive models for sentiment analysis and emotion detection. The comprehensive and multimodal nature of MMAFFBen could become the standard in future research.

*   **Justification:** MMAFFBen is a very strong contribution due to its unique characteristics in the area of affect analysis of LLMs/VLMs. However, there are some minor limitations listed above and room to expand the scope of this work, which limits it from being higher than 8/10.

**Score: 8/10**
- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Model Unlearning via Sparse Autoencoder Subspace Guided Projections
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24428v1)
- **Authors**: Xu Wang, Zihao Li, Benyou Wang, Yan Hu, Difan Zou
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) store vast amounts of information, making them powerful yet raising privacy and safety concerns when selective knowledge removal is required. Existing unlearning strategies, ranging from gradient-based fine-tuning and model editing to sparse autoencoder (SAE) steering, either lack interpretability or fail to provide a robust defense against adversarial prompts. We propose SAE-Guided Subspace Projection Unlearning (SSPU), a novel framework that leverages SAE features to drive targeted updates in the model's parameter space, enabling precise, interpretable, and robust unlearning. SSPU's three-stage pipeline performs data-driven layer and feature selection, subspace construction via QR decomposition, and constrained optimization that controls activations into an "irrelevant" subspace while preserving retained knowledge. Overall, we use SAE features to construct a subspace that supervises unlearning, refining the loss and adding a regularization term to guide interpretable parameter updates. In experiments on the WMDP-Cyber forget set and three utility benchmarks (MMLU, TruthfulQA, GSM8K), SSPU reduces harmful knowledge accuracy by 3.22% compared to the strongest baseline. It also improves adversarial robustness, lowering malicious accuracy under jailbreak prompts compared to baselines. Our findings expose the limitations of prior unlearning methods and demonstrate how interpretable subspace-guided optimization can achieve robust, controllable model behavior.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel framework called SAE-Guided Subspace Projection Unlearning (SSPU) for selectively removing knowledge from large language models (LLMs). SSPU addresses limitations in existing unlearning methods by leveraging sparse autoencoders (SAEs) to guide targeted updates in the model's parameter space. The method consists of three stages: data-driven layer and feature selection, subspace construction using QR decomposition, and constrained optimization to steer activations into an "irrelevant" subspace while preserving retained knowledge. Experimental results on the WMDP-Cyber dataset and several utility benchmarks (MMLU, TruthfulQA, GSM8K) demonstrate that SSPU improves forgetting performance, maintains utility, and enhances robustness against jailbreak attacks compared to baseline methods. The paper highlights the limitations of prior unlearning approaches and advocates for interpretable subspace-guided optimization to achieve more robust and controllable model behavior.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The primary novelty lies in the integration of SAEs with subspace projection for unlearning. Existing SAE-based methods mostly focus on inference-time activation steering, while gradient-based methods lack interpretability. SSPU bridges this gap by using SAE features to define a subspace for supervised inverse learning and refining the unlearning loss with a regularization term. The three-stage pipeline, particularly the data-driven layer and feature selection, also adds to the novelty by automating the process of identifying relevant SAE features for intervention.

**Significance:**

The significance of the paper stems from addressing key challenges in knowledge unlearning for LLMs. The ability to selectively erase information without degrading overall performance is crucial for privacy and safety. SSPU offers several advantages:

*   **Improved Forgetting:** SSPU demonstrates better forgetting performance compared to strong baselines like RMU, indicating its effectiveness in removing targeted knowledge.
*   **Utility Retention:** SSPU achieves a good balance between forgetting and utility retention, minimizing the impact on the model's performance on other tasks.
*   **Robustness:** The method demonstrates improved robustness against jailbreak attacks, a critical concern for deploying unlearned models in real-world scenarios.
*   **Interpretability:**  The use of SAEs provides a degree of interpretability, allowing researchers to understand which features are being modified and how they contribute to the unlearning process.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing unlearning techniques and motivates the need for a more interpretable and robust approach.
*   **Well-Defined Method:** The SSPU framework is well-defined and explained, with a clear description of each stage and its underlying rationale.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The experimental evaluation is comprehensive, with comparisons to multiple baselines and assessments of forgetting, utility, and robustness.
*   **Strong Results:** The experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of SSPU in achieving better forgetting performance, utility retention, and robustness against jailbreak attacks.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Dependency on SAEs:** SSPU relies on the availability of a well-trained SAE, which may not always be feasible or practical for all models and datasets.
*   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The performance of SSPU may be sensitive to the choice of hyperparameters, such as the number of features to select (K) and the subspace regularization multiplier (dreg).
*   **Limited Theoretical Analysis:** The paper lacks a theoretical analysis of the convergence and generalization properties of SSPU.
*   **Scalability:** The complexity of the method, especially the QR decomposition and constrained optimization, might pose challenges for scaling to larger models and datasets.
*   **Limited Discussion:** It lacks a comparative discussion related to training with sparse autoencoders.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to influence the field of knowledge unlearning by providing a more effective and interpretable approach. SSPU could inspire further research on integrating SAEs with other unlearning techniques and developing more robust and controllable methods for selectively erasing knowledge from LLMs. The findings on jailbreak vulnerability also raise important questions about the limitations of existing unlearning methods and the need for more robust defenses.

**Score:** 7

**Rationale:**
The paper presents a solid contribution to the field of knowledge unlearning. The SSPU framework is innovative and addresses key limitations in existing methods. It produces impressive results, especially around robustness. However, the reliance on SAEs, potential hyperparameter sensitivity, scalability challenges and lack of theoretical backing, and sparse autoencoders related discussions slightly detract from its potential impact and overall score. Future work could focus on addressing these limitations and exploring the broader applicability of SSPU.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Graph Flow Matching: Enhancing Image Generation with Neighbor-Aware Flow Fields
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24434v1)
- **Authors**: Md Shahriar Rahim Siddiqui, Moshe Eliasof, Eldad Haber
- **Abstract**: Flow matching casts sample generation as learning a continuous-time velocity field that transports noise to data. Existing flow matching networks typically predict each point's velocity independently, considering only its location and time along its flow trajectory, and ignoring neighboring points. However, this pointwise approach may overlook correlations between points along the generation trajectory that could enhance velocity predictions, thereby improving downstream generation quality. To address this, we propose Graph Flow Matching (GFM), a lightweight enhancement that decomposes the learned velocity into a reaction term -- any standard flow matching network -- and a diffusion term that aggregates neighbor information via a graph neural module. This reaction-diffusion formulation retains the scalability of deep flow models while enriching velocity predictions with local context, all at minimal additional computational cost. Operating in the latent space of a pretrained variational autoencoder, GFM consistently improves Fr\'echet Inception Distance (FID) and recall across five image generation benchmarks (LSUN Church, LSUN Bedroom, FFHQ, AFHQ-Cat, and CelebA-HQ at $256\times256$), demonstrating its effectiveness as a modular enhancement to existing flow matching architectures.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Graph Flow Matching: Enhancing Image Generation with Neighbor-Aware Flow Fields":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Graph Flow Matching (GFM), a novel enhancement to flow matching-based generative models.  GFM addresses the limitation of existing flow matching architectures that predict each point's velocity independently by incorporating information from neighboring points.  It achieves this through a reaction-diffusion framework, decomposing the velocity field into a standard pointwise prediction term (using any existing flow network) and a graph-based diffusion term that aggregates information from neighboring samples using a graph neural network. The authors demonstrate that GFM consistently improves image generation quality, as measured by FID and recall, across several benchmark datasets, while incurring minimal computational overhead.  The method operates in the latent space of a pre-trained VAE.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of incorporating neighborhood information into flow matching is a valuable contribution. While the pointwise prediction paradigm is effective, acknowledging and exploiting local coherence is a natural progression. The reaction-diffusion framework provides a sound and intuitive method for integrating this neighborhood awareness. The modularity is also a strong point. Flow Matching is a relatively new area so enhancements are valuable.
*   **Significance:** The reported improvements in FID and recall are significant and consistently observed across multiple datasets and architectural backbones (ADM, DiT).  The low computational overhead makes it a practical enhancement that can be readily adopted by existing flow matching pipelines. This has implications for making flow-matching a more promising architecture.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Principled Approach:** The reaction-diffusion framework provides a clear and well-motivated way to incorporate neighborhood information.
    *   **Modularity:** GFM is designed as a modular enhancement, making it easy to integrate with existing flow matching architectures and training strategies.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The paper presents extensive experimental results on several standard image generation benchmarks, demonstrating the effectiveness of GFM.
    *   **Low Overhead:** The method adds minimal computational overhead, making it practical for real-world applications.
    *   The theoretical justification for the technique helps support it and provide confidence.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Latent Space Dependency:**  The method's dependence on a pre-trained VAE limits its applicability to scenarios where a suitable VAE is not available or doesn't perform well. The choice to stay in latent space is for computational reasons and doesn't exploit pixel space.
    *   **Graph Construction:** The graph construction method using attention or k-NN in latent space might not always capture the true underlying relationships between data points. A more sophisticated graph construction method could potentially yield further improvements. However, the existing implementation is useful because it is practical to implement.
    *   **Ablation could be stronger:** While the ablation study does look at graph connectivity, it is somewhat limited to only those configurations and may not cover the full configuration space.

*   **Potential Influence:** GFM has the potential to influence the development of flow matching-based generative models by highlighting the importance of local coherence and providing a practical mechanism for incorporating neighborhood awareness. It opens up avenues for further research into graph-based enhancements for flow matching and other generative modeling techniques. It adds another dimension to flow-matching, pushing its popularity.

**Rationale for Score:**

The paper presents a novel, well-motivated, and empirically validated enhancement to flow matching. The results are significant, and the method's modularity and low overhead make it a practical contribution to the field. While the reliance on a pre-trained VAE and the relatively simple graph construction method are limitations, the overall contribution is substantial and is in a quickly developing field. The ablation study could be a stronger. The improvement to FID alone helps demonstrate a significant contribution to the field.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### SORCE: Small Object Retrieval in Complex Environments
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24441v1)
- **Authors**: Chunxu Liu, Chi Xie, Xiaxu Chen, Wei Li, Feng Zhu, Rui Zhao, Limin Wang
- **Abstract**: Text-to-Image Retrieval (T2IR) is a highly valuable task that aims to match a given textual query to images in a gallery. Existing benchmarks primarily focus on textual queries describing overall image semantics or foreground salient objects, possibly overlooking inconspicuous small objects, especially in complex environments. Such small object retrieval is crucial, as in real-world applications, the targets of interest are not always prominent in the image. Thus, we introduce SORCE (Small Object Retrieval in Complex Environments), a new subfield of T2IR, focusing on retrieving small objects in complex images with textual queries. We propose a new benchmark, SORCE-1K, consisting of images with complex environments and textual queries describing less conspicuous small objects with minimal contextual cues from other salient objects. Preliminary analysis on SORCE-1K finds that existing T2IR methods struggle to capture small objects and encode all the semantics into a single embedding, leading to poor retrieval performance on SORCE-1K. Therefore, we propose to represent each image with multiple distinctive embeddings. We leverage Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to extract multiple embeddings for each image instructed by a set of Regional Prompts (ReP). Experimental results show that our multi-embedding approach through MLLM and ReP significantly outperforms existing T2IR methods on SORCE-1K. Our experiments validate the effectiveness of SORCE-1K for benchmarking SORCE performances, highlighting the potential of multi-embedding representation and text-customized MLLM features for addressing this task.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a new subfield of Text-to-Image Retrieval (T2IR) called "Small Object Retrieval in Complex Environments" (SORCE). It highlights the challenge of retrieving images where the target of interest is a small, inconspicuous object within a complex background.  The authors present a new benchmark, SORCE-1K, specifically designed for this task, comprising images with complex scenes and textual queries describing these small objects. Recognizing that existing T2IR methods struggle with this fine-grained retrieval, the paper proposes a multi-embedding approach using Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) and Regional Prompts (ReP) to capture different aspects of the image. Experimental results on SORCE-1K demonstrate the superiority of this multi-embedding approach.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in defining SORCE as a distinct problem within T2IR and creating the SORCE-1K benchmark. While the *concept* of fine-grained retrieval exists, framing it within this specific context and providing a targeted dataset is a valuable contribution. The application of MLLMs and ReP is an incremental innovation; using MLLMs for feature extraction is explored in related literature, but customizing this approach for small object retrieval is novel.
*   **Significance:** The significance stems from addressing a practical limitation of current T2IR systems. Many real-world applications (e.g., surveillance, infrastructure monitoring) require the ability to find small, non-salient objects. SORCE-1K provides a concrete means to evaluate and compare algorithms on this challenging task. The results demonstrate that existing T2IR methods fall short and highlight the need for more sophisticated approaches. However, the performance improvement shown in the results isn't exceptionally high.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clearly defines a practical problem in T2IR.
    *   Provides a new benchmark (SORCE-1K) with focused challenges.
    *   Demonstrates the limitations of existing T2IR methods on SORCE.
    *   Proposes a reasonable MLLM-based solution with promising results.
    *   Well-written and organized paper.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The technical contribution (MLLM with ReP) is incremental rather than a breakthrough.  Using MLLMs for feature extraction isn't entirely new.
    *   The SORCE-1K dataset is limited to 1K images; a larger, more diverse dataset would increase its impact. The use of SA-1B dataset makes the environment very complex. However, the definition of "small object" could be better clarified. It is not always clear what constitutes a "small object" versus simply a less prominent object.
    *   The performance improvements, while positive, are not overwhelmingly large, suggesting room for significant further improvement.
    *   The broader impact discussion could be more in-depth, exploring potential ethical considerations and biases in the dataset.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research in T2IR by drawing attention to the importance of fine-grained retrieval and providing a benchmark for evaluation.  It encourages the development of methods that can effectively capture and represent the subtle details of images, especially for finding small and inconspicuous objects. However, the impact will depend on the adoption and use of the SORCE-1K dataset.
*    **Limitations:** The analysis of failure cases isn't present. This could provide deeper insights into the challenges faced by the model and guide future improvements.

**Score: 6**

**Justification:** While the problem definition and benchmark (SORCE and SORCE-1K) are valuable contributions that address a gap in T2IR research, the proposed solution is more of an incremental improvement. The paper does not present a radically new algorithm or technique. The MLLM-based approach with regional prompts is a logical extension of existing work. A higher score would be warranted if the performance gains were significantly greater, the dataset was larger/more diverse, or the proposed method was truly groundbreaking in its approach. The paper is good, well executed and clearly defines an important niche in T2IR, but it lacks revolutionary ideas.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### RMoA: Optimizing Mixture-of-Agents through Diversity Maximization and Residual Compensation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24442v1)
- **Authors**: Zhentao Xie, Chengcheng Han, Jinxin Shi, Wenjun Cui, Xin Zhao, Xingjiao Wu, Jiabao Zhao
- **Abstract**: Although multi-agent systems based on large language models show strong capabilities on multiple tasks, they are still limited by high computational overhead, information loss, and robustness. Inspired by ResNet's residual learning, we propose Residual Mixture-of-Agents (RMoA), integrating residual connections to optimize efficiency and reliability. To maximize information utilization from model responses while minimizing computational costs, we innovatively design an embedding-based diversity selection mechanism that greedily selects responses via vector similarity. Furthermore, to mitigate iterative information degradation, we introduce a Residual Extraction Agent to preserve cross-layer incremental information by capturing inter-layer response differences, coupled with a Residual Aggregation Agent for hierarchical information integration. Additionally, we propose an adaptive termination mechanism that dynamically halts processing based on residual convergence, further improving inference efficiency. RMoA achieves state-of-the-art performance on the benchmarks of across alignment, mathematical reasoning, code generation, and multitasking understanding, while significantly reducing computational overhead. Code is available at https://github.com/mindhunter01/RMoA.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces RMOA (Residual Mixture-of-Agents), an improved architecture based on Mixture-of-Agents (MoA) systems for large language models. Inspired by ResNet's residual learning, RMOA aims to address the limitations of standard MoA systems, such as high computational overhead, information loss during aggregation, and lack of robustness.  RMOA incorporates an embedding-based diversity selection mechanism, a Residual Extraction Agent, an Adaptive Termination Mechanism, and role-playing personas for agents to optimize efficiency, maintain information across layers, and enhance diversity in reasoning.  The paper presents experiments across multiple benchmarks (alignment, mathematical reasoning, code generation, and multi-task understanding) demonstrating RMOA achieves state-of-the-art performance with reduced computational cost.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents several innovations, including:
    *   *Embedding-based Diversity Selection:* This is a practical technique to minimize computational costs while maximizing information gain and ensuring greater information heterogeneity.
    *   *Residual Extraction and Aggregation Agents:*  The introduction of these agents, inspired by ResNet, to preserve incremental information across layers is a strong contribution and a clever adaptation for the multi-agent setting. It addresses a clear weakness of standard MoA architectures, where information loss can occur during aggregation.
    *   *Adaptive Termination Mechanism:* Dynamically halting processing based on response variations is a valuable addition, further improving inference efficiency.
    *   *Role-playing Personas:* While the concept of role-playing personas isn't entirely new, its integration within RMOA adds another layer of diversity and creativity in the reasoning process.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in addressing critical challenges associated with scaling multi-agent systems for LLMs. The results demonstrate a clear improvement in performance across various benchmarks, coupled with a reduction in computational costs.  This makes multi-agent systems more practical for real-world applications.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Experimental Evaluation:** The paper includes extensive experiments across diverse benchmarks and ablation studies to validate the effectiveness of individual components. The inclusion of results on models of varying sizes (including GPT-4) adds robustness to the findings.
    *   **Clear Problem Definition and Motivation:**  The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing MoA architectures and convincingly motivates the need for RMOA.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The reported state-of-the-art performance and reduced computational overhead provide compelling evidence for RMOA's effectiveness.
    *   **Open-Source Code:** Making the code available promotes reproducibility and encourages further research in this area.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope:** While the paper demonstrates strong results, the focus is primarily on optimizing existing MoA architectures. A deeper exploration of the theoretical underpinnings of multi-agent collaboration in LLMs would further enhance the paper's value.
    *   **Incremental Improvements:** Some of the gains over existing MoA approaches, while significant, could be seen as incremental improvements rather than a paradigm shift.  The core concepts (diversity, residual learning) are inspired by existing techniques in different contexts.
    *   **Hallucination Evaluation:** The assessment of hallucination is basic. Using specific evaluation metrics for LLM hallucinations would be more robust.
    *   **Scalability with much bigger LLMs:**  Results on much bigger LLMs beyond GPT-4 would further enhance the significance. The analysis focuses largely on 7B, 8B models.

*   **Potential Influence:** RMOA is likely to influence future research on multi-agent systems for LLMs by:
    *   Providing a more efficient and robust architecture for collaborative reasoning.
    *   Inspiring further exploration of residual learning techniques in multi-agent settings.
    *   Encouraging the development of adaptive mechanisms for optimizing resource utilization in LLMs.

**Justification of Score:**

I assign a score of **8** to this paper. The paper represents a significant advancement in the practical application of multi-agent systems for large language models. The proposed RMOA architecture addresses key limitations of existing MoA systems and delivers compelling empirical results across a variety of tasks. While the novelty is primarily in the *integration* and *adaptation* of existing techniques (diversity selection, residual learning), the resulting architecture is demonstrably more efficient and robust. The comprehensive experimental evaluation and open-source code significantly increase the paper's impact.  The weaknesses are mainly in the level of theoretical insight and the relative scope of the contribution.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 8/10

### Learning Safety Constraints for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24445v1)
- **Authors**: Xin Chen, Yarden As, Andreas Krause
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have emerged as powerful tools but pose significant safety risks through harmful outputs and vulnerability to adversarial attacks. We propose SaP, short for Safety Polytope, a geometric approach to LLM safety that learns and enforces multiple safety constraints directly in the model's representation space. We develop a framework that identifies safe and unsafe regions via the polytope's facets, enabling both detection and correction of unsafe outputs through geometric steering. Unlike existing approaches that modify model weights, SaP operates post-hoc in the representation space, preserving model capabilities while enforcing safety constraints. Experiments across multiple LLMs demonstrate that our method can effectively detect unethical inputs, reduce adversarial attack success rates while maintaining performance on standard tasks, thus highlighting the importance of having an explicit geometric model for safety. Analysis of the learned polytope facets reveals emergence of specialization in detecting different semantic notions of safety, providing interpretable insights into how safety is captured in LLMs' representation space.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "SaP" (Safety Polytope), a geometric framework for enhancing the safety of large language models (LLMs). SaP learns and enforces safety constraints directly within the LLM's representation space by modeling safe and unsafe regions as a polytope. It achieves this through a concept encoder that disentangles safety concepts and a steering algorithm that directs unsafe outputs back into the "safe set" (the polytope). The key advantage is that SaP operates post-hoc, preserving the model's original capabilities while enforcing safety. Experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of SaP in detecting unethical inputs, mitigating adversarial attacks, and maintaining performance on standard tasks.  Furthermore, the analysis of the learned polytope facets reveals specialization in detecting different semantic notions of safety, adding an interpretable layer to the LLM's safety mechanisms.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The geometric approach to LLM safety is a significant contribution.  While constraint learning and safe RLHF are established areas, framing LLM safety as a geometric constraint satisfaction problem and realizing it *post-hoc* in the representation space distinguishes this work. The use of polytopes and the concept encoder to explicitly model and disentangle safety concepts is also novel. A key strength lies in its inference time approach, which avoids the resource intensive nature of methods requiring retraining or fine tuning.

*   **Significance:**  The paper addresses a crucial problem: ensuring the safety of LLMs.  By operating in the representation space *without* modifying model weights, SaP offers a practical way to implement safety measures without degrading performance on other tasks. Demonstrating strong defense against adversarial attacks and maintaining MMLU accuracy showcases the practical significance of the work. The interpretability afforded by the facet analysis is another crucial feature, allowing for better understanding and control of LLM safety mechanisms. The method also provides interpretability to human safety concepts, by allowing for better detection and analysis of different safety concepts.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Strong experimental results across multiple LLMs.
    *   Clear framework and well-defined components (concept encoder, steering algorithm).
    *   Post-hoc operation preserves model capabilities.
    *   Geometric model enables interpretable safety analysis.
    *   Addresses a key limitation of current RLHF approaches (the inability to provide interpretability)

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The method relies on a labeled dataset of safe and unsafe examples for polytope training. The quality and bias of this dataset could significantly impact the effectiveness of SaP. The data complexity of constructing a robust polytope should be explored further.
    *   The long-term effects of steering and altering the representation space at inference time needs further scrutiny. While model capabilities are maintained, subtle biases may be introduced that need longer-term evaluation.
    *   The dependence on a particular intermediate layer’s activations may be vulnerable if that layer's role shifts with future fine-tuning of the base model.
    * The current reliance of heuristic facet selection could be improved.

*   **Potential Influence:**  SaP's geometric approach has the potential to influence future research in LLM safety, offering a new direction for exploration and opening up opportunities for further development of geometric methods. The framework could be extended to handle more complex safety constraints or combined with other safety mechanisms.  The interpretable nature of SaP could also lead to better understanding and control of LLM behavior. As identified, the method can also be applied to LLMs without being specific or unique to a particular class of models.

*   **Justification for Score:** While the paper presents a compelling framework, the current evaluation and theoretical analysis are not completely robust. A deeper consideration of data complexity, long-term effects, and scalability is needed. While there are other methods which improve LLM safety, SaP offers superior properties and allows for post-hoc modification of safety parameters. The approach is also generally applicable to other models and domains. As such, the contribution merits a good score.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Exploring the Impact of Occupational Personas on Domain-Specific QA
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24448v1)
- **Authors**: Eojin Kang, Jaehyuk Yu, Juae Kim
- **Abstract**: Recent studies on personas have improved the way Large Language Models (LLMs) interact with users. However, the effect of personas on domain-specific question-answering (QA) tasks remains a subject of debate. This study analyzes whether personas enhance specialized QA performance by introducing two types of persona: Profession-Based Personas (PBPs) (e.g., scientist), which directly relate to domain expertise, and Occupational Personality-Based Personas (OPBPs) (e.g., scientific person), which reflect cognitive tendencies rather than explicit expertise. Through empirical evaluations across multiple scientific domains, we demonstrate that while PBPs can slightly improve accuracy, OPBPs often degrade performance, even when semantically related to the task. Our findings suggest that persona relevance alone does not guarantee effective knowledge utilization and that they may impose cognitive constraints that hinder optimal knowledge application. Future research can explore how nuanced distinctions in persona representations guide LLMs, potentially contributing to reasoning and knowledge retrieval that more closely mirror human social conceptualization.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the impact of different types of occupational personas on the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) in domain-specific question-answering (QA) tasks. The authors distinguish between Profession-Based Personas (PBPs) which directly represent domain expertise (e.g., "scientist"), and Occupational Personality-Based Personas (OPBPs) which reflect cognitive tendencies related to a profession (e.g., "investigative person"). Through experiments on science-related datasets, they find that while PBPs can slightly improve accuracy, OPBPs often degrade performance, even when semantically related to the task. They analyze neuron activations to understand how personas influence knowledge retrieval and identify the layers where persona effects are most pronounced. The authors conclude that simply assigning a relevant persona doesn't guarantee effective knowledge utilization and may even introduce cognitive constraints.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a nuanced distinction between PBPs and OPBPs, moving beyond simple persona assignments to explore the cognitive aspects influencing LLM performance in domain-specific tasks. This categorization is useful.
    *   **Significance:**  The findings are significant as they challenge the assumption that all domain-relevant personas will improve LLM performance. The counter-intuitive result that OPBPs can degrade performance highlights the complexity of persona interaction and the need for a deeper understanding of how LLMs process and utilize knowledge in the presence of role-playing instructions. The neuron activation analysis is a useful (though preliminary) attempt to peek into the "black box" of LLM reasoning with personas.
    *   **Methodology:** The experimental setup is reasonably rigorous, using established datasets (MMLU) and multiple models (GPT-3.5, Llama-3-8B-Instruct) to assess the impact of different personas. The use of "Confession Rate" and "Abstention Rate" provides additional insight into how the LLMs handle domain knowledge tasks.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:** The study is confined to science-related domains. While this allows for focused analysis, it limits the generalizability of the findings to other fields that may require different types of reasoning or knowledge.
    *   **Statistical Significance:**  While the paper reports average accuracy and standard deviations, it lacks explicit statistical tests (e.g., t-tests, ANOVA) to confirm the significance of the observed performance differences. The "slightly improve" / "often degrade" claims require more rigorous statistical backing.
    *   **Depth of Analysis:** The neuron activation analysis, while promising, remains somewhat superficial. The paper identifies layers where differences occur, but it doesn't deeply connect specific neuron activation patterns to specific reasoning steps or knowledge elements. The explanation of how personas specifically influence the FFN layers is limited.
    *   **Oversimplification of Holland's theory**: The application of Holland's occupational themes seems somewhat forced and isn't fully exploited, weakening the OBP definition..
    *   **Inconsistencies between Models** The experimental results between models show many variabilities.

*   **Impact:**

    *   The paper contributes to a more critical understanding of persona-based prompting for LLMs. It encourages researchers to move beyond simply assigning roles and to consider the cognitive implications of different persona types. The study provides a foundation for future research to explore more nuanced persona representations and how they influence knowledge retrieval and reasoning in LLMs.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper has clear novelty and significance in its analysis of PBP vs. OPBP, revealing the complexity of the LLM-Persona interaction. However, the analysis scope is limited, statistical rigor is lacking, and the neuron activation analysis requires more depth. The dependence solely on the MMLU dataset provides a solid benchmark, but future expansions should include additional real-world and specific use-cases to validate the applicability of the findings. The somewhat rudimentary use of Holland's framework also diminishes the score. Therefore:

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### LPASS: Linear Probes as Stepping Stones for vulnerability detection using compressed LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24451v1)
- **Authors**: Luis Ibanez-Lissen, Lorena Gonzalez-Manzano, Jose Maria de Fuentes, Nicolas Anciaux
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are being extensively used for cybersecurity purposes. One of them is the detection of vulnerable codes. For the sake of efficiency and effectiveness, compression and fine-tuning techniques are being developed, respectively. However, they involve spending substantial computational efforts. In this vein, we analyse how Linear Probes (LPs) can be used to provide an estimation on the performance of a compressed LLM at an early phase -- before fine-tuning. We also show their suitability to set the cut-off point when applying layer pruning compression. Our approach, dubbed $LPASS$, is applied in BERT and Gemma for the detection of 12 of MITRE's Top 25 most dangerous vulnerabilities on 480k C/C++ samples. LPs can be computed in 142.97 s. and provide key findings: (1) 33.3 \% and 72.2\% of layers can be removed, respectively, with no precision loss; (2) they provide an early estimate of the post-fine-tuning and post-compression model effectiveness, with 3\% and 8.68\% as the lowest and average precision errors, respectively. $LPASS$-based LLMs outperform the state of the art, reaching 86.9\% of accuracy in multi-class vulnerability detection. Interestingly, $LPASS$-based compressed versions of Gemma outperform the original ones by 1.6\% of F1-score at a maximum while saving 29.4 \% and 23.8\% of training and inference time and 42.98\% of model size.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LPASS (Linear Probes as Stepping Stones), a technique that uses linear probes (LPs) to estimate the performance of compressed Large Language Models (LLMs) for vulnerability detection *before* fine-tuning. This allows for informed decisions on layer pruning and other compression techniques, saving computational resources. LPASS trains LPs on LLM internal activations to predict code features.  It applies LPASS to BERT and Gemma models, compressing them via layer pruning and quantization, and detecting 12 MITRE Top 25 vulnerabilities in C/C++ code. The results demonstrate that LPASS can accurately predict performance, guide layer pruning (removing 33.3% and 72.2% of layers in BERT and Gemma, respectively), and improve the state of the art in vulnerability detection.  The compressed Gemma even outperforms the original model in F1-score while reducing model size, training, and inference time.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The key novelty lies in using LPs *prospectively* to estimate the performance of LLM compression techniques *prior* to expensive fine-tuning.  Most existing work focuses on compression post-training or uses different methods. The application of LPs for this purpose, especially in the vulnerability detection domain, appears to be a unique contribution. The idea of leveraging LLMs for vulnerability detection is not new but it is an active research area; the specific approach outlined in this paper is original. The analysis in this paper offers a new perspective in how the LLM's performance can be gauged beforehand thus making the deployment of LLMs more cost-effective.

*   **Significance:**
    *   *Resource Efficiency:* The potential for substantial savings in computational resources (training time, memory) is a significant benefit, especially as LLMs continue to grow in size.  The results quantifying these savings are compelling.

    *   *Performance Improvement:*  The fact that compressed models can *outperform* original models is a key finding. The use of LPs to guide pruning helps retain important information during compression.

    *   *Explainability:* The use of LP also provides a first step to a explainable compression framework.
    * **Reproducibility and practicality** The experiments were repeated 3 times for each model, and the authors state that they will release code to foster further research,

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem statement and motivation (the computational cost of LLMs in cybersecurity).

    *   Well-defined methodology (LPASS).

    *   Comprehensive experiments with two LLMs (BERT and Gemma), different compression techniques, and multiple datasets.

    *   Quantifiable results demonstrate the effectiveness of LPASS.

    *   Comparison to the state of the art.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limited to C/C++.  The applicability to other languages is unknown.

    *   Specific vulnerabilities focused on. The paper focuses on identifying 12 MITRE's Top 25 vulnerabilities. Are the results generalizable to others?
    *   Specific LPs and Features Selected. The specific code features (CC and HD) and LPs used are task-dependent, as mentioned by the authors, but the general methodology may work for other contexts.
    *   Limited choice of model. The effectiveness with other models isn't guaranteed.

*   **Justification for Score:** The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of LLM compression and vulnerability detection. The resource efficiency gains and performance improvements are substantial. The limitations, while present, are clearly acknowledged by the authors and provide avenues for future research. The idea of using LPs to estimate LLM before the full training is compelling and should inspire new ways to deploy LLMs. I believe this paper represents a solid contribution with the potential to influence the research community.
