### UniGeo: Taming Video Diffusion for Unified Consistent Geometry Estimation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24521v1)
- **Authors**: Yang-Tian Sun, Xin Yu, Zehuan Huang, Yi-Hua Huang, Yuan-Chen Guo, Ziyi Yang, Yan-Pei Cao, Xiaojuan Qi
- **Abstract**: Recently, methods leveraging diffusion model priors to assist monocular geometric estimation (e.g., depth and normal) have gained significant attention due to their strong generalization ability. However, most existing works focus on estimating geometric properties within the camera coordinate system of individual video frames, neglecting the inherent ability of diffusion models to determine inter-frame correspondence. In this work, we demonstrate that, through appropriate design and fine-tuning, the intrinsic consistency of video generation models can be effectively harnessed for consistent geometric estimation. Specifically, we 1) select geometric attributes in the global coordinate system that share the same correspondence with video frames as the prediction targets, 2) introduce a novel and efficient conditioning method by reusing positional encodings, and 3) enhance performance through joint training on multiple geometric attributes that share the same correspondence. Our results achieve superior performance in predicting global geometric attributes in videos and can be directly applied to reconstruction tasks. Even when trained solely on static video data, our approach exhibits the potential to generalize to dynamic video scenes.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the UniGeo paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces UniGeo, a novel framework for consistent 3D geometry estimation from video, utilizing video diffusion models.  Instead of predicting geometric properties independently for each frame in its own camera coordinate system, UniGeo predicts geometric properties (like normals and global position) within a unified *global* coordinate system. This leverages the inherent inter-frame consistency learned by video diffusion models. The key innovations include: (1) representing geometry in a global coordinate system for better consistency, (2) conditioning the diffusion model using a Shared Positional Encoding (SPE) strategy (reusing positional embeddings from the RGB frames), and (3) jointly training on multiple geometric attributes.  Experiments demonstrate improved consistency and accuracy compared to existing methods, with direct applicability to 3D reconstruction. The method can even generalize to dynamic video scenes despite being trained primarily on static data.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core idea of using video diffusion for *consistent* geometry estimation is interesting, and the way it is implemented is arguably novel.  The crucial innovations lies in a) the global coordinate space formulation b) SPE (shared positional encoding) for RGB conditioning of the geometric properties. The method's direct applicability to reconstruction without camera parameter adjustments is also a valuable feature, arising from the use of global coordinates. The joint training of multiple attributes is less novel in itself but contributes to the practical effectiveness of the approach.

* **Significance:**  The paper addresses a critical limitation of many geometry estimation methods: frame-to-frame inconsistency.  By achieving better consistency, UniGeo potentially opens up avenues for more robust 3D scene understanding from videos, which has relevance to VR/AR, robotics, and autonomous driving. The demonstrated reconstruction quality is also promising. A significant aspect is the ability to generalize to dynamic scenes, which significantly expands the applicability of the method. The comparative results with image-based approaches are impressive (given no explicit multi-view data), thus making the case for the effectiveness of consistency.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Consistent Formulation:** The global coordinate system and shared positional embedding is an elegant approach to enforce consistency leveraging video priors.
    * **Direct Applicability to Reconstruction:** Avoids post-processing steps typical of per-frame estimation approaches.
    * **Generalization:** The ability to generalize to dynamic scenes without explicitly training on them is a major advantage.
    * **Unified Framework:** The multi-task training strategy allows for the creation of a single model that can estimate multiple geometric properties.
    * **Quantitative Results:** Extensive quantitative evaluation against a wide range of state-of-the-art methods showcases clear improvements.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Reliance on Synthetic Data:** Training exclusively on synthetic data raises concerns about real-world generalizability, despite the promising results on some real datasets. A discussion of how to address this (e.g., domain adaptation techniques) would be beneficial.
    * **Computational Cost:** The high computational cost (fine-tuning at relatively low resolutions) is a practical limitation.  The paper mentions efficient distillation as a possible solution, but this is left for future work. The ablation study does indicate one-step inference helps with the cost, but fine-tuning with higher resolutions for better performance might not be practically feasible for a lot of users.
    * **Limited Clip Length:** The limited-length segment processing due to memory constraint is definitely a constraint for real-world applications where long sequences are common.

* **Potential Influence:** If the computational cost can be reduced and real-world generalizability improved, this approach could become a standard component in pipelines for video-based 3D scene understanding. The code availability is also a plus, making it easier for the community to use the method.

* **Justification for the score:**  The paper presents a solid contribution with notable novelty in its consistent formulation and promising performance.  However, the reliance on synthetic data, high computational cost, and limited segment length slightly detract from its overall impact. While the generalization to dynamic scenes is encouraging, more comprehensive evaluation on more complex real-world datasets is needed. Thus, a score of 7 is assigned to the paper. The core ideas are sound and have a lot of potential, but further work is required to address the limitations before it becomes truly impactful.

### Stress-testing Machine Generated Text Detection: Shifting Language Models Writing Style to Fool Detectors
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24523v1)
- **Authors**: Andrea Pedrotti, Michele Papucci, Cristiano Ciaccio, Alessio Miaschi, Giovanni Puccetti, Felice Dell'Orletta, Andrea Esuli
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in Generative AI and Large Language Models (LLMs) have enabled the creation of highly realistic synthetic content, raising concerns about the potential for malicious use, such as misinformation and manipulation. Moreover, detecting Machine-Generated Text (MGT) remains challenging due to the lack of robust benchmarks that assess generalization to real-world scenarios. In this work, we present a pipeline to test the resilience of state-of-the-art MGT detectors (e.g., Mage, Radar, LLM-DetectAIve) to linguistically informed adversarial attacks. To challenge the detectors, we fine-tune language models using Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) to shift the MGT style toward human-written text (HWT). This exploits the detectors' reliance on stylistic clues, making new generations more challenging to detect. Additionally, we analyze the linguistic shifts induced by the alignment and which features are used by detectors to detect MGT texts. Our results show that detectors can be easily fooled with relatively few examples, resulting in a significant drop in detection performance. This highlights the importance of improving detection methods and making them robust to unseen in-domain texts.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of detecting machine-generated text (MGT) by proposing a method to stress-test existing MGT detectors. It focuses on the vulnerability of these detectors to linguistic shortcuts, which are stylistic differences between human-written text (HWT) and MGT. The core idea is to fine-tune language models (LLMs) using Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) to align the style of MGT more closely with HWT, thereby making the MGT harder to detect.  The paper presents a pipeline to generate such "adversarial" MGT, analyzes the linguistic shifts induced by the alignment, and evaluates the performance of state-of-the-art MGT detectors (Mage, Radar, LLM-DetectAIve) when challenged with this new MGT. Results show that current detectors can be easily fooled with relatively few examples, leading to a significant drop in detection performance. The authors release code, models, and data to support future research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its systematic approach to generating adversarial examples specifically designed to exploit the weaknesses of existing MGT detectors concerning linguistic style. While DPO and linguistic analysis are established techniques, their combined use to fine-tune LLMs for the purpose of evading MGT detection appears to be relatively new. Furthermore, the in-depth analysis of linguistic features that are changed through the DPO process, and the evaluation of how different detectors respond to those changes, is also a novel aspect.
*   **Significance:** The work has significant implications for the field of MGT detection and responsible AI deployment. By demonstrating the vulnerability of existing methods, the paper highlights the need for more robust detectors that are not overly reliant on easily-manipulated stylistic cues.  The released code, models, and data could serve as a valuable resource for the development of more generalizable and trustworthy MGT detection systems.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The methodology is well-defined and relatively easy to reproduce.
    *   The analysis of linguistic feature shifts is insightful and provides a deeper understanding of how MGT detectors are being fooled.
    *   The empirical results clearly demonstrate the vulnerability of several state-of-the-art detectors.
    *   The paper addresses a crucial challenge for the responsible deployment of large language models.
    *   The release of code, models, and data makes the research easily accessible and promotes further work.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The detectors' focus may not represent the most advanced methods in MGT detection, or specifically be robust in the given datasets and use cases. Future work should be spent extending the robustness tests.
    *   There may be better evaluation metrics for a more comprehensive coverage of the detector's utility.
    *   The specific datasets used (XSUM, arXiv abstracts) might limit the generalizability of the findings, as they are in specific domains.
    *   The focus is primarily on stylistic cues. The results might not generalize to detectors that rely on other signals, such as semantic or topical coherence.
    *   More sophisticated "defense" mechanisms against adversarial attacks, such as adversarial training, could have been incorporated.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research by:
    *   Inspiring the development of more robust MGT detection methods that are less susceptible to adversarial attacks.
    *   Encouraging the creation of more challenging benchmarks for MGT detection.
    *   Motivating further investigation into the linguistic characteristics of MGT and their relationship to human writing styles.
    *   Raising awareness of the limitations of current MGT detection techniques.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field by highlighting the fragility of MGT detection methods and offering a concrete approach for stressing these systems. The methodology is clear, and the results are compelling. While the scope of the evaluation and the reliance on specific datasets are limitations, the work has important practical implications and provides a valuable framework for future research. The strengths clearly outweigh the weaknesses.

### Transformers Are Universally Consistent
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24531v1)
- **Authors**: Sagar Ghosh, Kushal Bose, Swagatam Das
- **Abstract**: Despite their central role in the success of foundational models and large-scale language modeling, the theoretical foundations governing the operation of Transformers remain only partially understood. Contemporary research has largely focused on their representational capacity for language comprehension and their prowess in in-context learning, frequently under idealized assumptions such as linearized attention mechanisms. Initially conceived to model sequence-to-sequence transformations, a fundamental and unresolved question is whether Transformers can robustly perform functional regression over sequences of input tokens. This question assumes heightened importance given the inherently non-Euclidean geometry underlying real-world data distributions. In this work, we establish that Transformers equipped with softmax-based nonlinear attention are uniformly consistent when tasked with executing Ordinary Least Squares (OLS) regression, provided both the inputs and outputs are embedded in hyperbolic space. We derive deterministic upper bounds on the empirical error which, in the asymptotic regime, decay at a provable rate of $\mathcal{O}(t^{-1/2d})$, where $t$ denotes the number of input tokens and $d$ the embedding dimensionality. Notably, our analysis subsumes the Euclidean setting as a special case, recovering analogous convergence guarantees parameterized by the intrinsic dimensionality of the data manifold. These theoretical insights are corroborated through empirical evaluations on real-world datasets involving both continuous and categorical response variables.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the theoretical foundations of Transformers, specifically their ability to perform functional regression over sequences of input tokens.  The core question is whether Transformers can robustly perform functional regression, especially given the non-Euclidean geometry inherent in real-world data.  The authors establish that Transformers with softmax attention are uniformly consistent for Ordinary Least Squares (OLS) regression, *provided* both inputs and outputs are embedded in hyperbolic space.  They derive error bounds and demonstrate that the Euclidean setting is a special case of their more general analysis. They then propose a Hyperbolic Transformer (HyT), built on the Poincaré ball model, and provide empirical evidence that HyT outperforms vanilla Transformers in pre-training across diverse datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper's novelty lies in its theoretical analysis of Transformer consistency *in hyperbolic space*.  Prior works have explored the representational capacity of Transformers, but this paper tackles the statistical consistency question: will the empirical error converge to the true error as the sample size increases? Extending this analysis to hyperbolic space is a significant contribution, as it accounts for data often residing on non-Euclidean manifolds. Furthermore, the construction of a Hyperbolic Transformer variant (HyT) and the proofs regarding its consistency and approximation are novel. The use of the Poincaré ball model to do this is a deliberate choice to facilitate the mathematics. However, the idea of using hyperbolic embeddings in neural networks is not entirely new; prior work has explored hyperbolic embeddings for various tasks, although not specifically for Transformer consistency proofs.

**Significance:**

The significance of this paper is threefold:

1.  **Theoretical Foundation:** It provides a more robust theoretical understanding of Transformer behavior, moving beyond representational capacity to statistical consistency. This is crucial for building trustworthy and reliable models.
2.  **Hyperbolic Generalization:** By demonstrating consistency in hyperbolic space, the paper opens the door to more effective Transformer applications in domains with hierarchical or graph-structured data (e.g., knowledge graphs, social networks), where hyperbolic embeddings are known to be advantageous.
3.  **Practical Implications:** The proposed HyT architecture and its empirical validation suggest a pathway to improved pre-training performance.  This could lead to more efficient and effective transfer learning in various applications.

**Strengths:**

*   **Rigorous Theoretical Analysis:** The paper provides detailed proofs and mathematical justifications for its claims.
*   **Generalization of Existing Theory:** It effectively generalizes existing Transformer theory to a non-Euclidean setting.
*   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-structured and clearly explains the concepts and results.
*   **Empirical Validation:** The empirical results support the theoretical findings and demonstrate the potential benefits of HyT.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Idealized Assumptions:** The analysis relies on certain assumptions (e.g., i.i.d. data, bounded support) that may not hold in all real-world scenarios. While necessary for tractability, these assumptions limit the direct applicability of the results. The authors acknowledge these limitations.
*   **Limited Empirical Scope:** The empirical evaluation is focused on pre-training performance, and the benefits of HyT in downstream tasks are not fully explored. A more comprehensive evaluation would strengthen the paper's impact.
*   **Practical Complexity:** Hyperbolic operations can be computationally more expensive than their Euclidean counterparts. The paper does not delve into the potential performance trade-offs associated with HyT in large-scale applications.
*   **Incremental Value:** While a solid contribution and well justified, the value lies mainly in applying previous knowledge about hyperbolic embedding and transformer architectures to perform statistical analysis about its universal approximation.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a solid and rigorous theoretical analysis, contributing to the fundamental understanding of Transformers. The generalization to hyperbolic space is a significant step, and the proposed HyT architecture shows promise. However, the reliance on idealized assumptions and the somewhat limited empirical scope prevent it from being a truly groundbreaking work. Prior work in hyperbolic embeddings is an area to take into account when measuring novelty. Thus, a score of 7 is appropriate. It's a valuable contribution that extends existing knowledge, rather than revolutionizing the field, while setting the stage for future practical use.

### Beyond Linear Steering: Unified Multi-Attribute Control for Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24535v1)
- **Authors**: Narmeen Oozeer, Luke Marks, Fazl Barez, Amirali Abdullah
- **Abstract**: Controlling multiple behavioral attributes in large language models (LLMs) at inference time is a challenging problem due to interference between attributes and the limitations of linear steering methods, which assume additive behavior in activation space and require per-attribute tuning. We introduce K-Steering, a unified and flexible approach that trains a single non-linear multi-label classifier on hidden activations and computes intervention directions via gradients at inference time. This avoids linearity assumptions, removes the need for storing and tuning separate attribute vectors, and allows dynamic composition of behaviors without retraining. To evaluate our method, we propose two new benchmarks, ToneBank and DebateMix, targeting compositional behavioral control. Empirical results across 3 model families, validated by both activation-based classifiers and LLM-based judges, demonstrate that K-Steering outperforms strong baselines in accurately steering multiple behaviors.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Beyond Linear Steering: Unified Multi-Attribute Control for Language Models," based on the provided text:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces K-Steering, a novel method for controlling multiple behavioral attributes in large language models (LLMs) at inference time.  Unlike existing linear steering approaches that require separate tuning for each attribute and assume additive behavior in the activation space, K-Steering trains a single, non-linear multi-label classifier on hidden activations. At inference time, it uses the classifier's gradients to adjust the model's behavior toward a specified set of target attributes. This avoids linearity assumptions, eliminates the need to store and tune separate attribute vectors, and allows dynamic composition of behaviors without retraining. The authors introduce two new benchmarks, TONEBANK and DEBATEMIX, to evaluate compositional behavioral control. Empirical results across three model families, validated by both activation-based classifiers and LLM-based judges, demonstrate that K-Steering outperforms strong baselines in accurately steering multiple behaviors. The paper also investigates multi-layer and multi-step steering, and presents an ablation method for efficient removal of undesired attributes.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Novel Approach:** K-Steering presents a genuinely novel approach to multi-attribute steering. It addresses a significant limitation of existing linear steering methods by leveraging a non-linear classifier to model complex interdependencies between attributes.
*   **Unified Framework:** The unified classifier approach is a significant advantage, simplifying the steering process and reducing the need for separate tuning of individual attribute vectors.
*   **Dynamic Composition:**  The ability to dynamically compose behaviors without retraining is a significant improvement in flexibility and usability.
*   **New Benchmarks:** The introduction of TONEBANK and DEBATEMIX is a valuable contribution, providing datasets specifically designed for multi-attribute steering evaluation.  These benchmarks are essential for pushing the field forward.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The paper provides a robust evaluation using both activation-based classifiers and LLM-based judges, providing strong evidence for the effectiveness of K-Steering.  The discussion of MMLU benchmark and classifier score comparisons is also good to support the findings and analysis.
*   **Ablation Method:** The projection removal technique and the analysis are a valuable addition.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Dataset limitations:** The authors acknowledge the limited generalizability of their method due to the custom-constructed nature of TONEBANK and DEBATEMIX, emphasizing the need for naturally occurring datasets with complex attribute compositions, posing questions regarding broader applicability of the method.
*   **Limited combinations:** The number of possible steering directions grows exponentially with the number of target behaviors. The evaluation is restricted to a maximum of three behaviors per dataset to keep the experimental scope tractable.
*   **Scalability of evaluation setup:** The binary search approach for calibrating steering magnitudes requires many costly LLM-based judge calls. In addition to the cost of many API calls for steering magnitude selection, evaluation against larger datasets may be prohibitively expensive due to the approach of using LLM judges. This limits the scalability of the experimental setup.
*   **Baseline Coverage:** The choice to benchmark against CAA (which itself outperforms other methods) helps reduce the experimental overhead for comprehensive comparisons. However, the performance of K-steering against additional baselines could help validate the efficacy of this method in comparison to other methods.
*   **Computational Cost:** While K-Steering shows strong performance, the multi-step steering approach introduces a significant computational overhead. The linear increase in computational cost compared to single-step steering presents a barrier for scalability in resource-constrained environments.

**Significance:**

This work is significant because it overcomes a major bottleneck in activation steering research: controlling multiple, potentially interacting, attributes in LLMs.  The unified classifier approach and the benchmarks provided contribute directly to advancing the capabilities and reliability of LLMs in real-world applications. By developing a light weight method for multi attribute control, this work has applications for numerous high stakes settings. It may also offer the ability to create more personalized and adaptive responses, which is useful for multiple tasks.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **8.5** to this paper.

**Rationale:**

K-Steering presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of activation steering. It addresses a critical limitation of existing linear methods and proposes an elegant solution that enables multi-attribute control and dynamic composition. The introduction of new benchmarks and a rigorous evaluation framework further strengthens the paper's value. While the computational overhead of multi-step steering and the data set limitations are important limitations, the paper introduces the necessary techniques and methods required to address this problem. The insights gained on the non-linear relationships between attributes is important and can help with additional work within this problem space. Because this work is novel, I find this is an important and valuable contribution that pushes the field forward.

### CHIP: Chameleon Hash-based Irreversible Passport for Robust Deep Model Ownership Verification and Active Usage Control
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24536v1)
- **Authors**: Chaohui Xu, Qi Cui, Chip-Hong Chang
- **Abstract**: The pervasion of large-scale Deep Neural Networks (DNNs) and their enormous training costs make their intellectual property (IP) protection of paramount importance. Recently introduced passport-based methods attempt to steer DNN watermarking towards strengthening ownership verification against ambiguity attacks by modulating the affine parameters of normalization layers. Unfortunately, neither watermarking nor passport-based methods provide a holistic protection with robust ownership proof, high fidelity, active usage authorization and user traceability for offline access distributed models and multi-user Machine-Learning as a Service (MLaaS) cloud model. In this paper, we propose a Chameleon Hash-based Irreversible Passport (CHIP) protection framework that utilizes the cryptographic chameleon hash function to achieve all these goals. The collision-resistant property of chameleon hash allows for strong model ownership claim upon IP infringement and liable user traceability, while the trapdoor-collision property enables hashing of multiple user passports and licensee certificates to the same immutable signature to realize active usage control. Using the owner passport as an oracle, multiple user-specific triplets, each contains a passport-aware user model, a user passport, and a licensee certificate can be created for secure offline distribution. The watermarked master model can also be deployed for MLaaS with usage permission verifiable by the provision of any trapdoor-colliding user passports. CHIP is extensively evaluated on four datasets and two architectures to demonstrate its protection versatility and robustness. Our code is released at https://github.com/Dshm212/CHIP.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CHIP (Chameleon Hash-based Irreversible Passport), a novel framework for protecting the intellectual property (IP) of deep neural networks (DNNs). CHIP aims to provide robust ownership verification, active usage control, and user traceability, addressing limitations in existing passport-based methods. It leverages cryptographic chameleon hash functions to create immutable signatures derived from the owner's passport and licensor certificate, allowing for strong ownership claims and liable user traceability. The trapdoor-collision property of chameleon hash enables the creation of user-specific triplets (user model, user passport, licensee certificate) for secure offline distribution and online MLaaS deployment with fine-grained access control. Experimental results on four datasets and two architectures demonstrate CHIP's effectiveness, fidelity, and robustness against ambiguity and removal attacks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The key novelty lies in the integration of cryptographic chameleon hash functions into a passport-based DNN IP protection scheme. This approach allows for an immutable signature for ownership verification while simultaneously enabling flexible user-specific passport generation through trapdoor collisions. This tackles the trade-off between strong ownership claims and flexible usage control present in previous methods. While passport-based methods exist, the specific combination of chameleon hash for collision management and the skip connection is novel.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a relevant and increasingly important problem in the field of DNN security. As DNN models become more valuable and expensive to train, robust IP protection mechanisms are crucial. CHIP's ability to provide both strong ownership verification *and* active usage control (in both online and offline scenarios) is a significant advantage over prior work. The framework is particularly beneficial in scenarios where DNN models are distributed to multiple users or deployed as a service (MLaaS). The traceability aspect also allows for the identification of malicious users distributing unauthorized models.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Holistic Approach:** CHIP offers a more comprehensive solution than previous passport-based methods, covering watermarking, usage authorization, and user traceability.
    *   **Robustness:** The experimental results demonstrate CHIP's resilience against ambiguity attacks (including those with oracle passports) and removal attempts through fine-tuning and transfer learning. The active control mechanism effectively restricts model usage to authorized users.
    *   **Flexibility:** The chameleon hash allows for efficient generation of user-specific passports without requiring retraining the model, making it scalable for multi-user scenarios.
    *   **Generalizability:** Validating the effectiveness on graph classification tasks (GINs) highlights its versatility beyond image classification.
    *   **Complete Code:** The release of their code supports reproducibility.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Computational Overhead:** While the authors address the training and inference time increase due to the additional complexity, there is a measurable impact on performance. Further optimization could improve the practical applicability of CHIP.
    *   **Reliance on Cryptographic Assumptions:** The security of CHIP relies on the hardness of the discrete logarithm problem, which is fundamental to chameleon hash. While well-established, future advancements in cryptanalysis could potentially weaken the protection offered by CHIP.
    *   **Detailed key management for multiple users**, although the paper handles multiple users, the key management for each can be complex.
    *   **Parameter tuning**: More sensitivity analyses with the parameters is needed, which can enhance its stability and robustness.

*   **Potential Impact:** CHIP has the potential to influence the design of future DNN IP protection schemes. The use of chameleon hash functions for managing user access and ownership claims is a valuable contribution. The framework could be integrated into existing DNN deployment pipelines to provide enhanced security and control. The findings can be used to develop better methods for DNN IP protection.
    *  The work provides an opensource library that can further be advanced in the near future.

*   **Comparison to Existing Work:**
While existing passport based works focus on robust ownership claims against model infringement, CHIP solves both the aspects of model ownership, and traceability on offline and online scenarios. Also, in comparision to the previous work, CHIP is robust against attacks and provides improved performance.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses, I assign a score of **7.5**. The paper provides a notable advancement in DNN IP protection by introducing a comprehensive framework that addresses multiple aspects of security and usage control. The use of chameleon hash is a clever approach, and the experimental results are convincing. However, the computational overhead and reliance on cryptographic assumptions are limitations that warrant further investigation. It's a strong, solid paper that advances the field.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Don't Erase, Inform! Detecting and Contextualizing Harmful Language in Cultural Heritage Collections
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24538v1)
- **Authors**: Orfeas Menis Mastromichalakis, Jason Liartis, Kristina Rose, Antoine Isaac, Giorgos Stamou
- **Abstract**: Cultural Heritage (CH) data hold invaluable knowledge, reflecting the history, traditions, and identities of societies, and shaping our understanding of the past and present. However, many CH collections contain outdated or offensive descriptions that reflect historical biases. CH Institutions (CHIs) face significant challenges in curating these data due to the vast scale and complexity of the task. To address this, we develop an AI-powered tool that detects offensive terms in CH metadata and provides contextual insights into their historical background and contemporary perception. We leverage a multilingual vocabulary co-created with marginalized communities, researchers, and CH professionals, along with traditional NLP techniques and Large Language Models (LLMs). Available as a standalone web app and integrated with major CH platforms, the tool has processed over 7.9 million records, contextualizing the contentious terms detected in their metadata. Rather than erasing these terms, our approach seeks to inform, making biases visible and providing actionable insights for creating more inclusive and accessible CH collections.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces an AI-powered tool to detect and contextualize harmful language within Cultural Heritage (CH) collections. The tool leverages a multilingual vocabulary, co-created with marginalized communities, researchers, and CH professionals, coupled with NLP techniques and Large Language Models (LLMs).  It aims to make biases visible and provide actionable insights for creating more inclusive and accessible CH collections, rather than simply erasing or censoring problematic terms. The paper details the vocabulary creation process, the architecture of the tool, evaluation using a newly created dataset, and its integration into platforms like Europeana and MINT.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The novelty of the paper lies primarily in the application of existing techniques (LLMs, knowledge graphs) to a specific and important domain: Cultural Heritage. While the individual components (string matching, lemmatization, NER, LLM-based disambiguation) are not revolutionary, their combination and application to CH metadata, coupled with a carefully co-created vocabulary, is a valuable contribution. The human-in-the-loop approach in vocabulary development is a significant strength, differentiating it from standard NLP models trained solely on readily available, and potentially biased, datasets. The release of the evaluation dataset is also a plus.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical ethical issue in CH collections: outdated and offensive language. The tool's ability to flag such terms and provide contextual information to CH professionals is highly significant. The approach of *informing* rather than *erasing* recognizes the historical context while promoting sensitivity and inclusivity. The integration of the tool into established CH platforms increases its potential impact.  The multilingual aspect is also important for reflecting the diverse nature of cultural heritage.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Co-creation of Vocabulary:** The involvement of marginalized communities in developing the vocabulary is a major strength, ensuring cultural sensitivity and accuracy.
    *   **Contextualization:**  The LLM-based disambiguation module helps to reduce false positives by assessing the context of the term, which is crucial for CH data.
    *   **Practical Application:**  The integration with Europeana and MINT demonstrates the tool's practical utility and potential for wide-scale adoption.
    *   **Evaluation Dataset:**  The release of the annotated evaluation dataset is a valuable resource for the research community.
    *   **Multilingual Support:** Addressing biases in a multilingual setting adds complexity and value to the work.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Focus of Evaluation:** The evaluation focuses primarily on *precision*, with a less emphasized *recall*. While minimizing false positives is important for user trust, achieving high recall, that is to detect all biased words present in CH data, is also important. The lack of random dataset in evaluation is also a weakness.
    *   **Computational Requirements:**  The high computational demands of the tool, specifically the need for a GPU, could limit accessibility for some institutions, although API/web access is provided. This could hinder adoption by smaller, less well-funded CH organizations.
    *   **Model Size Constraints:** It's unclear how the resource constraints affected the choice of LLMs, and how that might have reduced the performance. More experimentation with bigger models could have delivered better results.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence how CH institutions approach the curation of their metadata. The tool and vocabulary provide a framework for identifying and addressing biases, leading to more inclusive and representative digital collections. Other cultural institutions (e.g. libraries, archives) beyond CH could adapt it. The released dataset is also a benchmark for future research in the area.

*   **Critical Score Justification:**

The work addresses an important problem and makes a practical contribution. The human-centered approach, practical implementation, and release of a valuable dataset are strong points. The limitations, mainly concerning evaluation (limited to precision and not randomly generated), along with relatively high computational requirements, slightly reduce its overall impact.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Localizing Persona Representations in LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24539v1)
- **Authors**: Celia Cintas, Miriam Rateike, Erik Miehling, Elizabeth Daly, Skyler Speakman
- **Abstract**: We present a study on how and where personas -- defined by distinct sets of human characteristics, values, and beliefs -- are encoded in the representation space of large language models (LLMs). Using a range of dimension reduction and pattern recognition methods, we first identify the model layers that show the greatest divergence in encoding these representations. We then analyze the activations within a selected layer to examine how specific personas are encoded relative to others, including their shared and distinct embedding spaces. We find that, across multiple pre-trained decoder-only LLMs, the analyzed personas show large differences in representation space only within the final third of the decoder layers. We observe overlapping activations for specific ethical perspectives -- such as moral nihilism and utilitarianism -- suggesting a degree of polysemy. In contrast, political ideologies like conservatism and liberalism appear to be represented in more distinct regions. These findings help to improve our understanding of how LLMs internally represent information and can inform future efforts in refining the modulation of specific human traits in LLM outputs. Warning: This paper includes potentially offensive sample statements.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores how personas (defined by characteristics, values, and beliefs) are encoded within the representation space of Large Language Models (LLMs).  The researchers use dimension reduction and pattern recognition to identify model layers that show the most divergence in encoding personas. They analyze activations to examine how specific personas are encoded relative to others, finding that the final third of the decoder layers captures the most variance in persona representations. They observe overlaps in activations for ethical perspectives like moral nihilism and utilitarianism, suggesting polysemy, while political ideologies seem more distinctly represented. The work aims to enhance understanding of LLM internals and inform future efforts to refine the modulation of human traits in LLM outputs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**

    * **Addresses an Important and Underexplored Question:** Understanding how LLMs encode personas is crucial for responsible AI development.  Personas are widely used to steer LLM behavior, so knowing *where* and *how* they are encoded is vital for controlling and mitigating potential biases or unintended consequences. This work tackles a largely underexplored area.
    * **Systematic Methodology:**  The paper employs a systematic approach, using a combination of PCA, Deep Scan, and various clustering metrics to analyze LLM activations.  The methodology is well-defined and allows for quantitative assessment of persona encoding.
    * **Use of Publicly Available Datasets & Models:**  The reliance on a publicly available collection of personas and open-source LLMs (Llama3, Granite, Mistral) enhances reproducibility and allows other researchers to build upon this work.
    * **Layer-wise Analysis:** The layer-wise analysis is particularly valuable, revealing the trend of persona representations becoming more refined in the later layers of the models.  This suggests a hierarchical processing of information within LLMs.
    * **Identification of Distinct Encoding Patterns:** The finding that ethical views tend to exhibit polysemy (sharing activations) while political views are more distinctly localized is a significant observation with implications for how these models reason about different types of human values.
    * **Validation through Performance Measurement:** Performing well on precision and recall measures confirms the efficacy of DeepScan for identifying informative activations.

* **Weaknesses:**

    * **Limited Scope of Personas and Models:**  The study focuses on a specific set of personas (from one dataset) and three decoder-only LLMs.  Generalizability to other types of personas (e.g., demographic profiles) and other LLM architectures (e.g., encoder-decoder models, Mixture of Experts) remains an open question.
    * **Dataset Assumptions:** The dataset relies on model-generated statements and a specific labeling process, which may introduce its own biases or limitations.  While the authors acknowledge the dataset assumptions, they warrant further critical consideration. The assumption that a NOTMATCHINGBEHAVIOR label doesn't automatically imply MATCHINGBEHAVIOR for a *different* persona could be too strong. There's an implicit ordinality in many of these traits that isn't fully captured.
    * **Focus on Last-Token Embeddings:** The reliance on last-token embeddings may miss important information encoded in other parts of the input sequence. Justification is needed on how that choice could impact findings.
    * **Limited Interventions:** The paper focuses on analysis rather than intervention.  While the findings provide valuable insights, it doesn't directly address *how* these insights can be used to effectively steer or control LLM behavior.  The logical next step of editing these embeddings is lacking.
    * **WEIRD Data:** As admitted, the training dataset for these LLMs contain a bias towards Western, Educated, Industrialized, Rich, and Democratic perspectives. How might these personas be encoded in LLMs trained on data from different cultures or political systems?
    * **Lack of Control Groups/Baselines:** Comparing to simple techniques such as random selection of activations may have provided further validation of the DeepScan approach.

* **Novelty and Significance:**

    The paper provides a novel, systematic analysis of where and how personas are encoded in LLMs. While prior work has explored biases and ethical considerations in LLMs, this study contributes by:

    *   Providing a fine-grained, layer-wise analysis of persona representations.
    *   Identifying specific activation patterns associated with different types of personas.
    *   Quantifying the degree of polysemy versus distinctness in the encoding of ethical and political views.

* **Potential Impact:**

    The findings can inform future research on:

    *   Developing more effective methods for controlling and customizing LLM behavior based on desired persona traits.
    *   Mitigating biases associated with specific personas.
    *   Improving the interpretability and transparency of LLMs.
    *   Designing targeted interventions to refine the modulation of human traits.

**Score:** 7/10

**Justification:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field by shedding light on how personas are encoded within LLMs. The systematic methodology and clear findings are notable. However, the limitations regarding the scope of personas/models, the reliance on a specific dataset, and the lack of direct interventions reduce the overall impact. The score reflects the novelty and significance of the work while acknowledging areas for improvement and future research. The core findings regarding layer-wise encoding and the difference in ethical vs. political representation are important observations. For a higher score, the study would need to broaden its scope, address the limitations of the persona dataset, and ideally, demonstrate how the identified activation patterns can be used to effectively control LLM behavior.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Mixpert: Mitigating Multimodal Learning Conflicts with Efficient Mixture-of-Vision-Experts
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24541v1)
- **Authors**: Xin He, Xumeng Han, Longhui Wei, Lingxi Xie, Qi Tian
- **Abstract**: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) require a nuanced interpretation of complex image information, typically leveraging a vision encoder to perceive various visual scenarios. However, relying solely on a single vision encoder to handle diverse task domains proves difficult and inevitably leads to conflicts. Recent work enhances data perception by directly integrating multiple domain-specific vision encoders, yet this structure adds complexity and limits the potential for joint optimization. In this paper, we introduce Mixpert, an efficient mixture-of-vision-experts architecture that inherits the joint learning advantages from a single vision encoder while being restructured into a multi-expert paradigm for task-specific fine-tuning across different visual tasks. Additionally, we design a dynamic routing mechanism that allocates input images to the most suitable visual expert. Mixpert effectively alleviates domain conflicts encountered by a single vision encoder in multi-task learning with minimal additional computational cost, making it more efficient than multiple encoders. Furthermore, Mixpert integrates seamlessly into any MLLM, with experimental results demonstrating substantial performance gains across various tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the Mixpert paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the issue of domain conflicts in multimodal large language models (MLLMs) arising from using a single vision encoder to handle diverse visual tasks. It proposes Mixpert, an efficient mixture-of-vision-experts architecture. Mixpert partitions the vision encoder into a shared component (for extracting foundational features) and a multi-expert component (with each expert specializing in a specific domain like charts, documents, math, OCR, or general images).  A dynamic routing mechanism, based on an MLP, assigns input images to the most suitable expert during inference. This approach aims to mitigate task interference during fine-tuning while maintaining joint learning advantages.  Experimental results demonstrate performance gains across various MLLM tasks with minimal additional computational cost.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using a mixture-of-experts for vision encoders in MLLMs isn't entirely novel, as MoE has been explored in different forms for various parts of large models.  However, the specific application to the *vision encoder* within the MLLM pipeline, the *manual definition of expert domains*, and the *efficient routing mechanism* based on task-specific image characteristics contribute to the novelty of the approach. The routing mechanism designed to minimize computational overhead is a key differentiator.
*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the potential to improve the performance of MLLMs on diverse visual tasks *without* drastically increasing computational cost or parameter count. The paper provides empirical evidence that this is indeed the case. The fact that Mixpert can be seamlessly integrated into existing MLLMs like LLaVA, Qwen2-VL, and InternVL2 significantly enhances its practical value and potential for adoption.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of domain conflicts in MLLM vision encoders and the limitations of existing solutions.
    *   **Efficient Architecture:** Mixpert's architecture is designed for efficiency. The shared component reduces redundancy, and the dynamic routing mechanism avoids activating all experts for every input.
    *   **Task-Specific Specialization:** By assigning experts to specific domains, Mixpert enables task-specific fine-tuning, leading to performance gains.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The paper presents comprehensive experimental results across a range of datasets and MLLM architectures, demonstrating the effectiveness of Mixpert. The ablation studies provide insights into the design choices and the contribution of different components.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Manual Expert Domain Definition:**  The manual definition of expert domains (charts, documents, etc.) might be a limitation. It requires prior knowledge and could be sub-optimal for certain datasets or applications. An automatic expert discovery or adaptive domain assignment mechanism could improve the approach.
    *   **Routing Robustness:** While the routing mechanism seems effective, its robustness to noisy or ambiguous inputs could be a concern. Although the versatile expert acts as a fallback, it is unclear if the routing method can adapt in situations where the image can be classified into multiple domains.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper's contribution is likely to have a moderate impact on the field. The efficiency and demonstrated performance gains suggest that Mixpert could be a valuable addition to the MLLM toolkit. Future research could explore automatic domain discovery, more sophisticated routing mechanisms, and the application of Mixpert to other modalities or tasks.

**Rigorous Rationale:**

While the core idea of MoE isn't new, its specific implementation in Mixpert - the vision encoder context, manual expert definition for task specialization, a routing strategy to maintain efficiency - displays sufficient novelty. The empirical evidence convincingly demonstrates improved performance across multiple tasks and models without dramatically increasing computational costs. The manual definition of experts and the reliance on a straightforward routing network are potential limitations that need improvement in future iterations. The potential impact on the field lies in Mixpert's promise as an efficient way to manage domain conflicts and enhance visual perception in MLLMs. The ability to seamlessly integrate Mixpert into existing architectures adds considerable value. However, the reliance on manual expert definition limits the score from being too high.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Cross-Attention Speculative Decoding
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24544v1)
- **Authors**: Wei Zhong, Manasa Bharadwaj, Yixiao Wang, Nikhil Verma, Yipeng Ji, Chul Lee
- **Abstract**: Speculative decoding (SD) is a widely adopted approach for accelerating inference in large language models (LLMs), particularly when the draft and target models are well aligned. However, state-of-the-art SD methods typically rely on tightly coupled, self-attention-based Transformer decoders, often augmented with auxiliary pooling or fusion layers. This coupling makes them increasingly complex and harder to generalize across different models. We present Budget EAGLE (Beagle), the first, to our knowledge, cross-attention-based Transformer decoder SD model that achieves performance on par with leading self-attention SD models (EAGLE-v2) while eliminating the need for pooling or auxiliary components, simplifying the architecture, improving training efficiency, and maintaining stable memory usage during training-time simulation. To enable effective training of this novel architecture, we propose Two-Stage Block-Attention Training, a new method that achieves training stability and convergence efficiency in block-level attention scenarios. Extensive experiments across multiple LLMs and datasets show that Beagle achieves competitive inference speedups and higher training efficiency than EAGLE-v2, offering a strong alternative for architectures in speculative decoding.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Budget EAGLE" (Beagle), a novel speculative decoding (SD) architecture for accelerating large language model (LLM) inference.  Beagle uses a cross-attention-based Transformer decoder, which simplifies the architecture by eliminating the need for auxiliary pooling or fusion layers commonly found in state-of-the-art SD methods like EAGLE-v2.  To effectively train Beagle, the authors propose "Two-Stage Block-Attention Training," a new method to maintain training stability and improve convergence in block-level attention scenarios.  Experiments across various LLMs and datasets demonstrate that Beagle achieves competitive inference speedups and higher training efficiency than EAGLE-v2.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a reasonably novel architecture for speculative decoding.  While cross-attention is not entirely new in this domain (Du et al., Zimmer et al., B. Xiao et al.), the simplicity of the proposed cross-attention block (single layer without auxiliary layers) and the introduction of the Two-Stage Block-Attention training method contribute to the novelty. It is an architectural improvement in its simplified form.

*   **Significance:**  The significance of the paper lies in its potential to make speculative decoding more accessible and efficient across different LLM architectures.  Simplifying the architecture through cross-attention and improving training efficiency addresses crucial challenges in adopting SD methods. The experiments demonstrating competitive performance against EAGLE-v2 on a comparable dataset bolsters its significance, especially given the widespread use and reported efficiency of Eagle. Its simpler architecture is also likely to make it easier to integrate with various frameworks.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Architectural Simplicity:**  The cross-attention-based architecture is simpler compared to self-attention-based alternatives like EAGLE-v2, potentially making it easier to implement and generalize.
    *   **Training Efficiency:** The Two-Stage Block-Attention Training method is a significant contribution, enabling effective training without memory issues associated with other TTT methods
    *   **Performance:** The experimental results demonstrate that Beagle achieves competitive performance (speedups and acceptance lengths) compared to state-of-the-art methods while reducing the overheads

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited comparison:** The study limits its baseline to lossless decoding methods and focuses on single-batch greedy decoding. While this approach allows for a controlled comparison, it may overlook the potential benefits of lossy decoding and other decoding strategies.

    *   **Dataset Limitation:** The use of only ShareGPT for training could limit the generalizability of the model to other types of data or tasks. Further experiments should evaluate the performance of the model on a wider range of datasets.

    *   **Scaling:** The paper acknowledges that it has conducted experiments only on small-scale models due to hardware limitations. Further experiments need to be conducted on larger models to evaluate scalability.

*   **Impact:**
    The potential impact is the simplification of speculative decoding models to be more readily integrated with smaller data and on different devices. The simplification of this model can push new explorations in other domains, such as Vision-Language Modeling, as the authors state in the paper.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper is not *groundbreaking* in that it does not introduce completely new ideas. It builds on existing concepts (speculative decoding, cross-attention) but provides a valuable improvement. The simplicity is crucial and the two-stage approach is a significant practical contribution. While limitations exist regarding datasets and model size, the results are promising.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### A*-Thought: Efficient Reasoning via Bidirectional Compression for Low-Resource Settings
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24550v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaoang Xu, Shuo Wang, Xu Han, Zhenghao Liu, Huijia Wu, Peipei Li, Zhiyuan Liu, Maosong Sun, Zhaofeng He
- **Abstract**: Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) achieve superior performance by extending the thought length. However, a lengthy thinking trajectory leads to reduced efficiency. Most of the existing methods are stuck in the assumption of overthinking and attempt to reason efficiently by compressing the Chain-of-Thought, but this often leads to performance degradation. To address this problem, we introduce A*-Thought, an efficient tree search-based unified framework designed to identify and isolate the most essential thoughts from the extensive reasoning chains produced by these models. It formulates the reasoning process of LRMs as a search tree, where each node represents a reasoning span in the giant reasoning space. By combining the A* search algorithm with a cost function specific to the reasoning path, it can efficiently compress the chain of thought and determine a reasoning path with high information density and low cost. In addition, we also propose a bidirectional importance estimation mechanism, which further refines this search process and enhances its efficiency beyond uniform sampling. Extensive experiments on several advanced math tasks show that A*-Thought effectively balances performance and efficiency over a huge search space. Specifically, A*-Thought can improve the performance of QwQ-32B by 2.39$\times$ with low-budget and reduce the length of the output token by nearly 50% with high-budget. The proposed method is also compatible with several other LRMs, demonstrating its generalization capability. The code can be accessed at: https://github.com/AI9Stars/AStar-Thought.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces A*-Thought, a novel tree search-based framework designed to improve the efficiency of large reasoning models (LRMs) in low-resource settings.  A*-Thought aims to identify and isolate the most essential thoughts from extensive reasoning chains by formulating the reasoning process as a search tree. The method combines the A* search algorithm with a cost function tailored to the reasoning path, enabling efficient compression of the chain of thought and identifying paths with high information density and low cost.  A key component is a bidirectional importance estimation mechanism (BIS) that refines the search process. Experiments on math tasks demonstrate that A*-Thought effectively balances performance and efficiency, improving performance and reducing output token length. The method is shown to be compatible with multiple LRMs, suggesting generalization capability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel approach to reasoning efficiency in LRMs. While the idea of compressing or shortening reasoning chains is not entirely new, the use of A* search combined with a bidirectional importance score (BIS) is a unique contribution. The specific formulation of the cost functions, considering both path quality and conditional self-information of the solution, distinguishes this work. Furthermore, the bidirectional approach to estimating the importance of each thought step is novel and potentially impactful. Prior work typically focuses on only the question or the final answer.

*   **Significance:** The significance lies in the potential to reduce the computational cost of using LRMs, making them more accessible in resource-constrained environments. The ability to improve performance while simultaneously reducing output length is valuable. The experimental results demonstrate substantial gains compared to several representative baselines, suggesting the practical utility of the approach. The compatibility across multiple LRMs further increases the significance.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The A* search provides a principled way to explore the vast space of possible reasoning chains.
    *   The bidirectional importance score (BIS) is a well-motivated and novel component, enhancing the efficiency of A* search.
    *   The experimental evaluation is thorough, covering multiple tasks, baselines, and model architectures.
    *   The results demonstrate a clear improvement in both performance and efficiency, especially in low-resource scenarios.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The A* search is highly dependent on the quality of the cost functions. While the paper provides a rationale for their design, further ablation studies and sensitivity analysis regarding the cost function parameters (α and β) would strengthen the analysis. Although Appendix does provide more detail, stronger reasoning on cost function impact would elevate the quality.
    *   The evaluation focuses mainly on mathematical reasoning tasks. While these tasks are challenging, it would be beneficial to demonstrate the effectiveness of A*-Thought on other types of reasoning tasks, such as commonsense reasoning or logical inference.
    *   The comparison baselines, although relevant, are not comprehensive. Including more competitive baselines or state-of-the-art methods would provide a stronger benchmark for A*-Thought.
    *   There is limited analysis of the types of "thoughts" that are deemed most important by BIS.  Understanding what characteristics of the reasoning steps are favored could provide further insights and suggest improvements.

*   **Potential Impact:**
    *   The paper has the potential to influence the design of more efficient reasoning methods for LRMs.
    *   The A*-Thought framework could be extended to other tasks and domains.
    *   The BIS mechanism could be incorporated into other reasoning efficiency techniques.
    *   The work could contribute to making LRMs more accessible for on-device or edge computing applications.

*   **Reasons for the score:** While the paper presents a novel and promising approach, the limitations mentioned above prevent it from receiving a higher score. The dependence on cost function parameters, the relatively narrow task domain, and the non-exhaustive set of baselines suggest that further research is needed to fully validate the effectiveness and generalizability of A*-Thought. It's a solid contribution, but needs more extensive validation.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Bench4KE: Benchmarking Automated Competency Question Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24554v1)
- **Authors**: Anna Sofia Lippolis, Minh Davide Ragagni, Paolo Ciancarini, Andrea Giovanni Nuzzolese, Valentina Presutti
- **Abstract**: The availability of Large Language Models (LLMs) presents a unique opportunity to reinvigorate research on Knowledge Engineering (KE) automation, a trend already evident in recent efforts developing LLM-based methods and tools for the automatic generation of Competency Questions (CQs). However, the evaluation of these tools lacks standardisation. This undermines the methodological rigour and hinders the replication and comparison of results. To address this gap, we introduce Bench4KE, an extensible API-based benchmarking system for KE automation. Its first release focuses on evaluating tools that generate CQs automatically. CQs are natural language questions used by ontology engineers to define the functional requirements of an ontology. Bench4KE provides a curated gold standard consisting of CQ datasets from four real-world ontology projects. It uses a suite of similarity metrics to assess the quality of the CQs generated. We present a comparative analysis of four recent CQ generation systems, which are based on LLMs, establishing a baseline for future research. Bench4KE is also designed to accommodate additional KE automation tasks, such as SPARQL query generation, ontology testing and drafting. Code and datasets are publicly available under the Apache 2.0 license.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Bench4KE, an API-based benchmarking system for evaluating Knowledge Engineering (KE) automation tools, with an initial focus on automated Competency Question (CQ) generation. CQs are natural language questions used to define ontology requirements. Bench4KE provides a curated gold standard of CQ datasets from real-world ontology projects and uses various similarity metrics (cosine, Jaccard, BLEU, ROUGE-L, sBERT, and LLM-based scoring) to assess the quality of generated CQs. The authors present a comparative analysis of four existing LLM-based CQ generation systems, establishing a baseline for future research. The system is designed to be extensible to accommodate other KE automation tasks like SPARQL query generation, ontology testing, and drafting. Code and datasets are available under an Apache 2.0 license.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses a clear gap:** The paper effectively identifies a significant problem in the field: the lack of standardized evaluation methods for automated CQ generation, which hinders progress and comparability.
    *   **Comprehensive approach:** Bench4KE offers a multifaceted solution, encompassing a curated dataset, a suite of similarity metrics, and a modular, extensible architecture.
    *   **Practical focus:** The system is designed for real-world use, supporting different input modalities (structured data and user stories) and providing both numerical and visual evaluation results.
    *   **Extensibility:** The system is designed to support the evaluation of diverse KE automation tasks in the future, making it more than a single-purpose benchmark.
    *   **Community Involvement:** Active inclusion of researchers in the early testing phase and collecting feedback.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited LLM Support:** Currently only supports OpenAI's GPT-4. While this is a powerful model, it limits the scope of the benchmark. Supporting a more diverse set of models, including open-source options, would enhance its value.
    *   **Reliance on Similarity Metrics:** The reliance on similarity metrics can be a limitation, as these metrics might not fully capture semantic equivalence, especially when paraphrasing is involved. While they address this with an LLM based scoring, it still is not a perfect method of evaluation.
    *   **Limited Dataset Scenarios:** The number of dataset-based scenarios is limited compared to story-based scenarios. This limits evaluating how systems perform on different source data.
    *   **Implementation Status:** While the API is designed, it is currently only available for local testing.

*   **Novelty:**

    *   The *combination* of a curated gold standard dataset *specifically for CQ generation*, a suite of diverse similarity metrics, and an extensible API-based architecture is indeed novel.
    *   The systematization of the evaluation process, coupled with community involvement, distinguishes Bench4KE from previous efforts that were often more ad-hoc.
    *   Extensibility into diverse KE tasks represents also a novel contribution.

*   **Significance:**

    *   Bench4KE has the potential to become a valuable community resource for driving progress in automated CQ generation and other KE tasks.
    *   By providing a standardized evaluation framework, it can facilitate fair comparisons between different approaches and identify areas for improvement.
    *   The extensibility of the system makes it a long-term investment in the field, as it can be adapted to evaluate new techniques and address evolving challenges.
    *   Providing a reference benchmark allows easier comparison of new tools for CQ generation.

**Justification of Score:**

Bench4KE makes a significant contribution by addressing a clear and existing gap in the KE field. The comprehensive approach, practical focus, extensibility, and initial community involvement are very strong points. Limitations on LLM support and dataset variation are definitely weak points. With all taken into account, I believe that the paper is a significant resource for comparing different CQ generation techniques, setting up a valuable research basis, and overall allowing better progress to be made on the topic.

**Score: 7.5**
