- **Score**: 7/10

### Mixture-of-Experts for Personalized and Semantic-Aware Next Location Prediction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24597v1)
- **Authors**: Shuai Liu, Ning Cao, Yile Chen, Yue Jiang, Gao Cong
- **Abstract**: Next location prediction plays a critical role in understanding human mobility patterns. However, existing approaches face two core limitations: (1) they fall short in capturing the complex, multi-functional semantics of real-world locations; and (2) they lack the capacity to model heterogeneous behavioral dynamics across diverse user groups. To tackle these challenges, we introduce NextLocMoE, a novel framework built upon large language models (LLMs) and structured around a dual-level Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) design. Our architecture comprises two specialized modules: a Location Semantics MoE that operates at the embedding level to encode rich functional semantics of locations, and a Personalized MoE embedded within the Transformer backbone to dynamically adapt to individual user mobility patterns. In addition, we incorporate a history-aware routing mechanism that leverages long-term trajectory data to enhance expert selection and ensure prediction stability. Empirical evaluations across several real-world urban datasets show that NextLocMoE achieves superior performance in terms of predictive accuracy, cross-domain generalization, and interpretability
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces NextLocMoE, a novel framework for next location prediction that leverages large language models (LLMs) and a dual-level Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architecture.  It addresses two key limitations in existing approaches: the inability to capture the multifaceted semantics of real-world locations and the lack of capacity to model heterogeneous user behaviors.  NextLocMoE incorporates a Location Semantics MoE module to encode rich functional location semantics and a Personalized MoE module within the Transformer backbone to adapt to individual user mobility patterns. A history-aware router is incorporated to improve expert selection stability. Empirical evaluations demonstrate superior performance in predictive accuracy, cross-domain generalization, and interpretability compared to existing baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The novelty lies primarily in the innovative application of the Mixture-of-Experts architecture within the context of LLM-based next location prediction and the specific design choices made for each MoE level. The dual-level MoE (Location Semantics MoE and Personalized MoE) is a novel approach to jointly model complex location semantics and diverse user behavior patterns. The history-aware router further enhances the system's novelty by incorporating long-term contextual information. While MoEs and LLMs have been used individually in other domains, their combination in this specific manner and for this specific task represents a significant contribution. The use of LLM embeddings to initialize location and user group experts improves interpretability and guides model specialization.

**Significance:**

The significance of this work stems from its potential to improve the accuracy, generalization capability, and interpretability of next location prediction systems. Accurate location prediction has broad applications in areas like intelligent transportation, personalized services, and urban management. The ability to capture complex location semantics and model diverse user behaviors is crucial for building robust and reliable systems. The proposed NextLocMoE framework addresses these limitations, resulting in improved predictive performance compared to existing methods. The demonstrated cross-domain generalization ability suggests that the framework can be deployed in new cities without extensive fine-tuning. The improved interpretability, achieved through expert-specific priors, can provide valuable insights into user mobility patterns and urban dynamics.

**Strengths:**

*   **Novel Architecture:** The dual-level MoE architecture is a key strength, effectively addressing the limitations of previous approaches.
*   **Improved Performance:** The empirical results demonstrate superior performance compared to a wide range of baselines across multiple datasets.
*   **Cross-Domain Generalization:** The ability to generalize to new cities is a significant advantage.
*   **Interpretability:** The expert-specific priors improve the interpretability of the model.
*   **Efficiency:** Streamlined design and LoRA adaptation reduce computational overhead.
*   **Thorough evaluation:** Comprehensive experiments covering multiple dimensions: accuracy, generalization, speed.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Computational cost:** The paper acknowledges the training-time memory costs due to the full FFN sub-networks per user group expert. While LoRA is used for parameter efficiency, the base FFN size dictates memory usage.
*   **Reliance on pre-defined categories:** The location function categories and user group categories are pre-defined. The performance of the system may be sensitive to the quality and completeness of these categories. Future work could explore methods for automatically discovering and refining these categories.
*   **Complexity:** The dual-level MoE architecture increases the complexity of the model, which may make it more difficult to debug and maintain.

**Justification of Score:**

I am assigning a score of **8**.  The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of next location prediction. The combination of LLMs and MoEs, along with the history-aware router, represents a creative and effective solution to the limitations of existing methods. The empirical results demonstrate significant improvements in predictive accuracy and cross-domain generalization. While the computational cost and reliance on pre-defined categories are potential weaknesses, the strengths of the paper outweigh these limitations. The improved interpretability and efficiency compared to full finetuning of larger LLMs also increases its value. This work is likely to influence future research in next location prediction and other related fields.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 0/10

### Harnessing Large Language Models for Scientific Novelty Detection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24615v1)
- **Authors**: Yan Liu, Zonglin Yang, Soujanya Poria, Thanh-Son Nguyen, Erik Cambria
- **Abstract**: In an era of exponential scientific growth, identifying novel research ideas is crucial and challenging in academia. Despite potential, the lack of an appropriate benchmark dataset hinders the research of novelty detection. More importantly, simply adopting existing NLP technologies, e.g., retrieving and then cross-checking, is not a one-size-fits-all solution due to the gap between textual similarity and idea conception. In this paper, we propose to harness large language models (LLMs) for scientific novelty detection (ND), associated with two new datasets in marketing and NLP domains. To construct the considerate datasets for ND, we propose to extract closure sets of papers based on their relationship, and then summarize their main ideas based on LLMs. To capture idea conception, we propose to train a lightweight retriever by distilling the idea-level knowledge from LLMs to align ideas with similar conception, enabling efficient and accurate idea retrieval for LLM novelty detection. Experiments show our method consistently outperforms others on the proposed benchmark datasets for idea retrieval and ND tasks. Codes and data are available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/NoveltyDetection-10FB/.
- **Summary**: **Summary**

This paper addresses the challenge of scientific novelty detection (ND) in an era of exponential growth of academic literature. Recognizing the limitations of existing NLP techniques and the lack of tailored datasets, the authors propose a novel approach that harnesses large language models (LLMs). They introduce two new ND-tailored benchmark datasets in the marketing and NLP domains constructed with topological closure and compactness. The authors present an LLM-based knowledge distillation (KD) framework to train an idea retriever, aiming to bridge the gap between surface-level text similarity and deeper idea conception. The paper evaluates their method on idea retrieval and ND tasks, demonstrating improved performance over existing approaches.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's main contributions are in the construction of ND-tailored benchmark datasets with closure property and compactness as well as the LLM-based KD framework for idea retrieval. The novelty lies in the approach to distil idea-level knowledge from LLMs into a lightweight retriever, which potentially aligns with idea conception compared to relying solely on text similarity.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical challenge in academia. The ability to identify truly novel research ideas is crucial for advancing knowledge and innovation. The benchmarks can potentially improve the process of knowledge discovery and help prevent redundant research efforts. Furthermore, the LLM-based KD framework provides a direction for automating novelty detection and has the potential to improve efficiency.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The dataset construction approach, ensuring closure property and compactness, is a valuable contribution that can aid in ND research.
    *   The LLM-based KD framework offers a novel way to bridge the gap between text similarity and idea conception.
    *   Experimental results demonstrate the superior performance of the proposed method in idea retrieval and ND tasks.
    *   The authors provide detailed descriptions of their methodology, including datasets and implementation details.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   While the approach uses LLMs and the performance metrics are provided, the specific aspects related to computational complexity, performance and resources required, and performance limitations are not explicitly mentioned.
    *   The LLM-generated non-novel ideas and ND scores are not guaranteed to be fully consistent. Such noise may affect the quality of retriever fine-tuning and ND.
    *   The framework currently models ND as a binary classification task. Novelty, in practice, is more of continuous scale.
    *   Experiments are conducted on two domains, which may not generalize to all scientific domains.

**Justification**

The paper presents a significant contribution to the field of ND by addressing the limitations of existing methods and introducing a novel LLM-based approach. The construction of ND-tailored benchmark datasets is a particularly valuable contribution. However, the paper has limitations in terms of evaluating computational cost, noise in LLM-generated data, binary classification modelling, and generality.

### Eye of Judgement: Dissecting the Evaluation of Russian-speaking LLMs with POLLUX
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24616v1)
- **Authors**: Nikita Martynov, Anastasia Mordasheva, Dmitriy Gorbetskiy, Danil Astafurov, Ulyana Isaeva, Elina Basyrova, Sergey Skachkov, Victoria Berestova, Nikolay Ivanov, Valeriia Zanina, Alena Fenogenova
- **Abstract**: We introduce POLLUX, a comprehensive open-source benchmark designed to evaluate the generative capabilities of large language models (LLMs) in Russian. Our main contribution is a novel evaluation methodology that enhances the interpretability of LLM assessment. For each task type, we define a set of detailed criteria and develop a scoring protocol where models evaluate responses and provide justifications for their ratings. This enables transparent, criteria-driven evaluation beyond traditional resource-consuming, side-by-side human comparisons. POLLUX includes a detailed, fine-grained taxonomy of 35 task types covering diverse generative domains such as code generation, creative writing, and practical assistant use cases, totaling 2,100 manually crafted and professionally authored prompts. Each task is categorized by difficulty (easy/medium/hard), with experts constructing the dataset entirely from scratch. We also release a family of LLM-as-a-Judge (7B and 32B) evaluators trained for nuanced assessment of generative outputs. This approach provides scalable, interpretable evaluation and annotation tools for model development, effectively replacing costly and less precise human judgments.
- **Summary**: **POLLUX: Dissecting the Evaluation of Russian-speaking LLMs**

This paper introduces POLLUX, a new open-source benchmark designed to evaluate the generative capabilities of large language models (LLMs) in the Russian language. The authors highlight a novel evaluation methodology that emphasizes interpretability by defining detailed criteria for each task type and developing a scoring protocol where models evaluate responses and justify their ratings. The benchmark includes a fine-grained taxonomy of 35 task types spanning diverse generative domains and comprises 2,100 manually crafted prompts. Additionally, the authors release LLM-as-a-Judge models (7B and 32B parameters) trained for nuanced assessments of generative outputs.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper presents a welcome contribution to the under-explored area of non-English LLM evaluation. Developing a comprehensive benchmark for Russian LLMs and introducing a detailed, criteria-driven evaluation framework addresses a significant gap in the field. The released LLM-as-a-Judge models provide a valuable, scalable tool for automated assessment, potentially reducing reliance on human judgment.

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive Dataset:** The dataset of 2,100 manually crafted prompts across 35 diverse task types presents a robust and comprehensive evaluation resource.
*   **Interpretability:** The criteria-driven evaluation methodology and justification protocol enhance the transparency and interpretability of LLM assessment.
*   **Scalability:** The LLM-as-a-Judge models offer a scalable approach to generative output evaluation.
*   **Language Focus:** Addressing the specific characteristics and challenges of the Russian language is a significant contribution.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Subjectivity:** While the criteria are well-defined, there remains a degree of subjectivity in evaluating generative outputs. The paper could benefit from exploring methods to further mitigate this subjectivity.
*   **Limited Scope:** The benchmark, while comprehensive, might not cover all potential generative tasks relevant to Russian-speaking users. Further expansion and adaptation of the benchmark would be beneficial.
*   **Model Bias:** As with any LLM, the LLM-as-a-Judge models may exhibit biases present in their training data. The paper should address this limitation explicitly, and ideally include an analysis of potential biases in the released models.

**Potential Influence:**

POLLUX has the potential to significantly influence the development and evaluation of Russian-speaking LLMs. The benchmark provides a valuable resource for researchers and practitioners working in this area, and the LLM-as-a-Judge models offer a scalable tool for automated assessment. However, the impact of POLLUX will depend on its adoption by the community and its ability to accurately reflect the nuances of human judgment. Further analysis on a broad range of models is necessary.

**Conclusion:**

While recognizing the limitations, POLLUX demonstrates significant added value for the field of Russian-speaking LLM evaluation by providing a methodologically sound benchmark. The work offers an efficient resource for continued research and innovation.

### Benchmarking Large Language Models for Cryptanalysis and Mismatched-Generalization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24621v1)
- **Authors**: Utsav Maskey, Chencheng Zhu, Usman Naseem
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in Large Language Models (LLMs) have transformed natural language understanding and generation, leading to extensive benchmarking across diverse tasks. However, cryptanalysis a critical area for data security and encryption has not yet been thoroughly explored in LLM evaluations. To address this gap, we evaluate cryptanalytic potential of state of the art LLMs on encrypted texts generated using a range of cryptographic algorithms. We introduce a novel benchmark dataset comprising diverse plain texts spanning various domains, lengths, writing styles, and topics paired with their encrypted versions. Using zero-shot and few shot settings, we assess multiple LLMs for decryption accuracy and semantic comprehension across different encryption schemes. Our findings reveal key insights into the strengths and limitations of LLMs in side-channel communication while raising concerns about their susceptibility to jailbreaking attacks. This research highlights the dual-use nature of LLMs in security contexts and contributes to the ongoing discussion on AI safety and security.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, with a focus on novelty and significance, and assign a justified score.

**Summary:**

The paper "Benchmarking Large Language Models for Cryptanalysis and Mismatched-Generalization" introduces a novel benchmark dataset for evaluating the cryptanalytic capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). The dataset comprises diverse plaintexts, including both human-written and LLM-generated text across various domains and lengths, paired with their encrypted versions using a range of cryptographic algorithms. The authors evaluate several state-of-the-art LLMs in zero-shot and few-shot settings, assessing their decryption accuracy, semantic comprehension of encrypted texts, and susceptibility to jailbreaking attacks exploiting vulnerabilities. The results reveal that LLMs primarily succeed with basic ciphers prevalent in their pre-training data and struggle with more complex encryption schemes or those involving high token inflation. Moreover, the study uncovers potential security implications, highlighting that partial comprehension of encrypted texts can make LLMs susceptible to jailbreaking attempts. The research suggests a need for LLM safety training to account for these vulnerabilities and explores various mitigation strategies.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the *creation of a comprehensive benchmark dataset specifically designed for evaluating LLMs in cryptanalysis*. While individual encryption/decryption tasks have been explored before, this paper compiles a structured dataset with varied domains, lengths, writing styles, and encryption algorithms. The study also makes a good effort in addressing mismatched-generalization and its impact on AI safety. However, the techniques for cryptanalysis of LLMs are not novel, and the study primarily relies on existing prompting techniques.

*   **Significance:** The paper is significant for several reasons:

    *   **Addressing a gap:** It addresses a crucial gap in LLM evaluation by focusing on cryptanalysis, a domain relevant to data security and ethical AI.
    *   **Highlighting vulnerabilities:** It highlights a potential vulnerability: the capacity of LLMs for partial comprehension of encrypted text, which could be exploited in jailbreaking attacks. This insight is valuable for the ongoing discussion on AI safety and security.
    *   **Practical implications:** It has practical implications for developing safeguards for LLMs to prevent their misuse in malicious activities. It points to the need for safety training tailored to address vulnerabilities arising from partial comprehension.
    *   **Informing Dataset Design:** The analysis of why certain LLMs fail on particular ciphers (e.g. Bacon cipher) also adds value to the research community on improving data set coverage that the models are trained on.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Dataset:** The creation of the benchmark dataset is a significant strength. This enables more systematic and reproducible evaluation of LLMs in cryptanalysis.
    *   **Detailed Analysis:** The paper provides a detailed analysis of the performance of different LLMs across various encryption schemes and text complexities.
    *   **Security Implications:** The identification of potential vulnerabilities related to partial comprehension is a valuable contribution.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly presents its findings.
    *   **Ethical Considerations:** Addressing the dual-use nature of LLMs and the ethical considerations of this type of research is a positive point.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Cryptographic Complexity:** The encryption algorithms used in the benchmark dataset, while diverse, are primarily classical and relatively straightforward. Evaluating against more complex, modern cryptographic methods would be valuable.
    *   **Generic Prompting Techniques:** The study primarily relies on zero-shot and few-shot prompting. While these methods are relevant to jailbreaking scenarios, exploring more advanced prompting techniques tailored for cryptanalysis could provide a more comprehensive evaluation.
    *   **Lack of Mitigation Strategies Evaluation**: Though the authors point out at several mitigations in the literature, they don't actually conduct an evaluation of their effectiveness against the demonstrated vulnerabilities.
    *   **Limited Model Scope:** While several LLMs were evaluated, a greater variety of open-source LLMs, along with their architecture types could have been explored.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research in the following areas:

    *   Development of more robust evaluation metrics for LLMs in security-sensitive domains.
    *   Development of new safety training techniques to address vulnerabilities related to partial comprehension.
    *   Further investigation into the potential of LLMs for both offensive and defensive cybersecurity applications.

**Justification:**

The paper introduces a novel benchmark dataset for evaluating cryptanalysis using LLMs, which makes a significant contribution to the field. It identifies a key vulnerability of LLMs in partially comprehending encrypted long-tail texts, which could be used to execute jailbreak attacks and highlights areas of data set coverage to be improved. The study is a comprehensive piece of work that provides several key directions to be pursued in LLM safety and dual-use AI research. However, the study is limited by lack of novel methods and a mitigation analysis.

### Random Rule Forest (RRF): Interpretable Ensembles of LLM-Generated Questions for Predicting Startup Success
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24622v1)
- **Authors**: Ben Griffin, Joseph Ternasky, Fuat Alican, Yigit Ihlamur
- **Abstract**: Predicting startup success requires models that are both accurate and interpretable. We present a lightweight ensemble framework that combines YES/NO questions generated by large language models (LLMs), forming a transparent decision-making system. Each question acts as a weak heuristic, and by filtering, ranking, and aggregating them through a threshold-based voting mechanism, we construct a strong ensemble predictor. On a test set where 10% of startups are classified as successful, our approach achieves a precision rate of 50%, representing a 5x improvement over random selection, while remaining fully transparent. When we incorporate expert-guided heuristics into the generation process, performance improves further to 54% precision. These results highlight the value of combining LLM reasoning with human insight and demonstrate that simple, interpretable ensembles can support high-stakes decisions in domains such as venture capital (VC).
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary**

The paper introduces Random Rule Forest (RRF), a novel framework for predicting startup success. RRF uses interpretable YES/NO questions generated by Large Language Models (LLMs) as weak heuristics. By filtering, ranking, and aggregating these questions using a threshold-based voting mechanism, the framework creates a transparent and accurate ensemble predictor. The paper also includes a feedback-driven pipeline for refining question quality using real startup data and an expert-in-the-loop extension that incorporates domain knowledge into the question generation process.  Experiments show RRF achieving a 5x improvement over random selection in precision.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The paper combines LLM-generated insights with ensemble methods in a relatively novel way, specifically targeting interpretability in high-stakes domains like VC.  The iterative feedback loop and expert-in-the-loop refinement are valuable contributions to the overall methodology. While individual components (LLMs, ensembles, feature engineering) are well-established, their specific integration within the RRF framework for this application is unique.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial need in the VC space: balancing predictive performance with transparency.  The RRF framework offers a practical alternative to "black-box" models, enabling decision-makers to understand the reasoning behind predictions.  The empirical results, while modest, demonstrate the potential of this approach to support high-stakes decisions.  The framework's adaptability and expandability further enhance its significance.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Interpretability:** The framework's use of simple YES/NO questions provides a high degree of interpretability, making it easier for humans to understand and trust the model's predictions.
    *   **Practicality:** The modular design of RRF allows for easy integration of new data sources and expert knowledge, making it adaptable to real-world VC environments.
    *   **Clear Methodology:** The paper provides a clear and detailed description of the RRF framework, including the data preprocessing, question generation, filtering, and ensemble construction steps.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Data:** The dataset size is relatively small, which may limit the generalizability of the results. The success rate being set at 10% in the validation and test sets differs significantly from the actual success rates in the real world.
    *   **Modest Performance Gains:** While the 5x improvement over random selection is significant, the absolute precision rate of 50-54% may still be too low for practical application in VC.
    *   **LLM Dependency:** The framework's performance is highly dependent on the quality of the LLM-generated questions. The paper does not explore the impact of using different LLMs or prompting strategies.
    *   **Over-Claiming Significance:** The paper claims "full transparency" which is an overstatement. While individual questions are interpretable, the ensemble's collective behavior, particularly with thresholding, can still obscure complex interactions.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could inspire further research on combining LLMs with ensemble methods for interpretable machine learning. The framework's modularity and adaptability make it a promising starting point for developing more sophisticated decision-support systems in high-stakes domains. The paper highlights the importance of transparency in AI-driven decision-making, which could influence the development of more ethical and responsible AI systems.
*   **Conclusion**: The paper has some good qualities, particularly focusing on interpretability and combining LLM-derived insights with ensemble methods, which is valuable. However, a small dataset, a specific dataset configuration that does not align with real-world ratios, modest performance, and an over-claiming of significance diminishes the impact.
