### Learning from Videos for 3D World: Enhancing MLLMs with 3D Vision Geometry Priors
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24625v1)
- **Authors**: Duo Zheng, Shijia Huang, Yanyang Li, Liwei Wang
- **Abstract**: Previous research has investigated the application of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) in understanding 3D scenes by interpreting them as videos. These approaches generally depend on comprehensive 3D data inputs, such as point clouds or reconstructed Bird's-Eye View (BEV) maps. In our research, we advance this field by enhancing the capability of MLLMs to understand and reason in 3D spaces directly from video data, without the need for additional 3D input. We propose a novel and efficient method, the Video-3D Geometry Large Language Model (VG LLM). Our approach employs a 3D visual geometry encoder that extracts 3D prior information from video sequences. This information is integrated with visual tokens and fed into the MLLM. Extensive experiments have shown that our method has achieved substantial improvements in various tasks related to 3D scene understanding and spatial reasoning, all directly learned from video sources. Impressively, our 4B model, which does not rely on explicit 3D data inputs, achieves competitive results compared to existing state-of-the-art methods, and even surpasses the Gemini-1.5-Pro in the VSI-Bench evaluations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces VG LLM, a novel approach to enhance the 3D scene understanding capabilities of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) using video input. The core idea is to incorporate 3D visual geometry priors without relying on explicit 3D data like point clouds or depth maps. VG LLM uses a pre-trained 3D visual geometry encoder to extract 3D prior information from video sequences and fuse it with visual tokens before feeding them into the MLLM backbone. Experiments demonstrate significant improvements in tasks like 3D visual grounding, dense captioning, and video object detection, even surpassing Gemini-1.5-Pro on the VSI-Bench spatial reasoning benchmark. The model shows strong egocentric-allocentric transformation capabilities and achieves competitive results compared to methods that require explicit 3D data.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the architecture and training strategy that allows an MLLM to understand 3D scenes directly from video, without explicit 3D data input. This is a valuable contribution, addressing a limitation of previous works that heavily rely on dense 3D representations, which may not always be available or accurate. The integration of a pre-trained 3D visual geometry encoder to inject spatial priors is a sound approach, and the ablation studies confirm its effectiveness. However, the idea of using pre-trained encoders to inject knowledge into LLMs is not entirely new; the novelty resides in the specific application to 3D scene understanding from video.

*   **Significance:** The paper presents compelling results that demonstrate the potential of learning 3D scene understanding directly from video data. This has significant implications for various applications, including robotics, embodied AI, and augmented reality, where acquiring dense 3D data can be challenging. The fact that a relatively small (4B) model can outperform larger, specialized models (and even proprietary models like Gemini-1.5-Pro on some benchmarks) highlights the effectiveness of the proposed approach. The demonstration of strong egocentric-allocentric transformation capabilities is also a significant contribution, indicating that the model is learning meaningful spatial relationships.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Statement:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing MLLM-based approaches for 3D scene understanding.
    *   **Effective Architecture:** The proposed VG LLM architecture is well-designed and the integration of the 3D visual geometry encoder is justified.
    *   **Strong Experimental Results:** The paper presents a comprehensive set of experiments on various 3D scene understanding and spatial reasoning tasks, demonstrating the effectiveness of the proposed approach.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the contribution of each component of the architecture.
    *   **Competitive Performance:** The model achieves state-of-the-art or competitive performance on several benchmarks, even outperforming proprietary models.
    *   **Resource-Efficient:** Shows competitive performance with a relatively smaller (4B) model.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependency on Pre-training:** The reliance on a pre-trained 3D visual geometry encoder means the performance is intrinsically limited by the encoder's capabilities. While VGGT is a good choice, the paper could discuss the implications of using different pre-trained models or training the encoder jointly with the MLLM.
    *   **Limited Data for Finetuning:** Although the paper demonstrates the effectiveness of fine-tuning with relatively small datasets (3% of SPAR-7M), it does not fully explore the impact of scaling up the fine-tuning data. Could larger fine-tuning datasets improve performance even further?
    *   **Some Visualization Results show imperfect orientation prediction:** Although impressive, some visualization results indicate imperfections in orientation prediction, suggesting that there's still room for improvement in capturing fine-grained 3D geometric details.
    *   **Incremental Novelty:** The core idea of injecting pre-trained priors is not entirely new. The paper's originality lies more in the *specific* application to 3D from video.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in several ways:

    *   It could encourage researchers to explore alternative ways of incorporating 3D priors into MLLMs without relying on explicit 3D data.
    *   It could inspire the development of new and improved 3D visual geometry encoders specifically designed for MLLM integration.
    *   It could lead to the development of more robust and generalizable 3D scene understanding models that can be applied to a wider range of applications.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses, I would assign this paper a **Score: 7**.

**Rationale:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field by addressing the limitation of requiring explicit 3D data for MLLMs to understand 3D scenes. The proposed approach is well-designed, and the experimental results are compelling. The model achieves competitive performance on various benchmarks and demonstrates strong egocentric-allocentric transformation capabilities. However, the dependency on a pre-trained encoder, limited data for fine-tuning, and existing imperfections in orientation prediction, prevent it from achieving a higher score.
Although it's novel to perform 3D scene understanding directly from video without additional 3D data, the contribution can be seen as an improvement of existing MLLM/encoder techniques by applying them to a new application area.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### The Hallucination Dilemma: Factuality-Aware Reinforcement Learning for Large Reasoning Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24630v1)
- **Authors**: Junyi Li, Hwee Tou Ng
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have significantly advanced in reasoning tasks through reinforcement learning (RL) optimization, achieving impressive capabilities across various challenging benchmarks. However, our empirical analysis reveals a critical drawback: reasoning-oriented RL fine-tuning significantly increases the prevalence of hallucinations. We theoretically analyze the RL training dynamics, identifying high-variance gradient, entropy-induced randomness, and susceptibility to spurious local optima as key factors leading to hallucinations. To address this drawback, we propose Factuality-aware Step-wise Policy Optimization (FSPO), an innovative RL fine-tuning algorithm incorporating explicit factuality verification at each reasoning step. FSPO leverages automated verification against given evidence to dynamically adjust token-level advantage values, incentivizing factual correctness throughout the reasoning process. Experiments across mathematical reasoning and hallucination benchmarks using Qwen2.5 and Llama models demonstrate that FSPO effectively reduces hallucinations while enhancing reasoning accuracy, substantially improving both reliability and performance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "The Hallucination Dilemma: Factuality-Aware Reinforcement Learning for Large Reasoning Models" addresses the problem of increased hallucinations in large language models (LLMs) after reinforcement learning (RL) fine-tuning for reasoning tasks. The authors theoretically analyze the RL training dynamics, identifying high-variance gradients, entropy-induced randomness, and susceptibility to spurious local optima as contributing factors. To mitigate this, they propose Factuality-aware Step-wise Policy Optimization (FSPO), an RL fine-tuning algorithm incorporating explicit factuality verification at each reasoning step.  FSPO leverages automated verification against given evidence to dynamically adjust token-level advantage values. Experiments on mathematical reasoning and hallucination benchmarks using Qwen2.5 and Llama models demonstrate that FSPO reduces hallucinations and enhances reasoning accuracy.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of incorporating factuality checks at each step of reasoning during RL fine-tuning is a significant step toward addressing the increased hallucinations introduced by RL. While factuality verification is not entirely new in the context of LLMs, applying it in a *step-wise* manner during *RL fine-tuning for reasoning* is a novel contribution. The theoretical analysis of the root causes of hallucination under RL is also valuable and adds to the novelty.
*   **Significance:** Hallucinations pose a major threat to the reliability and trustworthiness of LLMs, especially in reasoning tasks where incorrect intermediate steps can lead to flawed conclusions. By demonstrably reducing hallucinations while maintaining (or even improving) reasoning accuracy, this work makes a significant contribution toward building more dependable LLMs. FSPO holds promise for improving the reliability of LLMs in domains requiring high factual accuracy.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Sound Theoretical Analysis:** The paper provides a theoretical foundation to understand why RL fine-tuning exacerbates hallucination, going beyond simply observing the phenomenon.
    *   **Well-Defined Algorithm:** FSPO is clearly explained and seems relatively straightforward to implement, which should encourage adoption.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The experimental results, particularly the ablation studies, provide compelling evidence of FSPO's effectiveness in reducing hallucinations without sacrificing reasoning performance. The model outperforming the baselines across multiple benchmarks is encouraging.
    *   **Addresses a Critical Problem:** The paper tackles a crucial issue in the development of reliable LLMs, directly addressing concerns about their trustworthiness.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependency on Automated Verifiers:** FSPO's reliance on automated factuality verifiers introduces a new potential source of error. The performance of FSPO is directly linked to the accuracy and robustness of the verifier. Imperfect verifiers could still lead to incorrect advantage adjustment and hinder learning. The paper doesn't fully explore the impact of verifier quality.
    *   **Scalability Considerations:** While the paper mentions scaling to larger models, the experimental results are limited to 7B and 8B models. It's unclear if FSPO scales effectively to larger models with significantly more parameters and reasoning steps. The computational cost of step-wise verification could become prohibitive.
    *   **Limited Domain Focus:** The experiments focus mainly on mathematical and question answering tasks. The effectiveness of FSPO in other domains (e.g., code generation, creative writing) is not explored.
*   **Potential Impact:** The work has the potential to influence the design of future RL fine-tuning methods for LLMs, promoting factuality as a primary concern.  It could also inspire research into more robust and efficient factuality verification techniques.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the novelty of the step-wise factuality verification during RL, the solid theoretical justification, strong empirical results, and importance of the problem addressed, the paper represents a significant advance. However, the reliance on automated verifiers, scalability concerns, and limited domain scope prevent it from receiving an exceptionally high score. The success is contingent on the quality of external verifiers and may not immediately generalize to all domains. Therefore, a score reflecting a high, but not groundbreaking, contribution is warranted.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Disentangling Language and Culture for Evaluating Multilingual Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24635v1)
- **Authors**: Jiahao Ying, Wei Tang, Yiran Zhao, Yixin Cao, Yu Rong, Wenxuan Zhang
- **Abstract**: This paper introduces a Dual Evaluation Framework to comprehensively assess the multilingual capabilities of LLMs. By decomposing the evaluation along the dimensions of linguistic medium and cultural context, this framework enables a nuanced analysis of LLMs' ability to process questions within both native and cross-cultural contexts cross-lingually. Extensive evaluations are conducted on a wide range of models, revealing a notable "CulturalLinguistic Synergy" phenomenon, where models exhibit better performance when questions are culturally aligned with the language. This phenomenon is further explored through interpretability probing, which shows that a higher proportion of specific neurons are activated in a language's cultural context. This activation proportion could serve as a potential indicator for evaluating multilingual performance during model training. Our findings challenge the prevailing notion that LLMs, primarily trained on English data, perform uniformly across languages and highlight the necessity of culturally and linguistically model evaluations. Our code can be found at https://yingjiahao14. github.io/Dual-Evaluation/.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a Dual Evaluation Framework for a more nuanced assessment of Multilingual Large Language Models (LLMs).  Instead of just translating benchmarks, the framework decomposes evaluation into two dimensions: linguistic medium (language used) and cultural context (the cultural knowledge being tested).  This creates four distinct scenarios: native cultural-linguistic alignment, cross-lingual understanding, and cross-cultural ability.  The authors evaluate various open-source and closed-source models using a dataset built by extending the BLEnD benchmark, and identify a "Cultural-Linguistic Synergy" phenomenon.  This phenomenon occurs when models perform better when questions are culturally aligned with the language they're asked in. The paper further investigates this synergy by analyzing neuron activation patterns, finding that the proportion of specific neurons activated is higher when questions align linguistically and culturally, suggesting this activation proportion can serve as a metric for evaluating multilingual capabilities during training. The paper further presents two hypotheses regarding these specific neurons being indicators of model performance for language specific and cultural aspects which it validates.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of disentangling language and culture for evaluating LLMs is quite novel and addresses a clear gap in existing multilingual evaluation approaches that often focus solely on translation-based benchmarks. The identification of Cultural-Linguistic Synergy is an interesting and potentially important observation. The neuron activation analysis is a valuable initial step in understanding the underlying mechanisms.

*   **Significance:** The framework could lead to more robust and realistic evaluations of LLMs, highlighting their true capabilities and limitations when deployed in diverse cultural settings. This has implications for improving model training strategies and fairness. The findings related to neuron activation, if further validated, could provide a way to gauge multilingual understanding *during* training, which would be immensely valuable.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The Dual Evaluation Framework offers a principled way to decompose multilingual understanding.
    *   The study identifies a significant phenomenon ("Cultural-Linguistic Synergy") that challenges the prevailing assumption that LLMs trained mainly on English data perform uniformly across languages.
    *   The initial interpretability analysis provides valuable insights into the role of specific neurons in cultural and linguistic understanding.
    *   The paper is well-written and clearly articulates the research question, methodology, and findings.
    *   The validation of the hypotheses for specific neurons as indicators of model performance can be of significance in the future development of such models.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The BLEnD dataset, while valuable, may have inherent biases or limitations related to its construction (e.g., representation of different cultures).
    *   The neuron activation analysis, while a good start, is limited in scope (number of models, specific layers examined).  The causal relationship between neuron activation and the Cultural-Linguistic Synergy is correlational and needs further proof through intervention.
    *   The practical implications of the neuron activation findings are not fully explored. It's not clear how this knowledge can be directly used to improve model training *beyond* broad observations.
    *   The study might be too focused on the Western English culture compared to other languages cultures, despite it showing that most of the evaluated models have performed better on scenarios rooted in English-speaking culture.
    * The study mostly validates the hypotheses on same-family languages and regions. The application of the same findings on other regions (e.g. African, Australian) may provide additional insights into the validity of the same framework.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to shift the focus of multilingual LLM evaluation from purely translation-based approaches to methods that consider cultural context. The Cultural-Linguistic Synergy phenomenon, if confirmed by other studies, could prompt a re-evaluation of current training strategies. The neuron activation findings could inspire further research into how knowledge is represented in multilingual models.

*   **Overall Assessment:** While the paper has limitations, its novelty, clear methodology, and the significant phenomenon it identifies make it a valuable contribution to the field. It opens up promising avenues for future research and has the potential to influence how multilingual LLMs are evaluated and trained.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**
A score of 7 reflects the paper's solid contribution. The core idea is novel and significant. The experiments are well designed, and the finding of Cultural-Linguistic Synergy offers an interesting research path to follow. Also, the framework and hypotheses validation provide future model development improvements. The limitations, primarily related to the depth of interpretability analysis and the need for further validation on diverse datasets, prevent a higher score. However, the paper lays a strong foundation for future work in this area.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Efficient Text Encoders for Labor Market Analysis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24640v1)
- **Authors**: Jens-Joris Decorte, Jeroen Van Hautte, Chris Develder, Thomas Demeester
- **Abstract**: Labor market analysis relies on extracting insights from job advertisements, which provide valuable yet unstructured information on job titles and corresponding skill requirements. While state-of-the-art methods for skill extraction achieve strong performance, they depend on large language models (LLMs), which are computationally expensive and slow. In this paper, we propose \textbf{ConTeXT-match}, a novel contrastive learning approach with token-level attention that is well-suited for the extreme multi-label classification task of skill classification. \textbf{ConTeXT-match} significantly improves skill extraction efficiency and performance, achieving state-of-the-art results with a lightweight bi-encoder model. To support robust evaluation, we introduce \textbf{Skill-XL}, a new benchmark with exhaustive, sentence-level skill annotations that explicitly address the redundancy in the large label space. Finally, we present \textbf{JobBERT V2}, an improved job title normalization model that leverages extracted skills to produce high-quality job title representations. Experiments demonstrate that our models are efficient, accurate, and scalable, making them ideal for large-scale, real-time labor market analysis.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a new approach called ConTeXT-match for skill extraction from job advertisements, leveraging contrastive learning with token-level attention. It improves skill extraction efficiency and performance compared to existing methods, achieving state-of-the-art results with a lightweight bi-encoder model. The paper also presents Skill-XL, a new benchmark dataset for skill extraction with exhaustive sentence-level skill annotations addressing label redundancy, and JobBERT V2, an improved job title normalization model that utilizes extracted skills.  Experiments demonstrate the efficiency, accuracy, and scalability of the proposed models for large-scale labor market analysis.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces several novel components. ConTeXT-match brings a new token-level attention mechanism to contrastive learning for skill extraction, addressing limitations of previous sentence-level approaches. Skill-XL offers a unique and comprehensive benchmark dataset that addresses the important aspect of label redundancy in skill annotation, a problem often overlooked in existing benchmarks. JobBERT V2 further refines the job title normalization task by incorporating skill extraction, which provides a richer input for the model. The integration of skill extraction into job title normalization offers a more end-to-end approach for labor market analysis.

*   **Significance:** Skill extraction and job title normalization are crucial tasks for labor market analysis. The paper's contribution lies in making these tasks more efficient and accurate, paving the way for real-time analysis of large-scale job market data. The development of the Skill-XL benchmark is particularly significant as it provides a robust and comprehensive platform for evaluating skill extraction models, encouraging further research in the field. The efficiency of ConTeXT-match compared to LLM-based methods is also significant, as it enables resource-constrained organizations to perform advanced labor market analysis. The authors also release their models and datasets.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The proposed ConTeXT-match model demonstrates improved performance compared to existing methods, especially considering its relatively lightweight architecture compared to LLMs.
    *   The Skill-XL benchmark provides a valuable resource for the community, addressing the limitations of existing benchmarks by incorporating explicit label redundancy coding.
    *   The integration of skill extraction into job title normalization is a logical step towards a more holistic labor market analysis pipeline.
    *   A thorough evaluation of the model includes ablation studies, comparisons to LLM based models, and cost analysis.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The model and evaluations primarily focus on English language data. The generalizability to other languages is not thoroughly addressed.
    *   While the paper mentions a redundancy filtering step, the evaluation of its impact on downstream applications (e.g., improved user experience) could be strengthened.
    *   The inter-annotator agreement on the Skill-XL dataset, while deemed "high," could be further improved, and potential causes for disagreement explored.
    *   The paper demonstrates the usefulness of ESCO in extracting skills and normalising job titles. However, its dependence on ESCO also poses a challenge as the usefulness of the models depends on the relevance of ESCO across different languages and cultures.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by setting a new state-of-the-art for efficient skill extraction. The Skill-XL benchmark could become a widely used resource for evaluating and comparing different models. The release of the models will enable wider adoption.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**
The paper provides substantial novelty in the task of skill extraction with a new model and a newly constructed benchmark. It also offers a useful model for job title normalization, building on existing knowledge with improvements to existing methodology. The paper’s significance is somewhat limited by its focus on English language data and dependence on the ESCO taxonomy. However, the results are clearly documented and presented with thoroughness.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Adaptable Cardiovascular Disease Risk Prediction from Heterogeneous Data using Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24655v1)
- **Authors**: Frederike Lübeck, Jonas Wildberger, Frederik Träuble, Maximilian Mordig, Sergios Gatidis, Andreas Krause, Bernhard Schölkopf
- **Abstract**: Cardiovascular disease (CVD) risk prediction models are essential for identifying high-risk individuals and guiding preventive actions. However, existing models struggle with the challenges of real-world clinical practice as they oversimplify patient profiles, rely on rigid input schemas, and are sensitive to distribution shifts. We developed AdaCVD, an adaptable CVD risk prediction framework built on large language models extensively fine-tuned on over half a million participants from the UK Biobank. In benchmark comparisons, AdaCVD surpasses established risk scores and standard machine learning approaches, achieving state-of-the-art performance. Crucially, for the first time, it addresses key clinical challenges across three dimensions: it flexibly incorporates comprehensive yet variable patient information; it seamlessly integrates both structured data and unstructured text; and it rapidly adapts to new patient populations using minimal additional data. In stratified analyses, it demonstrates robust performance across demographic, socioeconomic, and clinical subgroups, including underrepresented cohorts. AdaCVD offers a promising path toward more flexible, AI-driven clinical decision support tools suited to the realities of heterogeneous and dynamic healthcare environments.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ADACVD, an adaptable cardiovascular disease (CVD) risk prediction framework built upon large language models (LLMs). The model is fine-tuned on a massive UK Biobank dataset and addresses key clinical challenges: flexible incorporation of variable patient information (structured and unstructured), seamless integration of structured data and unstructured text (clinical notes), and rapid adaptation to new patient populations with minimal additional data. ADACVD outperforms existing risk scores and standard machine learning approaches on benchmark datasets and exhibits robust performance across various demographic, socioeconomic, and clinical subgroups. The work explores techniques to adapt the model to handle varying data availability, unstructured text, and distribution shifts. The results show the model's ability to flexibly incorporate patient information improves risk assessment consistently across demographic, socioeconomic, and clinical subgroups. Notably, the incorporation of detailed information is especially beneficial for elderly individuals, current smokers, individuals without formal higher education, and individuals with diabetes.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in several key areas:

*   **Adaptability:** The explicit focus on model adaptability across different clinical data complexities (varying data availability, unstructured data, and distribution shifts) is a significant contribution. While LLMs have been used in healthcare, this specific focus on *adaptability* to real-world clinical data challenges is relatively new. The design of specific versions (ADACVD-FLEX, ADACVD-NOTES, and ADACVD-SHIFT) for these challenges demonstrates a thoughtful approach.

*   **Integration of Structured and Unstructured Data:** The ability to integrate both structured data and free-text clinical notes into a single risk prediction framework is a considerable advance. Much prior work focuses either on structured data or primarily on text.

*   **Data-efficient Adaptation:**  The demonstration that the model can adapt to new populations and formats with minimal additional data is highly valuable, addressing a critical practical limitation of many machine learning models. The comparison against training from scratch, showing significant data efficiency gains, is compelling.

**Significance:**

*   **Performance:** Achieving state-of-the-art performance on CVD risk prediction using LLMs, matching gradient boosting methods and exceeding medical risk scores, provides a strong baseline and proof of concept.
*   **Clinical Relevance:**  Addressing the limitations of existing risk scores and models (rigid input schemas, sensitivity to distribution shifts) directly improves the potential for practical clinical application. The subgroup analyses highlight the potential for reducing disparities in risk assessment.
*   **Framework for Future Research:** The ADACVD framework provides a valuable template for developing more adaptable and robust clinical decision support tools.  It demonstrates how LLMs can be used in a practical setting.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Synthetic Clinical Notes:** The use of LLMs to generate clinical notes, while a reasonable workaround, introduces a synthetic element. A true validation using real clinical notes would strengthen the results.
*   **Limited External Validation:** While the Framingham Heart Study is used for a distribution shift analysis, more extensive external validation across diverse healthcare systems would increase confidence in the model's generalizability.
*   **Black Box:** While the paper demonstrates strong predictive performance and adaptability, there is limited discussion of the model's interpretability. Providing insights into *why* the model makes certain predictions would be beneficial for building trust and facilitating clinical adoption.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the clear novelty in adaptability, integration of data types, data-efficient adaptation strategies and the state-of-the-art performance in a critical clinical task, I would give this paper a score of 8.

*Strengths:* The paper convincingly demonstrates that this adaptability doesn't come at a sacrifice in accuracy. It offers a framework that could have a real, practical impact on patient care. The authors clearly address real-world clinical challenges and their solutions are both effective and well-documented.
*Weaknesses:* The reliance on synthetic clinical notes, the need for more rigorous external validation (beyond Framingham), and limited interpretability somewhat detract from the paper's impact. These areas represent opportunities for future research that could further enhance the value of the ADACVD framework.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 8/10

### Can LLMs and humans be friends? Uncovering factors affecting human-AI intimacy formation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24658v1)
- **Authors**: Yeseon Hong, Junhyuk Choi, Minju Kim, Bugeun Kim
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly being used in conversational roles, yet little is known about how intimacy emerges in human-LLM interactions. Although previous work emphasized the importance of self-disclosure in human-chatbot interaction, it is questionable whether gradual and reciprocal self-disclosure is also helpful in human-LLM interaction. Thus, this study examined three possible aspects contributing to intimacy formation: gradual self-disclosure, reciprocity, and naturalness. Study 1 explored the impact of mutual, gradual self-disclosure with 29 users and a vanilla LLM. Study 2 adopted self-criticism methods for more natural responses and conducted a similar experiment with 53 users. Results indicate that gradual self-disclosure significantly enhances perceived social intimacy, regardless of persona reciprocity. Moreover, participants perceived utterances generated with self-criticism as more natural compared to those of vanilla LLMs; self-criticism fostered higher intimacy in early stages. Also, we observed that excessive empathetic expressions occasionally disrupted immersion, pointing to the importance of response calibration during intimacy formation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary**

The paper "Can LLMs and Humans be Friends? Uncovering Factors Affecting Human-AI Intimacy Formation" explores the factors that contribute to intimacy in human-LLM interactions. The study focuses on three key aspects: gradual self-disclosure, reciprocity in self-disclosure, and naturalness of conversational responses. The authors conducted two experiments where participants interacted with LLMs, manipulating the level of self-disclosure, persona similarity, and the presence of a self-criticism mechanism to enhance the LLM's naturalness. The findings suggest that gradual self-disclosure significantly enhances perceived intimacy, regardless of persona reciprocity. Furthermore, LLMs with self-criticism generate more natural responses, fostering higher intimacy early on. The study also observes a potential trade-off where excessive empathetic expressions can disrupt immersion, highlighting the importance of calibrated responses. Finally, they present guidelines for designing LLM-based conversational agents to encourage intimacy.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Addressing a Gap:** The paper tackles a crucial and underexplored area of human-AI interaction: intimacy formation.  Much of the existing research focuses on high-level outcomes (trust, satisfaction), while this paper delves into specific conversational processes.
    *   **Well-Designed Experiments:** The use of a modified "36 questions" intimacy-building paradigm, along with the manipulation of self-disclosure, persona, and a self-criticism mechanism, represents a sound experimental approach. The comparison between two studies provides stronger evidence for the impact of self-criticism.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The identified design guidelines (gradual self-disclosure, conversational quality, and calibrated empathy) provide valuable insights for creating more engaging and socially intelligent LLM-based chatbots.
    *   **Qualitative Insights:**  The inclusion of post-interview qualitative data adds depth to the quantitative findings, providing a nuanced understanding of participants' experiences. The detailed examples from the conversations are particularly helpful.
    *   **Focus on Gradualism and Reciprocity:** The investigation on gradualness of self-disclosure fills a void in current research. Many similar studies adopt static, one-time disclosure designs in their investigations.
    *   **Awareness Variable:** The addition of a blindness vs. non-blindness variable in Study 2 offers valuable evidence on whether people's perceptions of the interaction are affected by the presence of an AI partner.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope:** The sample population is limited to young South Korean adults, which might impact the generalizability of the findings to other cultures or age groups. More diverse populations should be investigated.
    *   **Operationalization of Persona:** While the attempt to manipulate persona similarity is commendable, its operationalization (inverting Likert scale responses) might not perfectly capture the nuances of real-world personality differences. Perhaps more sophisticated persona-generation techniques could be explored.
    *   **Potential for Bias:** Despite the stated attempts to reduce novelty bias, the participants' awareness of the study's focus on relationship formation may have influenced their responses.
    *   **Subjectivity of Empathy Evaluation:** The assessment of "excessive" empathy relies on subjective participant feedback. More objective metrics for evaluating the quality and appropriateness of empathetic responses could be explored.
    *   **Limited Study Duration:** The study's limited duration does not fully explore how intimacy can be sustained over the long term, thus calling for longitudinal studies on the effects on these variables on longer timelines.
    *   **No Task-Oriented Interactions:** Participants are not engaged in task-oriented interactions, and this needs to be studied in future investigations.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**
    *   The paper's strength lies in the combined investigation of the various aspects that impact intimacy, whereas existing studies focus on one aspect.
    *   The investigation of gradualness, reciprocity, and naturalness of LLMs is important and novel.
    *   The study is relevant to many researchers and industry practitioners in the chatbot design, AI-human interaction, and interpersonal communications spaces.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field by providing empirical evidence and practical guidelines for fostering intimacy in human-LLM interactions. The study is well-designed, addresses a gap in the literature, and yields valuable insights. However, the limitations regarding sample diversity and operationalization of persona prevent it from achieving a higher score. It is a well-executed study within a limited scope. The limitations of the study warrant further explorations on the various considerations presented, while its current contributions are highly helpful.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 7/10

### Multiple LLM Agents Debate for Equitable Cultural Alignment
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24671v1)
- **Authors**: Dayeon Ki, Rachel Rudinger, Tianyi Zhou, Marine Carpuat
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) need to adapt their predictions to diverse cultural contexts to benefit diverse communities across the world. While previous efforts have focused on single-LLM, single-turn approaches, we propose to exploit the complementary strengths of multiple LLMs to promote cultural adaptability. We introduce a Multi-Agent Debate framework, where two LLM-based agents debate over a cultural scenario and collaboratively reach a final decision. We propose two variants: one where either LLM agents exclusively debate and another where they dynamically choose between self-reflection and debate during their turns. We evaluate these approaches on 7 open-weight LLMs (and 21 LLM combinations) using the NormAd-ETI benchmark for social etiquette norms in 75 countries. Experiments show that debate improves both overall accuracy and cultural group parity over single-LLM baselines. Notably, multi-agent debate enables relatively small LLMs (7-9B) to achieve accuracies comparable to that of a much larger model (27B parameters).
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel framework called "Multi-Agent Debate" for improving the cultural alignment of Large Language Models (LLMs).  Instead of relying on single-LLM, single-turn approaches common in the field, it proposes using two LLM-based agents that debate a cultural scenario to reach a final decision, which is adjudicated by a judge LLM.  The paper explores two variants of this framework: "Debate-Only," where agents exclusively debate, and "Self-Reflect+Debate," where agents dynamically choose between self-reflection and debate during their turns.  The framework is evaluated using the NORMAD-ETI benchmark, probing social etiquette norms across 75 countries. Results show that debate improves both overall accuracy and cultural group parity compared to single-LLM baselines.  Importantly, the study finds that smaller LLMs (7-9B parameters) can achieve comparable accuracy to a much larger model (27B parameters) when using the multi-agent debate framework.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper presents a significant departure from the standard single-LLM, single-turn approach to cultural alignment. The use of a multi-agent debate framework for this specific problem *is* novel.  While debate-based LLM systems have been explored in other contexts (reasoning, factuality), applying it to *cultural alignment* and social norms is a unique contribution.  The introduction of the Self-Reflect+Debate variant adds another layer of novelty by allowing agents to dynamically choose between strategies.

*   **Significance:** The findings have notable implications. The fact that smaller LLMs, through debate, can match the performance of larger models suggests a path toward more resource-efficient cultural alignment. The observed improvement in cultural group parity, particularly for underrepresented groups, highlights the potential of this framework to mitigate allocational harm and promote fairer LLM applications across diverse communities. It addresses an important issue in AI - bias and fairness across cultures.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-defined framework:** The Multi-Agent Debate framework and its variants are clearly articulated.
    *   **Comprehensive evaluation:**  The paper thoroughly evaluates the framework across multiple LLMs, various cultural settings, and using a standard benchmark (NORMAD-ETI). The inclusion of cultural group parity as an evaluation metric is commendable. The analysis of decision dynamics provides useful insights into how the debate process affects outcomes.
    *   **Interesting findings:** The findings regarding the performance of smaller LLMs in the debate setting and the improvements in cultural group parity are valuable and suggest promising directions for future research.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The authors provide code and datasets to help replicate results.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **NORMAD-ETI limitations:** The NORMAD-ETI dataset, while comprehensive, has limitations. It is ternary classification, thus simplified, and may not capture the nuances of social norms. The conclusions are somewhat limited by the scope of this specific benchmark.
    *   **Judge LLM Dependence:** The framework relies on a judge LLM. While the paper explores some variations, a deeper investigation into the impact of different judge LLMs, their biases, and how they influence debate outcomes would strengthen the analysis. The specific choice of GEMMA-2-27B needs more justification beyond good single-model performance - particularly about its cultural knowledge.
    *   **Debate Strategy Simplicity:** The debate strategy is relatively simple. A single round of feedback may be insufficient for complex cultural scenarios. The Self-Reflect+Debate has potential to be explored further, but the paper has not fully unlocked that potential.
    *   **Lack of Qualitative Analysis:** While the quantitative analysis is strong, the paper could benefit from a more in-depth qualitative analysis of the debates themselves. Examining specific examples of successful and unsuccessful debates could reveal insights into the reasoning strategies employed by the LLM agents and how cultural understanding is manifested (or not) during the interaction.
    *   **Limited Generalizability Claims:** The study primarily focuses on social etiquette norms. It is unclear how well the framework would generalize to other culturally sensitive tasks, such as moral reasoning or value alignment.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to influence the field by shifting the focus from single-LLM solutions to collaborative multi-agent approaches for cultural alignment. It encourages further research into debate strategies, the role of judge LLMs, and the development of more nuanced cultural benchmarks.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a novel and significant contribution by introducing a multi-agent debate framework for cultural alignment. The experimental results are compelling, demonstrating improved accuracy and cultural group parity. The use of smaller LLMs in this setting is also a valuable finding. While the paper has some limitations (dependence on the judge LLM, simple debate strategies, dataset scope), its strengths outweigh the weaknesses. This is a strong, impactful first step.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### TRIDENT: Enhancing Large Language Model Safety with Tri-Dimensional Diversified Red-Teaming Data Synthesis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24672v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaorui Wu, Xiaofeng Mao, Fei Li, Xin Zhang, Xuanhong Li, Chong Teng, Donghong Ji, Zhuang Li
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) excel in various natural language processing tasks but remain vulnerable to generating harmful content or being exploited for malicious purposes. Although safety alignment datasets have been introduced to mitigate such risks through supervised fine-tuning (SFT), these datasets often lack comprehensive risk coverage. Most existing datasets focus primarily on lexical diversity while neglecting other critical dimensions. To address this limitation, we propose a novel analysis framework to systematically measure the risk coverage of alignment datasets across three essential dimensions: Lexical Diversity, Malicious Intent, and Jailbreak Tactics. We further introduce TRIDENT, an automated pipeline that leverages persona-based, zero-shot LLM generation to produce diverse and comprehensive instructions spanning these dimensions. Each harmful instruction is paired with an ethically aligned response, resulting in two datasets: TRIDENT-Core, comprising 26,311 examples, and TRIDENT-Edge, with 18,773 examples. Fine-tuning Llama 3.1-8B on TRIDENT-Edge demonstrates substantial improvements, achieving an average 14.29% reduction in Harm Score, and a 20% decrease in Attack Success Rate compared to the best-performing baseline model fine-tuned on the WildBreak dataset.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "TRIDENT: Enhancing Large Language Model Safety with Tri-Dimensional Diversified Red-Teaming Data Synthesis" addresses the challenge of improving the safety of Large Language Models (LLMs) by creating more comprehensive and diverse red-teaming datasets. The authors argue that existing datasets primarily focus on lexical diversity but neglect other crucial dimensions like malicious intent and jailbreak tactics. They propose a novel analysis framework to measure the risk coverage of alignment datasets across these three dimensions: Lexical Diversity, Malicious Intent, and Jailbreak Tactics. They then introduce TRIDENT, an automated pipeline leveraging persona-based, zero-shot LLM generation to produce diverse and comprehensive instructions spanning these dimensions. The generated data is used to create two datasets: TRIDENT-CORE and TRIDENT-EDGE. Fine-tuning Meta-Llama-3.1-8B on TRIDENT-EDGE shows significant improvements in reducing harm scores and attack success rates compared to baseline models fine-tuned on existing datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's core novelty lies in the introduction of a three-dimensional framework (Lexical Diversity, Malicious Intent Diversity, and Jailbreak Tactic Diversity) for analyzing and enhancing red-teaming datasets.  While each dimension is not entirely new in isolation, the structured combination and application of these dimensions to guide data synthesis is innovative.  The automated TRIDENT pipeline is a practical contribution, though persona-based instruction generation is not entirely new either. The specific datasets created (TRIDENT-CORE and TRIDENT-EDGE) are also novel resources made available to the community.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its potential to improve LLM safety alignment. By creating datasets with better coverage of potential risks, the paper aims to make LLMs more robust against generating harmful content and resistant to adversarial attacks. The experimental results, demonstrating improvements in harm scores and attack success rates, support this claim. This is a crucial step in making LLMs more responsible and deployable in real-world applications. However, the focus on a single model architecture (Meta-Llama) somewhat limits the generalizability of the findings.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The three-dimensional framework provides a clear and structured approach to evaluating and improving red-teaming datasets.
    *   The TRIDENT pipeline automates the data generation process, making it scalable and efficient.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate significant improvements in LLM safety when fine-tuned on the TRIDENT-EDGE dataset.
    *   The paper provides a detailed analysis of the datasets and their characteristics, including lexical diversity and malicious intent distribution.
    *   The open sourcing of the datasets fosters further research in the community.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The dependence on GPT-4o-MINI for some evaluation metrics (harmfulness) introduces potential biases and reliance on a closed-source model.
    *   While persona-based instruction generation is useful, it has been previously explored.
    *   The experiments primarily focus on Meta-Llama-3.1-8B limiting the generalizability of results to other LLM architectures. Demonstrating improvements on other model types would strengthen the work.
    *   The reliance on LLAMA-GUARD-3-8B, another potentially flawed and closed-source tool, to filter "unsafe" instructions has the possibility of introducing bias into what is considered unsafe, and the reliance on filtering only the most unsafe instructions means there's still potentially unsafe examples being used.
    *   Although the datasets are released, the access protocols may slow down widespread adoption.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by:
    *   Promoting the use of a multi-dimensional approach to red-teaming dataset creation.
    *   Providing a valuable resource (TRIDENT-CORE and TRIDENT-EDGE) for training safer LLMs.
    *   Encouraging further research on automated data generation techniques for safety alignment.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses, I assign a score of 7.

*Reasoning:*  The paper offers a valuable contribution to LLM safety alignment by introducing a structured framework and an automated pipeline for generating diverse red-teaming data.  The experimental results demonstrate tangible improvements in model safety, and the public release of the datasets is a significant positive. However, the limitations regarding the reliance on single model architectures, GPT-4o-MINI, potentially biased filtering of data, and a lack of novelty in the methods, prevent it from achieving a higher score.  The work represents a solid advancement in the field but is not revolutionary. The potential influence through providing a new perspective on dataset creation and making the data available pushes the paper beyond a simple incremental improvement.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### A Simple Linear Patch Revives Layer-Pruned Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24680v1)
- **Authors**: Xinrui Chen, Haoli Bai, Tao Yuan, Ruikang Liu, Kang Zhao, Xianzhi Yu, Lu Hou, Tian Guan, Yonghong He, Chun Yuan
- **Abstract**: Layer pruning has become a popular technique for compressing large language models (LLMs) due to its simplicity. However, existing layer pruning methods often suffer from significant performance drops. We identify that this degradation stems from the mismatch of activation magnitudes across layers and tokens at the pruning interface. To address this, we propose LinearPatch, a simple plug-and-play technique to revive the layer-pruned LLMs. The proposed method adopts Hadamard transformation to suppress massive outliers in particular tokens, and channel-wise scaling to align the activation magnitudes. These operations can be fused into a single matrix, which functions as a patch to bridge the pruning interface with negligible inference overhead. LinearPatch retains up to 94.15% performance of the original model when pruning 5 layers of LLaMA-3-8B on the question answering benchmark, surpassing existing state-of-the-art methods by 4%. In addition, the patch matrix can be further optimized with memory efficient offline knowledge distillation. With only 5K samples, the retained performance of LinearPatch can be further boosted to 95.16% within 30 minutes on a single computing card.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary**

The paper introduces LINEARPATCH, a simple plug-and-play technique to mitigate performance degradation in layer-pruned large language models (LLMs). The authors identify a key problem: a mismatch in activation magnitudes across layers and tokens at the pruning interface. LINEARPATCH addresses this by: 1) using a Hadamard transformation to suppress activation outliers in special tokens, and 2) applying channel-wise scaling to align activation magnitudes. These operations are fused into a single matrix, minimizing inference overhead. The patch matrix can be further optimized through memory-efficient offline knowledge distillation. Experiments demonstrate LINEARPATCH's effectiveness across various LLMs and tasks, surpassing existing layer pruning methods in retaining performance.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The paper identifies a specific issue (activation mismatch at the pruning interface) that hasn't been explicitly addressed in existing layer-pruning literature. The proposed solution, LINEARPATCH, is relatively simple yet effective. The combination of Hadamard transformation and channel-wise scaling, while not entirely novel on their own, is applied in a new context to address the identified problem. The memory-efficient knowledge distillation is a nice additional feature.

*   **Significance:** Layer pruning is an important technique for compressing and deploying LLMs. A technique like LINEARPATCH that can significantly improve performance retention after pruning has clear practical value. The paper's empirical results are strong, demonstrating performance improvements over existing methods. The fact that it's plug-and-play and has minimal inference overhead further enhances its practical significance. The experiments are comprehensive, covering a good range of models and benchmarks. The ablation studies help to understand the contributions of each component of LINEARPATCH.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem identification and explanation.
    *   Simple and effective solution.
    *   Strong empirical results with comparisons to state-of-the-art methods.
    *   Comprehensive experiments across multiple models and benchmarks.
    *   Low inference overhead, making it practical for deployment.
    *   Memory-efficient knowledge distillation is a valuable addition.
    *   Well-written and easy to follow.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The individual components (Hadamard transformation, channel-wise scaling) are not entirely new. The novelty lies more in the application and combination of these techniques to solve a specific problem in LLM layer pruning.
    *   While the paper covers multiple LLMs and benchmarks, an even broader evaluation could further strengthen its generalizability claims. It could benefit from a robustness check by varying the training parameters.
    *   The experiments were conducted with pruning ratios below 30%, whether this method holds at a higher compression rate has yet to be demonstrated.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research and practice in LLM compression.  The LINEARPATCH technique could become a standard component in layer-pruning pipelines. The paper's analysis of activation mismatch could inspire further research into other factors that contribute to performance degradation during pruning.

*   **Score Rationale:**

The paper presents a well-defined problem, a simple yet effective solution, and strong empirical results. While the individual components aren't entirely new, the application and combination are novel and contribute significantly to the field of LLM compression. The clear explanation and practical benefits enhance its potential impact. However, a few limitations persist, particularly with the lack of more extensive experimentation or further improvement of compression.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Soft Reasoning: Navigating Solution Spaces in Large Language Models through Controlled Embedding Exploration
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24688v1)
- **Authors**: Qinglin Zhu, Runcong Zhao, Hanqi Yan, Yulan He, Yudong Chen, Lin Gui
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) struggle with complex reasoning due to limited diversity and inefficient search. We propose Soft Reasoning, an embedding-based search framework that optimises the embedding of the first token to guide generation. It combines (1) embedding perturbation for controlled exploration and (2) Bayesian optimisation to refine embeddings via a verifier-guided objective, balancing exploration and exploitation. This approach improves reasoning accuracy and coherence while avoiding reliance on heuristic search. Experiments demonstrate superior correctness with minimal computation, making it a scalable, model-agnostic solution.
- **Summary**: The paper introduces Soft Reasoning, a novel embedding-based search framework for improving reasoning capabilities in Large Language Models (LLMs). It tackles limitations in existing methods that rely on temperature scaling for diversity or heuristic-guided search, which can be inefficient and lead to a “wild goose chase”. Soft Reasoning combines embedding perturbation using Gaussian noise and Bayesian optimization to refine embeddings via a verifier-guided objective, balancing exploration and exploitation. Experiments on various LLMs and reasoning tasks demonstrate improved correctness and efficiency compared to traditional decoding methods and strong baselines. Ablation studies validate the importance of the verifier score and coherence term within the objective function, and analysis explores the impact of lower-dimensional space dimensionality and special token placement. The paper demonstrates that Soft Reasoning is a model-agnostic and scalable solution.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper presents a genuinely novel approach to improving LLM reasoning by manipulating the embedding space of the first token. This contrasts with existing methods that primarily focus on prompting strategies, token sampling techniques, or additional training. The core idea of perturbing the embedding and using Bayesian optimization to guide the search is sound and well-motivated.

**Strengths:**

*   **Novelty:** The embedding-based search framework combined with Bayesian optimization and a verifier-guided reward is a fresh perspective in the LLM reasoning domain.
*   **Model-Agnosticism:** The method doesn't require access to the internal model parameters, allowing for integration with various LLMs.
*   **Efficiency:** The framework minimizes computation and token usage compared to methods like RAP, making it scalable.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper provides extensive experimental results on diverse datasets and LLMs, comparing against strong baselines.
*   **Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies shed light on the contribution of individual components (verifier, coherence) of the framework.
*   **Neuron Activation Analysis:** The paper attempts to explain why the embedding perturbation is better than temperature scaling through neural activation rates.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on a Verifier:** The performance is tied to the quality of the verifier, which, although the paper tests different strategies, remains a potential bottleneck. Even using a model's generative capabilities, better verification can improve performance. The verifier itself could be an area for improvement through things like ensembling verifiers or iteratively refining the verification step.
*   **Limited Theoretical Justification for Embeddings:** While the paper demonstrates the effectiveness of manipulating embeddings, a deeper theoretical understanding of why this works so well could improve future work.
*   **Limited Exploration of the Embedding Space:** The paper optimizes the embedding of the *first* token. While a good starting point, this is still restrictive. Optimizing a sequence of token embeddings within a set window could be a future exploration.
*   **Incremental Benefit in Some Cases:** While the results show statistically significant improvements, the magnitude of the gain, especially in the few-shot setting, isn't always dramatic, particularly with strong baseline models, suggesting the gains may only be realized on certain complex reasoning tasks.
*   **Potential for Bias Amplification:** Perturbing embeddings could potentially amplify existing biases in the LLM. While the paper doesn't directly address this, it's a relevant ethical consideration.

**Significance:**

The paper has the potential to influence the field by offering a new direction in LLM reasoning research. It moves beyond traditional prompting and sampling techniques by operating directly on the internal representations of the model. This opens the door for future exploration of embedding space manipulation for various LLM tasks. The model-agnostic nature of the approach increases its practical applicability.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper is a solid contribution with a novel approach, supported by strong experimental results. The limitations mostly concern the verifier component's need for refinement, and the potential for deeper theoretical grounding to drive improvements in the design of such framework and further explorations. However, the innovation presented and the potential impact on the efficiency and scalability of LLM reasoning warrant a reasonably high score.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### BPE Stays on SCRIPT: Structured Encoding for Robust Multilingual Pretokenization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24689v1)
- **Authors**: Sander Land, Catherine Arnett
- **Abstract**: Byte Pair Encoding (BPE) tokenizers, widely used in Large Language Models, face challenges in multilingual settings, including penalization of non-Western scripts and the creation of tokens with partial UTF-8 sequences. Pretokenization, often reliant on complex regular expressions, can also introduce fragility and unexpected edge cases. We propose SCRIPT (Script Category Representation in PreTokenization), a novel encoding scheme that bypasses UTF-8 byte conversion by using initial tokens based on Unicode script and category properties. This approach enables a simple, rule-based pretokenization strategy that respects script boundaries, offering a robust alternative to pretokenization strategies based on regular expressions. We also introduce and validate a constrained BPE merging strategy that enforces character integrity, applicable to both SCRIPT-BPE and byte-based BPE. Our experiments demonstrate that SCRIPT-BPE achieves competitive compression while eliminating encoding-based penalties for non-Latin-script languages.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "BPE Stays on SCRIPT: Structured Encoding for Robust Multilingual Pretokenization":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SCRIPT-BPE, a novel tokenization scheme designed to address the challenges faced by Byte Pair Encoding (BPE) in multilingual settings. These challenges include byte premium effects that penalize non-Latin scripts and the creation of tokens containing partial UTF-8 sequences, often stemming from complex and fragile regular expressions used in pretokenization. SCRIPT-BPE bypasses UTF-8 byte conversion by using initial tokens based on Unicode script and category properties. This enables a simpler, rule-based pretokenization strategy that respects script boundaries. The authors also propose and validate a constrained BPE merging strategy that enforces character integrity, applicable to both SCRIPT-BPE and byte-based BPE. Experiments demonstrate that SCRIPT-BPE achieves competitive compression while eliminating encoding-based penalties for non-Latin script languages.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the *encoding scheme*, which uses Unicode script and category properties to create initial tokens *instead* of relying directly on bytes or unicode code points. The rule-based pretokenization built on this encoding scheme, *while simple*, is presented as a *robust* alternative to regular expressions. The constrained BPE merging is a *valuable refinement*, addressing a specific problem in existing BPE implementations.

*   **Significance:**  The paper addresses a *real and important problem* in multilingual NLP – the biases and inefficiencies introduced by common tokenization approaches.  Byte premium effects and partial UTF-8 sequences *can indeed harm* model performance and fairness across languages.  By offering a way to mitigate these issues while maintaining competitive compression, the authors *contribute to more equitable and efficient language modeling*. The elimination of tokens with partial UTF-8 sequences could potentially improve model interpretability and reduce the risk of generating invalid encodings. The constrained BPE merges strategy, applicable to *both* SCRIPT-BPE and standard byte BPE, increases the *broader applicability* of the approach.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Statement:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing BPE tokenization methods in multilingual settings.
    *   **Well-Defined Solution:** The SCRIPT-BPE encoding scheme and constrained merging strategy are well-defined and easy to understand.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of SCRIPT-BPE in eliminating byte premium effects and generating more meaningful tokens.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The code is available on Github which promotes reproducibility

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Improvement:**  While the approach is novel, the *performance gains* in terms of compression appear *relatively incremental* compared to heavily optimized byte-based BPE implementations (e.g., the 'o200k' variant).  The paper focuses heavily on eliminating *undesirable token types* and creating a more *linguistically meaningful* encoding, but the link between these qualities and *downstream model performance* needs further investigation. *Ablation studies showing how performance changes with different settings would improve the paper.*
    *   **Limited Evaluation Scope:**  The evaluation focuses primarily on compression rate on the validation set. Assessing the *impact on downstream tasks* (e.g., machine translation, language generation) is *crucial for demonstrating the true value* of SCRIPT-BPE.  The authors acknowledge this limitation, but the lack of downstream task results *limits the overall impact* of the paper.
    *   **Parameter Sensitivity:** It's possible that some hyperparameter tuning of existing methods could lessen the gap to SCRIPT-BPE.
    *   **Complexity Overhead:** Implementing SCRIPT-BPE might introduce a small overhead during tokenization compared to byte-based approaches, which is worth further investigation.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more equitable and robust multilingual language models. By addressing the inherent biases in existing tokenization schemes, SCRIPT-BPE could contribute to improving the performance and fairness of models across different languages.

*   **Justification for Score:** The paper presents a *well-motivated and carefully designed* solution to a *real problem* in multilingual NLP.  The *novelty of the encoding scheme*, coupled with the *constrained merging strategy*, represents a valuable contribution. However, the *incremental improvements* in compression and the *lack of downstream task results* prevent the paper from achieving a higher score. The significance is clear, but the demonstrable *impact is currently limited*.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Speech-to-Text Translation with Phoneme-Augmented CoT: Enhancing Cross-Lingual Transfer in Low-Resource Scenarios
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24691v1)
- **Authors**: Gerard I. Gállego, Oriol Pareras, Martí Cortada Garcia, Lucas Takanori, Javier Hernando
- **Abstract**: We propose a Speech-to-Text Translation (S2TT) approach that integrates phoneme representations into a Chain-of-Thought (CoT) framework to improve translation in low-resource and zero-resource settings. By introducing phoneme recognition as an intermediate step, we enhance cross-lingual transfer, enabling translation even for languages with no labeled speech data. Our system builds on a multilingual LLM, which we extend to process speech and phonemes. Training follows a curriculum learning strategy that progressively introduces more complex tasks. Experiments on multilingual S2TT benchmarks show that phoneme-augmented CoT improves translation quality in low-resource conditions and enables zero-resource translation, while slightly impacting high-resource performance. Despite this trade-off, our findings demonstrate that phoneme-based CoT is a promising step toward making S2TT more accessible across diverse languages.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SALAMANDRA-ST, a Speech-to-Text Translation (S2TT) system that integrates phoneme representations within a Chain-of-Thought (CoT) framework. This approach aims to improve translation performance, particularly in low-resource and zero-resource scenarios. By incorporating phoneme recognition as an intermediate step, the system enhances cross-lingual transfer, enabling translation even when labeled speech data is scarce or non-existent. The system leverages a multilingual Large Language Model (LLM) and employs a curriculum learning strategy. Experimental results demonstrate that phoneme-augmented CoT improves translation quality under limited resource conditions and facilitates zero-resource translation, with minimal impact on high-resource performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the integration of phoneme recognition into a CoT framework for S2TT, specifically targeting low-resource and zero-resource scenarios. While CoT prompting for S2TT has been explored before, the explicit use of phoneme recognition within this framework as a means to bridge the gap between speech and text across languages and to alleviate reliance on transcribed speech data is a distinctive contribution. The application of curriculum learning in conjunction with the phoneme-augmented CoT is also a positive point.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical challenge in S2TT: the scarcity of labeled speech data for many languages. The ability to perform translation in low-resource and zero-resource settings is highly significant, as it broadens the applicability of S2TT technology to a wider range of languages. The results demonstrate a clear improvement in translation quality in these challenging scenarios, suggesting that the proposed method has the potential to make S2TT more accessible across diverse languages. The analysis of how cross-lingual transfer depends on linguistic similarity and data availability is insightful.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   The methodology is well-defined and explained clearly, including the architecture, the phoneme recognition integration, and the curriculum learning strategy.
    *   The experimental setup is comprehensive, with a focus on a multilingual setting and a detailed description of the datasets used.
    *   The results are presented clearly and are accompanied by a thorough analysis. The authors investigate the impact of phoneme augmentation, data augmentation techniques (PDA), and inference strategies (DPS).
    *   The paper acknowledges the trade-off in high-resource settings and proposes methods (PDA, DPS) to mitigate it.
    *   The insights into the role of language proximity in cross-lingual transfer are valuable.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The performance degradation in high-resource settings with the basic SALAMANDRA-STCOT-PH configuration is a concern. While the authors propose PDA and DPS to address this, it indicates that simply adding phonemes isn't universally beneficial. The limitations section should perhaps explore *why* performance is reduced in high resource scenarios – is the model overfitting to phonemes, or are the phonemes introducing noise?
    *   The reliance on eSpeak for generating phoneme sequences is a limitation, as the quality of these synthetic phonemes may vary across languages and introduce errors. It would be useful to compare to other more robust forced alignment tools for this generation.
    *   The limited evaluation languages may not fully generalize this approach to other low resource languages that don't fall into the language families tested.
    *   The gains in low-resource languages while positive, are still marginal (around 1 BLEU point). Further improvements may be necessary for more practical usage.
    *   A more thorough ablation study (e.g., ablating the CoT element entirely), or comparing against existing, more mature low-resource S2TT approaches would strengthen the paper further. The comparison to just 'Direct' translation is a fairly weak baseline.
*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to influence research in low-resource S2TT and cross-lingual transfer. The phoneme-augmented CoT framework could be adopted and extended by other researchers working on S2TT for underrepresented languages. The insights into the relationship between language proximity, data availability, and transfer learning could guide future research in this area. The curriculum learning strategy and the proposed data augmentation techniques could also be valuable for other low-resource NLP tasks.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and well-executed approach to low-resource S2TT. The core idea of integrating phoneme recognition into a CoT framework is promising, and the experimental results demonstrate its effectiveness in improving translation quality in challenging scenarios. However, the performance degradation in high-resource settings, the reliance on synthetic phoneme generation, and marginal low resource gains, limit the immediate impact of the work. While the suggested improvements (PDA, DPS) mitigate some of these limitations, they also introduce further complexity. A comparison to stronger S2TT baselines would significantly strengthen the conclusions and enhance its contribution to the field. Nevertheless, the paper offers valuable insights and a promising research direction, making it a solid contribution that warrants further investigation and refinement.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Multi-Domain ABSA Conversation Dataset Generation via LLMs for Real-World Evaluation and Model Comparison
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24701v1)
- **Authors**: Tejul Pandit, Meet Raval, Dhvani Upadhyay
- **Abstract**: Aspect-Based Sentiment Analysis (ABSA) offers granular insights into opinions but often suffers from the scarcity of diverse, labeled datasets that reflect real-world conversational nuances. This paper presents an approach for generating synthetic ABSA data using Large Language Models (LLMs) to address this gap. We detail the generation process aimed at producing data with consistent topic and sentiment distributions across multiple domains using GPT-4o. The quality and utility of the generated data were evaluated by assessing the performance of three state-of-the-art LLMs (Gemini 1.5 Pro, Claude 3.5 Sonnet, and DeepSeek-R1) on topic and sentiment classification tasks. Our results demonstrate the effectiveness of the synthetic data, revealing distinct performance trade-offs among the models: DeepSeekR1 showed higher precision, Gemini 1.5 Pro and Claude 3.5 Sonnet exhibited strong recall, and Gemini 1.5 Pro offered significantly faster inference. We conclude that LLM-based synthetic data generation is a viable and flexible method for creating valuable ABSA resources, facilitating research and model evaluation without reliance on limited or inaccessible real-world labeled data.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the scarcity of diverse, labeled datasets for Aspect-Based Sentiment Analysis (ABSA) that reflect real-world conversational nuances. It proposes a method for generating synthetic ABSA data using Large Language Models (LLMs), specifically GPT-40. The authors detail the data generation process, aiming to produce data with consistent topic and sentiment distributions across multiple domains (Technology, Healthcare, Finance, and Legal). They then evaluate the generated data by assessing the performance of three state-of-the-art LLMs (Gemini 1.5 Pro, Claude 3.5 Sonnet, and DeepSeek-R1) on topic and sentiment classification tasks. The results demonstrate the effectiveness of the synthetic data, revealing distinct performance trade-offs among the models in terms of precision, recall, and inference speed.  The paper concludes that LLM-based synthetic data generation is a viable and flexible method for creating valuable ABSA resources.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**  The idea of using LLMs to generate synthetic data is not entirely new, as it has been explored in various NLP tasks.
