However, the paper's novelty lies in its focused application to the ABSA task and its attempt to address the specific challenges of real-world conversational data, which often lacks the clean structure and labeling of benchmark datasets.  The controlled generation process with consistent topic and sentiment distribution also adds to the novelty. The study also offers practical insights into the comparative strengths and weaknesses of different LLMs (Gemini 1.5 Pro, Claude 3.5 Sonnet, and DeepSeek-R1) for this specific application.

**Significance:** The significance of this paper rests on its potential to alleviate the data scarcity problem in ABSA, particularly for real-world applications. The ability to generate synthetic data with controlled characteristics allows researchers and practitioners to:

*   Develop and evaluate ABSA models that are more robust and generalizable.
*   Explore specific domains or scenarios where labeled data is limited or unavailable.
*   Experiment with different sentiment distributions or topic mixtures.
*   Reduce reliance on potentially biased or proprietary real-world datasets.

The experimental results provide valuable insights into the performance of different LLMs on ABSA tasks using the synthetic data, enabling informed model selection for different application requirements (e.g., real-time analysis vs. high accuracy).

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing ABSA datasets and the need for more diverse and representative data.
*   **Well-Defined Methodology:** The data generation process is described in detail, including the prompts used for GPT-40 and the algorithm for topic selection and sentiment assignment.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper evaluates the generated data using multiple LLMs and a range of metrics, providing a thorough assessment of its quality and utility.
*   **Practical Insights:** The paper offers practical insights into the performance trade-offs of different LLMs for ABSA tasks, aiding in model selection for real-world applications.
*  **Addresses a critical bottleneck:** High-quality labeled data generation is a major bottleneck for many NLP tasks, including ABSA. This paper proposes a scalable solution to this problem.
*  **Reproducibility:** The datasets, code and prompts are made available in Github, thus increasing reproducibility.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Synthetic Data Limitations:**  Synthetic data, by its nature, may not fully capture the complexities and nuances of real-world conversations.  The study acknowledges this limitation but doesn't fully address potential biases or artifacts introduced by the generation process. The synthetic data might lack the subtle, real-world complexities that are hard to replicate algorithmically.
*   **LLM Dependence:** The quality of the synthetic data is heavily dependent on the capabilities of the LLM used for generation (GPT-40 in this case). Advancements in LLMs could render the generated data obsolete or require frequent updates.
*   **Limited Evaluation Scope:** While the evaluation includes three LLMs, it could be expanded to include other models or different evaluation tasks (e.g., end-to-end ABSA).
*   **Lack of Comparison to Real-World Data:**  The paper would be strengthened by comparing the performance of models trained on the synthetic data with models trained on real-world ABSA datasets (if available for the target domains). This would provide a more direct measure of the effectiveness of the synthetic data.
*   **Algorithm 1 complexity**: While claimed to be O(mn) complexity, the call to getRandomSentiments might include a complexity, depending on how it is implemented.

**Overall:**

This paper presents a valuable contribution to the ABSA field by providing a practical and scalable method for generating synthetic data. While the synthetic data has limitations, it offers a promising approach for addressing the data scarcity problem and enabling more robust model development. The experimental results provide useful insights into the performance of different LLMs on ABSA tasks, and the paper's open-source datasets encourage further research in this area.  However, the reliance on LLMs and the limitations of synthetic data warrant a somewhat cautious evaluation.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** The score reflects the paper's significant potential impact and novelty in addressing a critical problem in the ABSA field, but with the acknowledgment of its limitations regarding the inherent nature of synthetic data and the dependence on LLM technology. A higher score would require a more thorough comparison with real-world data and a more comprehensive analysis of potential biases in the generated data.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Causal-aware Large Language Models: Enhancing Decision-Making Through Learning, Adapting and Acting
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24710v1)
- **Authors**: Wei Chen, Jiahao Zhang, Haipeng Zhu, Boyan Xu, Zhifeng Hao, Keli Zhang, Junjian Ye, Ruichu Cai
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have shown great potential in decision-making due to the vast amount of knowledge stored within the models. However, these pre-trained models are prone to lack reasoning abilities and are difficult to adapt to new environments, further hindering their application to complex real-world tasks. To address these challenges, inspired by the human cognitive process, we propose Causal-aware LLMs, which integrate the structural causal model (SCM) into the decision-making process to model, update, and utilize structured knowledge of the environment in a ``learning-adapting-acting" paradigm. Specifically, in the learning stage, we first utilize an LLM to extract the environment-specific causal entities and their causal relations to initialize a structured causal model of the environment. Subsequently,in the adapting stage, we update the structured causal model through external feedback about the environment, via an idea of causal intervention. Finally, in the acting stage, Causal-aware LLMs exploit structured causal knowledge for more efficient policy-making through the reinforcement learning agent. The above processes are performed iteratively to learn causal knowledge, ultimately enabling the causal-aware LLMs to achieve a more accurate understanding of the environment and make more efficient decisions. Experimental results across 22 diverse tasks within the open-world game ``Crafter" validate the effectiveness of our proposed method.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Causal-aware LLMs," a framework that integrates structural causal models (SCMs) with large language models (LLMs) to improve decision-making in dynamic environments. The framework operates in three stages: learning (extracting causal knowledge), adapting (updating the causal graph through causal interventions), and acting (using the causal knowledge to guide reinforcement learning). This iterative "learning-adapting-acting" cycle allows the LLM to better understand the environment and make more efficient decisions. The authors validate their approach on the "Crafter" open-world game, demonstrating improved performance across various tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of integrating SCMs with LLMs to enhance reasoning and adaptation is relatively novel in the context of RL and decision-making. While there's existing work on using LLMs to assist RL agents (reward shaping, policy refinement) and on exploring causal knowledge in LLMs, the explicit use of SCMs and causal intervention for iterative model refinement is a distinguishing feature. The "learning-adapting-acting" paradigm, while conceptually intuitive, is presented as a practical workflow leveraging the LLMs causal-extraction and reasoning augmented through external feedback.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from addressing a recognized limitation of current LLMs: their inability to reason structurally and adapt rapidly to new environments. By incorporating causal knowledge, the framework aims to improve the LLMs' reasoning abilities, leading to better policy learning and more efficient decision-making. Improved performance in Crafter, a complex open-world environment, suggests the potential for application in other real-world scenarios where understanding causal relationships is crucial. The ablation studies contribute to understand the importance of each stage of their proposed framework

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper clearly articulates the problem being addressed (limitations of LLMs in complex decision-making).
    *   The proposed framework is well-defined, with a clear description of each stage (learning, adapting, acting) and how they interact.
    *   The use of causal intervention as a means of updating the causal graph addresses the known issue of LLM hallucinations.
    *   The experiments in the Crafter environment provide empirical evidence supporting the effectiveness of the framework.
    *   Ablation studies show the importance of each stage in the framwork.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The description of the implementation details of the LLM prompts (particularly in the learning and adapting stages) could be more detailed. What is the specific prompting strategy used? How are the interventions realized?
    *   The performance metrics reported in Table 1 relies on literature scores for baseline models. While justified, a direct comparison using the same setup would strengthen the results.
    *   The choice of baselines might be argued to be somewhat outdated. While the chosen baselines (ReAct, Reflexion, AdaRefiner) do represent LLM-enhanced RL, the field is rapidly evolving, and comparison against more recent and powerful models could provide a stronger demonstration of superiority.
    *   The evaluation focuses solely on the Crafter environment. Evaluating the framework in different environments (e.g., robotics, simulated control) would broaden its applicability and generalizability.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in the area of LLMs and RL. It demonstrates a promising approach for integrating structured knowledge representation (SCMs) with LLMs to improve decision-making in complex environments. The concept of iterative refinement of causal models through interaction and feedback could be adopted and extended in other domains.

**Score:** 7.5

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and well-defined framework that addresses a significant limitation of current LLMs in decision-making. The integration of SCMs and causal intervention is a valuable contribution. The experimental results in the Crafter environment provide empirical support for the framework's effectiveness. However, the limitations regarding implementation details, baseline comparisons, and the breadth of evaluation prevent a higher score. Further development and evaluation in more diverse environments and with more up-to-date baselines would significantly increase the paper's impact.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### HESEIA: A community-based dataset for evaluating social biases in large language models, co-designed in real school settings in Latin America
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24712v1)
- **Authors**: Guido Ivetta, Marcos J. Gomez, Sofía Martinelli, Pietro Palombini, M. Emilia Echeveste, Nair Carolina Mazzeo, Beatriz Busaniche, Luciana Benotti
- **Abstract**: Most resources for evaluating social biases in Large Language Models are developed without co-design from the communities affected by these biases, and rarely involve participatory approaches. We introduce HESEIA, a dataset of 46,499 sentences created in a professional development course. The course involved 370 high-school teachers and 5,370 students from 189 Latin-American schools. Unlike existing benchmarks, HESEIA captures intersectional biases across multiple demographic axes and school subjects. It reflects local contexts through the lived experience and pedagogical expertise of educators. Teachers used minimal pairs to create sentences that express stereotypes relevant to their school subjects and communities. We show the dataset diversity in term of demographic axes represented and also in terms of the knowledge areas included. We demonstrate that the dataset contains more stereotypes unrecognized by current LLMs than previous datasets. HESEIA is available to support bias assessments grounded in educational communities.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces HESEIA, a novel dataset for evaluating social biases in Large Language Models (LLMs). What distinguishes HESEIA is its community-based and participatory design, involving 370 high-school teachers and 5,370 students from 189 Latin American schools. This approach contrasts with existing bias evaluation datasets that are often created without input from affected communities. HESEIA aims to capture intersectional biases across demographics and school subjects, reflecting local contexts and lived experiences of educators. The authors demonstrate that HESEIA contains stereotypes that are harder for LLMs to recognize compared to previous datasets, advocating for bias assessments grounded in educational communities. The paper details the methodology of dataset creation, a professional development course for teachers, and preliminary experiments evaluating LLM performance on HESEIA.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The core novelty lies in the *participatory co-design* of a bias evaluation dataset. Existing datasets are typically created by researchers or crowdworkers, potentially missing nuances relevant to specific communities. HESEIA flips this model by empowering educators to identify and document biases relevant to their students and local contexts. The focus on *intersectional biases* is also a positive, filling a gap in existing bias assessment resources.

**Significance:**

The significance stems from the potential to shift the paradigm of bias assessment. By grounding the evaluation in the experiences of affected communities, HESEIA can lead to more relevant and nuanced understandings of bias in LLMs. The paper also makes a methodological contribution by showcasing a professional development course and a participatory data creation process, which other researchers can adapt and apply in their own contexts. This opens the possibility of creating more diverse and inclusive AI systems. Furthermore, the real-world educational setting adds a layer of practical significance, as the created resource can directly inform educational practices and student awareness of AI biases.

**Strengths:**

*   **Participatory Approach:** The involvement of teachers and students ensures that the dataset reflects the realities and concerns of the target community.
*   **Intersectional Focus:** Capturing intersectional biases is a crucial step towards a more comprehensive understanding of AI fairness.
*   **Contextual Relevance:** By focusing on local contexts, HESEIA provides insights into biases that may not be captured by global benchmarks.
*   **Methodological Contribution:** The detailed description of the professional development course and the data creation process provides a valuable template for future research.
*   **Demonstrated Utility:** Preliminary experiments show that HESEIA reveals biases that are not easily detected by existing LLMs, highlighting its potential value.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Geographic Scope:** The dataset is limited to Latin America, which means the biases it captures may not be generalizable to other regions. While a focus on a specific region can bring richer context, the overall influence is lessened compared to a more globally diverse study.
*   **Self-Selection Bias:**  The voluntary participation of teachers might introduce a self-selection bias, where educators already interested in AI and bias are overrepresented. This can lead to a skewed perspective. This limitation, while acknowledged by the authors, impacts the generalizability.
*   **Dataset limitations:** Although the authors explain their reasons for excluding overtly offensive content, this decision narrows the focus of the dataset and may prevent the exploration of more systemic or severe AI biases.
*   **LLM Evaluation Depth:** The experiments with LLMs are relatively basic. While they show that HESEIA reveals less recognized biases, more detailed analysis using different evaluation metrics could strengthen the claims.
*   **Lack of Out-group Validation:** The dataset lacks out-group validation, making it difficult to assess whether the perceived biases differ from the experiences of other communities beyond those represented in the classrooms.

**Overall Impact:**

The paper is a valuable contribution to the field because it emphasizes community involvement in addressing social biases in AI. However, the relatively limited analysis of the data limits the paper's significance, and the lack of diversity in demographic areas limits the generalization of the results. This dataset paves the way for future studies involving cross-cultural co-designing methods for evaluating bias in AI models.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### FinMME: Benchmark Dataset for Financial Multi-Modal Reasoning Evaluation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24714v1)
- **Authors**: Junyu Luo, Zhizhuo Kou, Liming Yang, Xiao Luo, Jinsheng Huang, Zhiping Xiao, Jingshu Peng, Chengzhong Liu, Jiaming Ji, Xuanzhe Liu, Sirui Han, Ming Zhang, Yike Guo
- **Abstract**: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have experienced rapid development in recent years. However, in the financial domain, there is a notable lack of effective and specialized multimodal evaluation datasets. To advance the development of MLLMs in the finance domain, we introduce FinMME, encompassing more than 11,000 high-quality financial research samples across 18 financial domains and 6 asset classes, featuring 10 major chart types and 21 subtypes. We ensure data quality through 20 annotators and carefully designed validation mechanisms. Additionally, we develop FinScore, an evaluation system incorporating hallucination penalties and multi-dimensional capability assessment to provide an unbiased evaluation. Extensive experimental results demonstrate that even state-of-the-art models like GPT-4o exhibit unsatisfactory performance on FinMME, highlighting its challenging nature. The benchmark exhibits high robustness with prediction variations under different prompts remaining below 1%, demonstrating superior reliability compared to existing datasets. Our dataset and evaluation protocol are available at https://huggingface.co/datasets/luojunyu/FinMME and https://github.com/luo-junyu/FinMME.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces FINMME, a new benchmark dataset designed to evaluate the financial reasoning capabilities of multimodal large language models (MLLMs).  The dataset contains over 11,000 financial research samples spanning 18 domains, six asset classes, and incorporates various chart types. The authors emphasize data quality achieved through a rigorous annotation process, including a team of annotators, LLM-assisted quality control, and expert validation. They also introduce FinScore, a new evaluation metric that penalizes hallucination and accounts for domain-specific difficulty. Experimental results demonstrate that even state-of-the-art MLLMs underperform on FINMME, highlighting its challenging nature and the need for specialized benchmarks in the financial domain.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Dataset Novelty and Coverage:** The paper addresses a significant gap by providing a large-scale, high-quality financial multimodal dataset. Existing datasets are either smaller, less focused on financial reasoning, or lack the same level of rigorous annotation and validation. The broad coverage of financial domains and asset classes is a major strength.
    *   **Rigorous Annotation and Quality Control:** The multi-stage annotation process, incorporating both human and LLM annotators, along with expert review, is a significant contribution.  The low annotation error rate and the detailed description of the quality control protocol inspire confidence in the dataset's reliability.
    *   **FinScore:** The introduction of FinScore is valuable. Hallucination is a major concern in applying LLMs to critical domains like finance, and a metric that explicitly penalizes it is well-motivated. Domain-normalized scoring also helps to address inherent differences in difficulty across financial topics, contributing to a fairer evaluation.
    *   **Challenging Benchmark:** The paper convincingly demonstrates that even powerful MLLMs struggle on FINMME, showing that the benchmark effectively probes the limitations of current models in this specialized domain. The robustness testing, analyzing variations in model predictions under different prompts is also a welcome addition, demonstrating the dataset's reliability.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Evaluation Tasks:**  The paper focuses primarily on multiple-choice questions and calculations.  While these are valuable, they don't fully capture the nuances of real-world financial analysis, which often involves more open-ended reasoning, report writing, and scenario analysis.  Expanding the range of evaluation tasks could significantly enhance the benchmark's practical relevance.
    *   **Potential for Bias:** Despite the rigorous annotation process, there is still a potential for bias in the dataset. The annotators' backgrounds and perspectives, as well as the selection of research reports, could introduce biases that affect the performance of different models. More detailed demographic information about the annotators and the sources of the data would be helpful.
    *   **Comparison to Domain-Specific LLMs:** While comparing against strong MLLMs is useful, the paper lacks a comparison against LLMs specifically trained on financial data. A side-by-side comparison would show how well these MLLMs perform and demonstrate whether adding visual data improves performance.
    *   **Lack of Deeper Analysis on Model Errors:** The paper reports overall performance but provides limited qualitative analysis of the types of errors made by different models. A deeper dive into the specific challenges posed by FINMME would be valuable for guiding future research.
    *   **Scalability of the annotation processes:** The method of getting an LLM to act as an annotator requires significant manual human work. The paper does not mention how scalable such an approach would be, which is a crucial consideration if the dataset is ever to be expanded.

*   **Significance and Potential Influence:**

    FINMME has the potential to become a widely used benchmark in the financial AI community.  It provides a much-needed resource for developing and evaluating MLLMs capable of tackling complex financial reasoning tasks.  The dataset could stimulate research on improving MLLM performance in areas such as financial forecasting, risk assessment, and investment analysis. However, the degree of influence hinges on its accessibility, maintainability, and continued evolution to address the limitations noted above.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**

The paper makes a significant contribution by introducing a novel, high-quality dataset and a practical evaluation metric for financial multimodal reasoning. The rigorous annotation process and focus on hallucination are major strengths. However, the limited range of evaluation tasks, the potential for bias, and the lack of more detailed error analysis prevent it from achieving a higher score. Additionally, there are missing comparisons to existing domain-specific LLMs.  While promising, the dataset's long-term impact will depend on its continued development and adoption by the research community.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Towards Scalable Schema Mapping using Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24716v1)
- **Authors**: Christopher Buss, Mahdis Safari, Arash Termehchy, Stefan Lee, David Maier
- **Abstract**: The growing need to integrate information from a large number of diverse sources poses significant scalability challenges for data integration systems. These systems often rely on manually written schema mappings, which are complex, source-specific, and costly to maintain as sources evolve. While recent advances suggest that large language models (LLMs) can assist in automating schema matching by leveraging both structural and natural language cues, key challenges remain. In this paper, we identify three core issues with using LLMs for schema mapping: (1) inconsistent outputs due to sensitivity to input phrasing and structure, which we propose methods to address through sampling and aggregation techniques; (2) the need for more expressive mappings (e.g., GLaV), which strain the limited context windows of LLMs; and (3) the computational cost of repeated LLM calls, which we propose to mitigate through strategies like data type prefiltering.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenges of using large language models (LLMs) to automate schema mapping, a crucial task for data integration systems.  The authors identify three core issues: inconsistent LLM outputs due to prompt sensitivity, the difficulty of representing expressive Global-Local-as-View (GLaV) mappings within LLM context windows, and the computational cost of repeated LLM calls. They propose solutions for each: sampling and aggregation techniques to handle inconsistent outputs, considerations for representing complex mappings (GLaV), and data type prefiltering to reduce computational cost.  They present preliminary results showing improvements using symmetric transformations and bidirectional matching techniques with an open-source LLM, achieving competitive performance compared to proprietary models. The paper also delves into chunking strategies to manage input size and explores methods for reducing poor-quality mappings through constraint-based and model-based filtering.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** While the idea of using LLMs for schema mapping isn't entirely novel, the paper makes several valuable contributions:

    *   **Focused Problem Definition:**  The paper clearly identifies and articulates specific challenges of using LLMs in this context (inconsistent outputs, expressive mappings, computational cost).  This is a step beyond simply applying LLMs to the problem.
    *   **Specific Solutions:** The proposed solutions are concrete and actionable. The sampling and aggregation techniques for addressing inconsistent outputs, and the exploration of chunking strategies and data type filtering are valuable additions. The bidirectional schema matching and stable matching algorithm further enhances the approach.
    *   **GLaV Mapping Focus:** The explicit focus on handling GLaV mappings addresses a limitation of much of the existing research. It acknowledges the need for more complex mappings than simple one-to-one attribute alignments.
    *   **Open-Source LLM Emphasis:**  The experiments and results with an open-source LLM model (Meta Llama-3.1) are significant.  Most existing research relies on proprietary APIs, making the findings less accessible and verifiable. Demonstrating competitiveness with a smaller, open model is a key strength.

*   **Significance:**  The paper has the potential to influence the field of data integration and schema mapping in several ways:

    *   **Practical Guidance:**  The identified challenges and proposed solutions provide practical guidance for researchers and practitioners working with LLMs for schema mapping.
    *   **Focus on Scalability:** By addressing the challenges of computational cost and large input sizes, the paper moves towards more scalable solutions for real-world data integration problems.
    *   **Open-Source and Reproducibility:** The emphasis on open-source models and transparent methodologies increases the accessibility and reproducibility of the research, fostering further innovation in the field.
    *   **Real-World Datasets:** The experiments on the MIMIC dataset contribute to the robustness of the research and indicate the potential applicability to complex medical scenarios.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Preliminary Results:** The experimental results are preliminary and would benefit from more extensive evaluation on diverse datasets. Also, more details on prompt template and confidence score computation are needed to thoroughly assess the method.
    *   **Limited Evaluation of Constraint- and Model-Based Filtering:** The paper proposes constraint-based and model-based filtering, but doesn't offer any empirical evaluation of these methods.
    *   **SQL Generation is Problematic:** As the paper acknowledges, using SQL may be limiting since it represents GaV rules, therefore limiting expressiveness for GLaV mappings. A complete data integration system requires higher expressiveness.
    *   **Need for more detail in certain areas:** Specifically, there needs to be more elaboration around the symmetric transformations and the benefits conferred by these transformations to the LLM's final output. Also, more context regarding the stable matching algorithm and how it is designed would improve the understanding of the paper.

*   **Impact and Influence:**  The paper is a solid contribution that helps advance the understanding of how to build data integration systems with LLMs. The paper is most impactful for researchers seeking to improve the robustness and scalability of data integration systems using publicly available LLMs.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7**. This score reflects the paper's:

*   Clear problem definition and identification of critical challenges.
*   Well-defined and practical proposed solutions (e.g., sampling, chunking, data type prefiltering).
*   Emphasis on GLaV mappings, addressing a significant limitation in the existing literature.
*   Strong effort to work with open-source models and improve accessibility to the presented research.
*   Use of real-world datasets.

However, the paper is not without its limitations. The lack of experimental results regarding data type filtering and lack of explicit details about the symmetric transformations limit the impact. The preliminary results also need to be extended. While it demonstrates a good contribution to the field, some further work and elaboration is needed before it can have a groundbreaking influence.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.DB
- **Score**: 7/10

### PDE-Transformer: Efficient and Versatile Transformers for Physics Simulations
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24717v1)
- **Authors**: Benjamin Holzschuh, Qiang Liu, Georg Kohl, Nils Thuerey
- **Abstract**: We introduce PDE-Transformer, an improved transformer-based architecture for surrogate modeling of physics simulations on regular grids. We combine recent architectural improvements of diffusion transformers with adjustments specific for large-scale simulations to yield a more scalable and versatile general-purpose transformer architecture, which can be used as the backbone for building large-scale foundation models in physical sciences. We demonstrate that our proposed architecture outperforms state-of-the-art transformer architectures for computer vision on a large dataset of 16 different types of PDEs. We propose to embed different physical channels individually as spatio-temporal tokens, which interact via channel-wise self-attention. This helps to maintain a consistent information density of tokens when learning multiple types of PDEs simultaneously. We demonstrate that our pre-trained models achieve improved performance on several challenging downstream tasks compared to training from scratch and also beat other foundation model architectures for physics simulations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PDE-Transformer, a novel transformer-based architecture designed for surrogate modeling of physics simulations on regular grids. It improves upon existing diffusion transformer architectures by incorporating techniques tailored for large-scale simulations, including token down- and upsampling, shifted window attention, and channel-wise self-attention. The authors demonstrate the PDE-Transformer's superior performance compared to state-of-the-art transformers for computer vision on a dataset of 16 different PDEs and further highlight its generalization capabilities by fine-tuning the pre-trained model for challenging downstream tasks, achieving improved performance and outperforming other foundation model architectures for physics simulations. The paper presents both a mixed-channel and a separate-channel version, the latter showing superior pre-training and fine-tuning performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper's primary novelty lies in the architectural adaptations of diffusion transformers explicitly designed for physics simulation data. While individual components like shifted window attention and U-Net architectures are not new, their combination and modification specifically to address the multi-scale nature of PDEs, different physical channels, and the computational demands of high-resolution simulations represent a significant and unique contribution. The idea of separating the channels and then combining using axial self-attention is also a significant contribution and could possibly be applied to other areas besides just physics simulations. The comparison between the mixed-channel and separate-channel approaches provides valuable insight.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial challenge in scientific machine learning: building versatile and scalable surrogate models for physics simulations. A successful PDE-Transformer can significantly impact fields reliant on computationally expensive simulations by enabling faster design cycles, real-time analysis, and uncertainty quantification. Demonstrating that the PDE-Transformer can effectively transfer knowledge between diverse PDEs via pre-training is highly significant, opening avenues for creating general-purpose foundation models for physical sciences.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper presents a thorough evaluation, comparing the proposed architecture against various state-of-the-art transformers and neural operators across diverse PDEs and challenging downstream tasks.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies meticulously analyze the impact of different architectural choices (window size, patch size, separate vs. mixed channels), providing valuable insights for future research.
    *   **Scalability:** The focus on scalability is a key strength, as it directly addresses a major limitation of transformers when applied to high-resolution simulation data.
    *   **Generalization:** The demonstrated generalization to downstream tasks is compelling, showing the potential of pre-training for physics-informed machine learning.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:**  The current implementation is limited to 2D regular grids. Expanding the architecture to handle 3D simulations or unstructured meshes would significantly broaden its applicability.
    *   **Reliance on Supervised Learning:** Although the paper mentions the use of diffusion models, the core evaluation is based on supervised training. Exploring unsupervised or self-supervised pre-training strategies could further enhance the model's generalization capabilities.
    *   **Computational Cost:** While the paper addresses scalability, the computational demands of training and deploying PDE-Transformer (especially in larger configurations) remain significant.  Further optimization and resource requirement details would strengthen the practical impact.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of foundation models in the physical sciences. The architectural insights, particularly regarding channel-wise self-attention and multi-scale modeling, could guide the design of future models for broader applications in scientific machine learning.

**Justification for Score:**

The PDE-Transformer presents a solid contribution to the field of scientific machine learning. While it builds upon existing transformer architectures, the carefully considered adaptations for physics simulations, the comprehensive evaluation, and the demonstration of transfer learning capabilities justify a high score. The limitations related to the 2D scope and reliance on supervised learning highlight areas for future research. The ability to tackle a variety of different equations is extremely significant. The contributions are novel enough for a high score.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### Reinforcing Video Reasoning with Focused Thinking
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24718v1)
- **Authors**: Jisheng Dang, Jingze Wu, Teng Wang, Xuanhui Lin, Nannan Zhu, Hongbo Chen, Wei-Shi Zheng, Meng Wang, Tat-Seng Chua
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in reinforcement learning, particularly through Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), have significantly improved multimodal large language models for complex reasoning tasks. However, two critical limitations persist: 1) they often produce unfocused, verbose reasoning chains that obscure salient spatiotemporal cues and 2) binary rewarding fails to account for partially correct answers, resulting in high reward variance and inefficient learning. In this paper, we propose TW-GRPO, a novel framework that enhances visual reasoning with focused thinking and dense reward granularity. Specifically, we employs a token weighting mechanism that prioritizes tokens with high informational density (estimated by intra-group variance), suppressing redundant tokens like generic reasoning prefixes. Furthermore, we reformulate RL training by shifting from single-choice to multi-choice QA tasks, where soft rewards enable finer-grained gradient estimation by distinguishing partial correctness. Additionally, we propose question-answer inversion, a data augmentation strategy to generate diverse multi-choice samples from existing benchmarks. Experiments demonstrate state-of-the-art performance on several video reasoning and general understanding benchmarks. Notably, TW-GRPO achieves 50.4\% accuracy on CLEVRER (18.8\% improvement over Video-R1) and 65.8\% on MMVU. Our codes are available at \href{https://github.com/longmalongma/TW-GRPO}{https://github.com/longmalongma/TW-GRPO}.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TW-GRPO, a novel reinforcement learning framework designed to enhance video reasoning capabilities in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). TW-GRPO addresses two key limitations of existing GRPO-based approaches: the production of unfocused, verbose reasoning chains and the use of sparse, binary rewards that fail to account for partial correctness. The framework incorporates a token weighting mechanism that prioritizes tokens with high informational density, suppressing redundant tokens. It also reformulates RL training by shifting from single-choice to multi-choice QA tasks, using soft rewards to enable finer-grained gradient estimation.  A question-answer inversion data augmentation strategy is used to mitigate multi-choice data scarcity. The paper presents experimental results demonstrating state-of-the-art performance on several video reasoning and general understanding benchmarks, claiming significant improvements over existing methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a combination of techniques, some of which are individually inspired by prior work, but combined in a novel way.  The token weighting mechanism, while drawing inspiration from entropy minimization and self-certainty methods, specifically tailors this to prioritize tokens with high intra-group variance to focus reasoning chains, a novel approach.  The soft reward re-formulation for Video-QA by converting to multi-choice and implementing a question-answer inversion strategy for data augmentation is a practical and potentially impactful innovation.

*   **Significance:** The paper targets an important problem in the field of MLLMs – improving the efficiency and focus of reasoning, and introducing a more nuanced reward system. Improved video reasoning has broad applications.  The claimed performance gains on standard benchmarks are substantial, which would imply that the approach is effective. The combination of methods into a single framework is valuable, especially with the data augmentation component, which improves usability. The source code release increases replicability and further impact.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-explained methodology with theoretical justification (e.g., discussion of Assumption B.1).
    *   Strong empirical results on multiple benchmarks.
    *   Ablation studies demonstrating the contribution of individual components.
    *   Qualitative analysis providing insights into the reasoning process.
    * Data augmentation strategy addresses data scarcity.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    * The results depend on the quality of data augmentation by question answering inversion which is still relatively simple in approach and therefore may perform differently for different data.
    * The hyperparameter a appears sensitive. Although the optimal value is relatively stable, it is still dataset dependent.
    * The assumption that models generate stable outputs during training is questionable, although addressed by weighting based on trajectory divergence.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by providing a practical framework for improving video reasoning in MLLMs. The token weighting mechanism and the multi-choice QA re-formulation could be adopted by other researchers. The comprehensive evaluation and analysis contribute to a better understanding of the challenges and opportunities in this area.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper represents a solid contribution to the field. While the individual components are not entirely groundbreaking, their combination and the novel application of intra-group variance for token weighting, plus the practical data augmentation strategy, warrant recognition. The strong empirical results and thorough analysis further strengthen the paper's value. The implementation details, code availability, and complete benchmark results enhance replicability. The weaknesses are acknowledged, but the overall impact and potential influence of the paper are considerable.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### HELM: Hyperbolic Large Language Models via Mixture-of-Curvature Experts
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24722v1)
- **Authors**: Neil He, Rishabh Anand, Hiren Madhu, Ali Maatouk, Smita Krishnaswamy, Leandros Tassiulas, Menglin Yang, Rex Ying
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have shown great success in text modeling tasks across domains. However, natural language exhibits inherent semantic hierarchies and nuanced geometric structure, which current LLMs do not capture completely owing to their reliance on Euclidean operations. Recent studies have also shown that not respecting the geometry of token embeddings leads to training instabilities and degradation of generative capabilities. These findings suggest that shifting to non-Euclidean geometries can better align language models with the underlying geometry of text. We thus propose to operate fully in Hyperbolic space, known for its expansive, scale-free, and low-distortion properties. We thus introduce HELM, a family of HypErbolic Large Language Models, offering a geometric rethinking of the Transformer-based LLM that addresses the representational inflexibility, missing set of necessary operations, and poor scalability of existing hyperbolic LMs. We additionally introduce a Mixture-of-Curvature Experts model, HELM-MICE, where each expert operates in a distinct curvature space to encode more fine-grained geometric structure from text, as well as a dense model, HELM-D. For HELM-MICE, we further develop hyperbolic Multi-Head Latent Attention (HMLA) for efficient, reduced-KV-cache training and inference. For both models, we develop essential hyperbolic equivalents of rotary positional encodings and RMS normalization. We are the first to train fully hyperbolic LLMs at billion-parameter scale, and evaluate them on well-known benchmarks such as MMLU and ARC, spanning STEM problem-solving, general knowledge, and commonsense reasoning. Our results show consistent gains from our HELM architectures -- up to 4% -- over popular Euclidean architectures used in LLaMA and DeepSeek, highlighting the efficacy and enhanced reasoning afforded by hyperbolic geometry in large-scale LM pretraining.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces HELM (HypErbolic Large Language Models), a family of large language models built entirely in hyperbolic space. This contrasts with conventional LLMs that operate primarily in Euclidean space. The motivation stems from the observation that natural language has inherent hierarchical and geometric structure that is better captured by hyperbolic geometry, known for its expansive, scale-free, and low-distortion properties.  HELM addresses limitations of prior hyperbolic models by introducing novel components like Hyperbolic Rotary Positional Encodings (HOPE), Hyperbolic RMSNorm, and Hyperbolic Multi-head Latent Attention (HMLA).  It also presents HELM-MICE, a Mixture-of-Curvature Experts model where each expert operates in a distinct hyperbolic space, aiming to encode more fine-grained geometric structure.  The authors train these models at billion-parameter scales and evaluate them on benchmarks like MMLU and ARC, demonstrating consistent performance gains over Euclidean architectures like LLaMA and DeepSeek.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of building a *fully* hyperbolic LLM and training it at a large scale is a significant step forward. While individual components of hyperbolic neural networks have been explored before, the combination of these techniques into a complete, pre-trainable LLM architecture is novel. The introduction of HOPE, HMLA, and HELM-MICE all contribute to addressing specific limitations of existing hyperbolic and Euclidean models. The theoretical analysis provided for HOPE and RMSNorm strengthens the novelty. HELM-MICE offers a new approach to incorporating geometric understanding to language modeling, while HMLA provides efficient implementation for large models.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its potential to shift the paradigm of LLM architecture. The consistent performance improvements achieved by HELM models on various benchmarks (especially on tasks requiring reasoning like MMLU and ARC) suggest that hyperbolic geometry can indeed offer advantages for language understanding and generation. Demonstrating that *full* hyperbolic models can be trained effectively at scale is crucial for wider adoption. The models and operations are scalable which allows for further exploration of how to best exploit the geometry within large models.
    The HELM-MICE contribution demonstrates an approach for incorporating varying curvatures to account for more nuanced understanding of the hierarchical data. The HMLA contribution offers an efficient framework that scales to modern needs, allowing for practical usage of HELM.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Approach:** The paper tackles the problem of adapting LLMs to hyperbolic geometry in a holistic way, addressing representational inflexibility, necessary operations, and scalability concerns.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The consistent performance gains of HELM over Euclidean baselines across various benchmarks provide strong evidence for the effectiveness of the proposed approach.
    *   **Theoretical Justification:** The theoretical analysis of HOPE and RMSNorm strengthens the claims made about the proposed components.
    *   **Scalability:**  Demonstrating training at the billion-parameter scale is essential for showing the practical feasibility of hyperbolic LLMs.
    *   **Code Release:** The public availability of the code will facilitate further research and development in this area.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Computational Cost:** The paper acknowledges that HELM models are computationally more expensive to train than their Euclidean counterparts.  A deeper analysis of the source of this cost (e.g., specific operations) and potential optimization strategies would be valuable.
    *   **Limited Dataset Size Comparison:**  The comparison to Euclidean LLMs trained on the same 5B tokens is a limitation.  A more compelling comparison would involve training both HELM and Euclidean models on much larger datasets (comparable to commercial LLMs) to see if the benefits of hyperbolic geometry persist as data scales.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** While the ablation studies for HOPE and HMLA are useful, more in-depth ablation of different components of HELM-MICE (e.g., varying number of experts, different load-balancing schemes) could provide a more nuanced understanding of its workings.
    *   **Data Under-Exposure**: The models could be under-exposed to certain areas, such as mathematical reasoning.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the future of LLM research. It opens up new avenues for exploring geometric approaches to language modeling and provides a solid foundation for further development in this area. The provided models and code will stimulate more research in using geometry to align LLMs with underlying data structures.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper demonstrates considerable novelty and significance. While limitations exist, especially around the comparison with large-scale commercial LLMs and computational cost, the consistent performance gains, comprehensive approach, theoretical analysis, and code release make a strong contribution to the field. The introduction of the fully hyperbolic HELM architecture and its various components, along with empirical validation at billion-parameter scale, justifies a high score. However, the caveats mentioned above prevent it from achieving the highest marks. Therefore, the paper earns a:

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### Reflect, Retry, Reward: Self-Improving LLMs via Reinforcement Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24726v1)
- **Authors**: Shelly Bensal, Umar Jamil, Christopher Bryant, Melisa Russak, Kiran Kamble, Dmytro Mozolevskyi, Muayad Ali, Waseem AlShikh
- **Abstract**: We explore a method for improving the performance of large language models through self-reflection and reinforcement learning. By incentivizing the model to generate better self-reflections when it answers incorrectly, we demonstrate that a model's ability to solve complex, verifiable tasks can be enhanced even when generating synthetic data is infeasible and only binary feedback is available. Our framework operates in two stages: first, upon failing a given task, the model generates a self-reflective commentary analyzing its previous attempt; second, the model is given another attempt at the task with the self-reflection in context. If the subsequent attempt succeeds, the tokens generated during the self-reflection phase are rewarded. Our experimental results show substantial performance gains across a variety of model architectures, as high as 34.7% improvement at math equation writing and 18.1% improvement at function calling. Notably, smaller fine-tuned models (1.5 billion to 7 billion parameters) outperform models in the same family that are 10 times larger. Our novel paradigm is thus an exciting pathway to more useful and reliable language models that can self-improve on challenging tasks with limited external feedback.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Reflect, Retry, Reward: Self-Improving LLMs via Reinforcement Learning":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel reinforcement learning (RL) approach to improve the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) on complex tasks where only binary success/failure feedback is available. The key idea is to incentivize LLMs to generate more effective self-reflections when they fail at a task. The framework operates in two stages: first, upon task failure, the model generates a self-reflective commentary; second, the model retries the task, incorporating the self-reflection.  If the retry is successful, the tokens generated during the self-reflection phase are rewarded using Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO).  The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of this approach on function calling (APIGen dataset) and math equation solving (Countdown dataset), showing significant performance gains and even enabling smaller fine-tuned models to outperform larger base models. The method aims to be task-agnostic, improving general reasoning abilities rather than specializing in particular tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a reasonably novel method.  While self-reflection in LLMs and using RL for LLM improvement are not new individually, the combination of incentivizing *better self-reflection* specifically through RL based on *binary* feedback is the paper's key contribution.  The task-agnostic approach to improving reflection and the use of GRPO to achieve this within the constraints of binary feedback adds further novelty.  It is an original combination of existing concepts with a clear goal to improve self-correction abilities.

*   **Significance:**  The significance lies in the potential to improve LLMs on tasks where generating synthetic data is difficult or impossible and where fine-grained rewards are unavailable.  Binary feedback is often easier to obtain in many real-world scenarios (e.g., code execution, API calls). The results demonstrating significant gains and smaller models outperforming larger ones highlight the potential for resource-efficient LLM improvement. The framework's task-agnostic nature, focusing on improving reflection as a general skill, could have broader implications.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Statement:** The paper clearly addresses the problem of improving LLM performance on complex tasks with limited feedback.
    *   **Well-Defined Methodology:** The "Reflect, Retry, Reward" framework is clearly explained, along with the use of GRPO.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The experiments on two distinct and relevant tasks (function calling, equation solving) provide strong empirical support.
    *   **Interesting Results:** The findings regarding the ability of smaller, fine-tuned models to outperform larger base models are particularly compelling.
    *   **Catastrophic Forgetting Analysis:** A section dedicated to catastrophic forgetting addresses a common concern with finetuning LLMs.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dependency on Validator:**  The approach relies on a binary success/failure validator, which might be challenging to define for every task. Although the paper points this out, it is an important limitation as the quality of the validator is paramount.
    *   **Model-Specific Considerations:** The authors acknowledge that the approach doesn't work for all models. There must be a degree of starting ability to reflect and self-correct. This needs further analysis.
    *   **Limited Model Range:** While the experiments cover several model families, focusing on models between 1.5B and 8B leaves room for exploring the scalability of the approach with larger models.
    *   **Lack of comparison to other self-improving methods:** The paper introduces a novel approach but does not offer much comparative analysis to existing self-improvement methods such as ReAct or other similar methodologies.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research on improving LLMs with limited supervision. The idea of focusing on improving the quality of self-reflection as a general skill could lead to new approaches in RL for LLMs and could also inform methods for teaching humans how to learn better. It is a promising contribution to the field.

**Overall:**

The paper provides a valuable contribution to the field of LLM improvement by presenting a novel and effective method for leveraging self-reflection with limited feedback.  The task-agnostic approach and the empirical validation are strong points. The limitations regarding the validator, model dependency, and comparison with other methods detract from the overall impact, but do not make it a trivial contribution.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a novel, well-executed idea that is likely to generate further interest and development. While there are limitations, the potential for impact on resource-efficient LLM improvement is significant, justifying a relatively high score. The empirical results are compelling and the methodology is clearly defined, reinforcing the robustness of the reported findings.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### SUMO: Subspace-Aware Moment-Orthogonalization for Accelerating Memory-Efficient LLM Training
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24749v1)
- **Authors**: Yehonathan Refael, Guy Smorodinsky, Tom Tirer, Ofir Lindenbaum
- **Abstract**: Low-rank gradient-based optimization methods have significantly improved memory efficiency during the training of large language models (LLMs), enabling operations within constrained hardware without sacrificing performance. However, these methods primarily emphasize memory savings, often overlooking potential acceleration in convergence due to their reliance on standard isotropic steepest descent techniques, which can perform suboptimally in the highly anisotropic landscapes typical of deep networks, particularly LLMs. In this paper, we propose SUMO (Subspace-Aware Moment-Orthogonalization), an optimizer that employs exact singular value decomposition (SVD) for moment orthogonalization within a dynamically adapted low-dimensional subspace, enabling norm-inducing steepest descent optimization steps. By explicitly aligning optimization steps with the spectral characteristics of the loss landscape, SUMO effectively mitigates approximation errors associated with commonly used methods like Newton-Schulz orthogonalization approximation. We theoretically establish an upper bound on these approximation errors, proving their dependence on the condition numbers of moments, conditions we analytically demonstrate are encountered during LLM training. Furthermore, we both theoretically and empirically illustrate that exact orthogonalization via SVD substantially improves convergence rates while reducing overall complexity. Empirical evaluations confirm that SUMO accelerates convergence, enhances stability, improves performance, and reduces memory requirements by up to 20% compared to state-of-the-art methods.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SUMO (Subspace-Aware Moment-Orthogonalization), a new optimization algorithm designed to accelerate memory-efficient training of large language models (LLMs). SUMO leverages the low-rank structure of gradients observed during LLM training. It employs exact Singular Value Decomposition (SVD) for moment orthogonalization within a dynamically adapted low-dimensional subspace. By aligning optimization steps with the spectral characteristics of the loss landscape, SUMO mitigates approximation errors found in other methods. The authors provide theoretical analysis and empirical results demonstrating that SUMO improves convergence rates, enhances stability, boosts performance, and reduces memory requirements compared to state-of-the-art techniques like GaLore, Muon, and LoRA.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's core novelty lies in its use of exact SVD for moment orthogonalization within a low-rank subspace for LLM training. While low-rank methods and orthogonalization are not entirely new concepts in optimization, the SUMO algorithm presents a specific combination and application tailored to LLMs. A key contribution is the analysis of approximation errors associated with methods like Newton-Schulz orthogonalization and demonstrating that these errors can be significant during LLM training due to ill-conditioned moment matrices. The adaptive subspace selection combined with exact SVD is also a notable innovation, offering a balance between computational efficiency and accuracy. It moves beyond typical isotropic assumptions, incorporating spectral characteristics in a practical way. The convergence proof that explicitly accounts for the exact orthogonalization (SVD), contrasting previous work that used simplified assumptions, strengthens the theoretical foundation.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its potential to further accelerate and improve the accessibility of LLM training. Memory efficiency is a critical bottleneck, and SUMO's reduction in memory footprint makes it possible to train larger models on constrained hardware. More importantly, the claims of improved convergence rates and stability are significant, translating into faster experimentation cycles and potentially higher-quality models. The empirical results, demonstrating superior performance over existing methods on GLUE and LLaMA pre-training, support the practical benefits. While the experimental scope could be expanded to include an even wider array of models, datasets, and hyperparameter settings, the presented evidence makes a compelling case. The paper also offers valuable insights into the geometry of LLM loss landscapes, identifying limitations of approximate orthogonalization methods.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Strong Theoretical Foundation:** The paper provides a solid theoretical analysis of the proposed algorithm, including error bounds and a convergence guarantee.
    *   **Practical Efficiency:**  The use of adaptive low-rank subspace selection enables exact SVD calculations at a reasonable computational cost.
    *   **Clear Empirical Validation:** Experiments show substantial improvements over existing memory-efficient LLM training techniques.
    *   **Analysis of Existing Methods:** The analysis of Newton-Schulz orthogonalization's limitations provides context and motivation for the proposed approach.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Evaluation Scope:** The experimental evaluation could be expanded to include a wider range of LLM architectures, datasets, and tasks.
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The paper could benefit from a more detailed discussion of hyperparameter tuning and sensitivity, particularly for the subspace update frequency and rank selection. The impact of different hyperparameters requires further exploration to demonstrate SUMO's robustness.
    *   **Scalability to Extremely Large Models:** While the paper addresses memory efficiency, there is a limited discussion of the practical scalability of SUMO to extremely large models (e.g., models with trillions of parameters). The analysis of computational overhead would be valuable when scaling model size to assess feasibility.

*   **Potential Influence:** SUMO has the potential to influence the development of future LLM optimization algorithms. It could also be integrated into existing LLM training frameworks, enabling faster and more efficient training. The insights into the limitations of approximate orthogonalization methods may also spur further research into more accurate and efficient techniques.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:**

SUMO presents a valuable and novel approach to memory-efficient LLM training by combining exact SVD-based moment orthogonalization with adaptive subspace selection. The theoretical analysis and empirical results demonstrate significant improvements in convergence, stability, and memory usage compared to existing methods. While the experimental scope could be broader and more discussion of hyperparameter sensitivity and scalability is needed, the paper's strengths outweigh its weaknesses. The paper provides an innovative framework for optimizing LLM training, has the potential to influence the field, and warrants a high score. The combination of SVD and subspace adaptation provides a strong rationale, avoiding simplifications and addressing current challenges. The claim of up to 20% memory reduction warrants significant importance as well.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### LGAR: Zero-Shot LLM-Guided Neural Ranking for Abstract Screening in Systematic Literature Reviews
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24757v1)
- **Authors**: Christian Jaumann, Andreas Wiedholz, Annemarie Friedrich
- **Abstract**: The scientific literature is growing rapidly, making it hard to keep track of the state-of-the-art. Systematic literature reviews (SLRs) aim to identify and evaluate all relevant papers on a topic. After retrieving a set of candidate papers, the abstract screening phase determines initial relevance. To date, abstract screening methods using large language models (LLMs) focus on binary classification settings; existing question answering (QA) based ranking approaches suffer from error propagation. LLMs offer a unique opportunity to evaluate the SLR's inclusion and exclusion criteria, yet, existing benchmarks do not provide them exhaustively. We manually extract these criteria as well as research questions for 57 SLRs, mostly in the medical domain, enabling principled comparisons between approaches. Moreover, we propose LGAR, a zero-shot LLM Guided Abstract Ranker composed of an LLM based graded relevance scorer and a dense re-ranker. Our extensive experiments show that LGAR outperforms existing QA-based methods by 5-10 pp. in mean average precision. Our code and data is publicly available.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "LGAR: Zero-Shot LLM-Guided Neural Ranking for Abstract Screening in Systematic Literature Reviews":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LGAR (LLM-Guided Abstract Ranker), a novel zero-shot method for abstract screening in systematic literature reviews (SLRs). It aims to improve the efficiency of SLR by using large language models (LLMs) to rank papers based on their relevance to the research question and inclusion/exclusion criteria of the SLR. LGAR uses a two-stage approach: first, an LLM assigns graded relevance scores to abstracts, and second, a dense neural re-ranker refines the ranking within each score group.  The authors also contribute a manually curated dataset of inclusion/exclusion criteria and research questions from 57 SLRs to enable more realistic evaluation. Experimental results demonstrate that LGAR outperforms existing QA-based methods by a significant margin on two established datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper presents a reasonably novel approach to applying LLMs for abstract screening. While LLMs have been used for binary classification in this context, LGAR's two-stage ranking approach, which combines graded relevance scoring and dense re-ranking, is a worthwhile contribution. The creation of a dataset with fully specified inclusion/exclusion criteria and research questions directly extracted from SLR full-texts also adds value. The work improves upon previous LLM abstract screening methods by creating a better and more realistic method to handle the task.

**Significance:**

Systematic literature reviews are time-consuming and expensive. Automated abstract screening has the potential to significantly reduce the workload for researchers. LGAR appears to offer a practical improvement over existing methods, potentially reducing the time and effort required for this crucial phase.  The dataset will likely be valuable to other researchers in the field. The experiments are thorough, and the ablation studies provide insights into the effectiveness of different components of LGAR. The comparison to Akinseloyin et al. (2024), a closely related work, is especially important.

**Strengths:**

*   **Zero-Shot Approach:**  LGAR leverages the zero-shot capabilities of LLMs, reducing the need for task-specific training data.
*   **Two-Stage Ranking:** The combination of graded relevance scoring and dense re-ranking seems effective for leveraging LLMs at scale. The LLM determines the coarse ranking and the denser ranker fine-tunes the ranking.
*   **Dataset Contribution:** The manually curated dataset of inclusion/exclusion criteria and research questions will facilitate more rigorous evaluation of abstract screening methods. The manual and exhaustive extraction ensures a level of detail often missing from other datasets.
*   **Thorough Experiments:** The paper includes comprehensive experimental evaluations, including ablation studies and comparisons to multiple baselines.
*   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and the method is clearly explained.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Domain:** The evaluation is primarily focused on the medical domain. While the authors acknowledge this limitation, it raises questions about the generalizability of LGAR to other research areas.
*   **Data Contamination:** As the authors also acknowledge, the datasets might contain information already present in the LLM training data, potentially inflating the results. The authors could have explored experiments to better estimate the potential impact of data contamination.
*   **Evaluation details:** The comparison to fine-tuned models is limited. Given the emphasis on zero-shot learning, it would have been insightful to see LGAR compared to more state-of-the-art fine-tuned rankers.
*   **Lack of error analysis**: the authors do not present a detailed error analysis that explains why LGAR works better than previous methods.
*   **Computational Cost:** While the paper focuses on zero-shot capabilities, the computational cost of running LLMs for a large number of abstracts may be a barrier to adoption in some settings.  More discussion on the cost vs. benefit trade-off would be helpful.
*   **Explainability Trade-off:** As the authors note, LGAR trades off some explainability for performance compared to a QA-based approach. This could be a concern for some users who value transparency in the screening process.

**Potential Influence:**

LGAR has the potential to influence the field of SLR automation by providing a practical and effective method for abstract screening. The dataset contribution could also drive further research in this area. The work is strong enough to have an impact and contribute to the goal of making the research process more efficient.

**Rigorous Rationale:**

I am assigning a score of 7 because the paper presents a novel and effective approach to abstract screening using LLMs. The contribution of the dataset and comprehensive experimental evaluation strengthens the results. However, the limited domain, potential for data contamination, and the need for further exploration of computational costs prevent a higher score. Despite the weaknesses, the strengths outweigh the weaknesses, and this work makes a positive contribution to the field.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### A survey of using EHR as real-world evidence for discovering and validating new drug indications
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24767v1)
- **Authors**: Nabasmita Talukdar, Xiaodan Zhang, Shreya Paithankar, Hui Wang, Bin Chen
- **Abstract**: Electronic Health Records (EHRs) have been increasingly used as real-world evidence (RWE) to support the discovery and validation of new drug indications. This paper surveys current approaches to EHR-based drug repurposing, covering data sources, processing methodologies, and representation techniques. It discusses study designs and statistical frameworks for evaluating drug efficacy. Key challenges in validation are discussed, with emphasis on the role of large language models (LLMs) and target trial emulation. By synthesizing recent developments and methodological advances, this work provides a foundational resource for researchers aiming to translate real-world data into actionable drug-repurposing evidence.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper you provided:

**Summary:**

The paper is a survey reviewing the use of Electronic Health Records (EHRs) as real-world evidence (RWE) for discovering and validating new drug indications (drug repurposing). It covers various aspects of this process, including data sources, processing methodologies (NLP, ML), standardization techniques, study designs (cohort, case-control), statistical frameworks (Kaplan-Meier, Cox models, Propensity Score Matching), and the role of Large Language Models (LLMs) and target trial emulation.  It also touches upon challenges in data access, privacy, quality, integration, small sample sizes, drug-drug interactions, and inconsistent results. The authors highlight the potential of LLMs to enhance the efficiency and efficacy of drug repurposing efforts. Finally, they showcase successful examples of repurposed drugs for Alzheimer's Disease (AD), COVID-19, and cancer, identified through EHR/RWD analyses.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Scope:** The paper provides a broad overview of the RWE-driven drug repurposing landscape, covering most key aspects.
    *   **Practical Guidance:** It offers practical insights into choosing appropriate study designs, statistical methods, and data processing techniques.
    *   **Timely Focus:** The discussion of LLMs is particularly timely given the current surge in AI applications.
    *   **Identifies Challenges:**  The paper does well in acknowledging the significant hurdles in using EHR data for drug repurposing.
    *   **Well-Organized:** The structure and organization are clear, making the information accessible.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Novelty:** This is primarily a survey. While it synthesizes existing knowledge, it does not present any new research findings or significantly advance methodological understanding.
    *   **Depth of Analysis:** While the breadth is good, the depth of analysis in specific areas (e.g., LLMs, statistical methods) is somewhat limited. It primarily lists methods without critically evaluating their pros and cons in specific repurposing scenarios.
    *   **Lack of Critical Evaluation of Successes:** While it presents examples of repurposed drugs, it doesn't critically assess the strength of the evidence supporting these repurposing claims based on RWD.  It doesn't delve into the potential biases or limitations of the studies that "validated" these findings.
    *   **Overly Descriptive:**  The paper leans heavily towards description rather than critical analysis and synthesis.
    *   **Limited Discussion on Causal Inference:** Propensity score matching is mentioned, but a deeper dive into causal inference methods suitable for RWD (e.g., instrumental variables, difference-in-differences) would have strengthened the paper, especially in light of known limitations in RWD.

*   **Significance:**

    *   **Value to Beginners:** The paper could be helpful for researchers new to the field of RWE-driven drug repurposing.  It serves as a good starting point for understanding the different components of the pipeline.
    *   **Potential for Inspiration:** By highlighting successful examples, the paper might inspire researchers to explore similar strategies.

*   **Overall:**

The paper is a useful, relatively comprehensive survey of the field. However, its lack of significant novelty and critical evaluation of the evidence limits its broader impact. While it provides a good overview, it doesn't push the boundaries of knowledge or offer fundamentally new perspectives.

**Score: 5**

**Rationale:** The paper provides a decent synthesis of existing knowledge making it a fairly standard survey. The lack of novelty, limited critical analysis, and insufficient depth prevent a higher score.

- **Classification**: stat.AP
- **Score**: 5/10

### Generalization Dynamics of Linear Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24769v1)
- **Authors**: Claudia Merger, Sebastian Goldt
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models trained on finite datasets with $N$ samples from a target distribution exhibit a transition from memorisation, where the model reproduces training examples, to generalisation, where it produces novel samples that reflect the underlying data distribution. Understanding this transition is key to characterising the sample efficiency and reliability of generative models, but our theoretical understanding of this transition is incomplete. Here, we analytically study the memorisation-to-generalisation transition in a simple model using linear denoisers, which allow explicit computation of test errors, sampling distributions, and Kullback-Leibler divergences between samples and target distribution. Using these measures, we predict that this transition occurs roughly when $N \asymp d$, the dimension of the inputs. When $N$ is smaller than the dimension of the inputs $d$, so that only a fraction of relevant directions of variation are present in the training data, we demonstrate how both regularization and early stopping help to prevent overfitting. For $N > d$, we find that the sampling distributions of linear diffusion models approach their optimum (measured by the Kullback-Leibler divergence) linearly with $d/N$, independent of the specifics of the data distribution. Our work clarifies how sample complexity governs generalisation in a simple model of diffusion-based generative models and provides insight into the training dynamics of linear denoisers.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Generalization Dynamics of Linear Diffusion Models":

**Summary:**

The paper provides an analytical study of the memorization-generalization transition in diffusion models. It focuses on a simplified model employing linear denoisers, allowing for explicit computation of test errors, sampling distributions, and Kullback-Leibler divergence (DKL).  The authors demonstrate that the transition roughly occurs when the number of training samples *N* equals the input dimension *d*. For *N* < *d*, the paper shows how regularization and early stopping can prevent overfitting. For *N* > *d*, the sampling distributions of linear diffusion models approach their optimum with a DKL divergence that scales as *d/N*, independent of the data distribution details.
