Finally, the paper compares the mappings learned by linear denoisers to those by non-linear denoisers.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper offers a valuable analytical perspective on generalization in diffusion models, a topic where theoretical understanding is still developing. Analyzing linear denoisers provides a crucial simplified setting to understand the role of sample complexity and regularization. The explicit computation of test losses and DKL is a significant achievement.  The paper extends previous work on linear models to the generative context of diffusion.  The comparison of linear and nonlinear denoisers is also a useful contribution.

**Significance:** Diffusion models are becoming very important in AI. Understanding generalization is therefore paramount to sample efficiency and model reliability. The findings about regularization and early stopping are highly relevant. The analytical insights gained from the linear model may provide a valuable benchmark for understanding and improving the performance of more complex, non-linear diffusion models. However, the simplification to linear denoisers limits the direct applicability of these results to real-world diffusion models. Moreover, the paper primarily focuses on the theoretical analysis and lacks extensive experimental validation on real-world datasets with complex architectures.  Therefore, the potential impact of the paper should be carefully examined.

**Strengths:**

*   **Analytical Tractability:**  The use of linear denoisers enables a fully analytical theory, revealing clear relationships between data structure, training dynamics, and generalization.
*   **Explicit Calculations:** Derivation of test and training loss, as well as DKL, provides concrete tools for understanding the model's behavior.
*   **Insights into Regularization:** Demonstrating the effectiveness of regularization and early stopping in preventing overfitting, particularly in the *N* < *d* regime.
*   **Scaling Law:** The discovery of the *d/N* scaling for DKL in the *N* > *d* regime.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Simplification:**  The linearity assumption is a strong simplification. Real-world diffusion models use much more complex architectures.  Therefore, the results may not fully generalize.
*   **Limited Empirical Validation:** While the paper has some experiments, there are limitations on its validation on real-world datasets. This is a theoretical analysis which could be better validated.
*   **Lack of Practical Guidance:** The paper is theoretical and provides only general guidance on parameter selection and optimization of real-world diffusion models.
*   **Limited Novelty (incremental research):** As mentioned in the `Further related work` section of the original paper, the paper doesn't present "groundbreaking" ideas, but it provides a rigorous analytical approach, while several other papers work on more specific and practical aspects, such as learning dynamics, data structure or lower-dimensional manifold.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper is a solid theoretical contribution.  It provides insights into a critical area of research, but it would benefit from further empirical validation and analysis with more realistic scenarios. Because of the simplification and limitation in general applicability and concrete guidelines for real-world models, it does not constitute a breakthrough.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: stat.ML
- **Score**: 6/10

### AFLoRA: Adaptive Federated Fine-Tuning of Large Language Models with Resource-Aware Low-Rank Adaption
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24773v1)
- **Authors**: Yajie Zhou, Xiaoyi Pang, Zhibo Wang
- **Abstract**: Federated fine-tuning has emerged as a promising approach to adapt foundation models to downstream tasks using decentralized data. However, real-world deployment remains challenging due to the high computational and communication demands of fine-tuning Large Language Models (LLMs) on clients with data and system resources that are heterogeneous and constrained. In such settings, the global model's performance is often bottlenecked by the weakest clients and further degraded by the non-IID nature of local data. Although existing methods leverage parameter-efficient techniques such as Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) to reduce communication and computation overhead, they often fail to simultaneously ensure accurate aggregation of low-rank updates and maintain low system costs, thereby hindering overall performance. To address these challenges, we propose AFLoRA, an adaptive and lightweight federated fine-tuning framework for LLMs. AFLoRA decouples shared and client-specific updates to reduce overhead and improve aggregation accuracy, incorporates diagonal matrix-based rank pruning to better utilize local resources, and employs rank-aware aggregation with public data refinement to strengthen generalization under data heterogeneity. Extensive experiments demonstrate that AFLoRA outperforms state-of-the-art methods in both accuracy and efficiency, providing a practical solution for efficient LLM adaptation in heterogeneous environments in the real world.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the AFLORA paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces AFLORA, a novel federated learning framework for fine-tuning large language models (LLMs) in resource-constrained and heterogeneous environments.  AFLORA addresses the challenges of limited computational resources, non-IID data, and interference during aggregation by structurally decoupling the LoRA updates (separating the A and B matrices) and incorporating a dynamically rank-aware mechanism using a learnable diagonal matrix. It also employs server-side heterogeneous aggregation with public data refinement to further enhance generalization. Experimental results demonstrate that AFLORA outperforms state-of-the-art methods in both accuracy and efficiency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper presents several novel components.  The decoupling of the A and B matrices in LoRA for federated learning is a key contribution, as it attempts to directly address the interference problem identified in previous work. The diagonal-based adaptive rank mechanism is also innovative, enabling dynamic adjustment of the low-rank dimension based on both client resources and data complexity. The combination of rank-aware aggregation and public data refinement on the server-side further enhances the method's robustness. While individual components like LoRA and FedAvg are not new, the *integration* and adaptation of these techniques into a unified framework specifically designed for heterogeneous federated learning with LLMs exhibits novelty.

* **Significance:** Federated learning of LLMs is a critical area, especially with increasing concerns about data privacy and the need to train on decentralized data. AFLORA offers a practical solution to the resource constraints and data heterogeneity that often hinder real-world deployment of federated LLMs.  The results demonstrate improved performance compared to existing methods, suggesting that AFLORA could significantly advance the field of federated learning for LLMs. The gains in communication efficiency by freezing A locally are practically important. Furthermore, the framework is well-motivated by the identified limitations of prior LoRA-based federated learning strategies.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear problem definition:** The paper clearly identifies the challenges in federated fine-tuning of LLMs.
    * **Well-motivated approach:** AFLORA's design choices are well-justified by the analysis of existing methods and the nature of the problem.
    * **Comprehensive evaluation:** The experiments cover both general-purpose and domain-specific datasets, demonstrating the framework's effectiveness across different scenarios. Evaluation includes both accuracy and communication/computation cost, providing a holistic assessment.
    * **Strong empirical results:** AFLORA consistently outperforms baseline methods across various datasets.
    * **Addresses a significant gap:** Prior work struggles with balancing resource constraints, data heterogeneity, and aggregation accuracy - AFLORA attempts a more holistic approach.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Complexity:** While the individual components are relatively simple, the overall AFLORA framework can seem complex with multiple moving parts (decoupling, rank-aware aggregation, public data refinement). The description of the algorithm could benefit from further simplification and clarification.
    * **Hyperparameter sensitivity:**  The performance of AFLORA may be sensitive to the hyperparameters, particularly the fusion proportion α, hyperparameter y, and sparsity threshold β. The paper doesn't provide an exhaustive analysis of these parameters. How sensitive are the results to suboptimal hyperparameter selection? What are principled strategies for hyperparameter selection in practice?
    * **Scalability analysis:** While the paper demonstrates good performance with a relatively small number of clients (10), it would be beneficial to analyze the scalability of AFLORA with a much larger number of clients. Does the communication overhead increase linearly with the number of clients, or are there any bottlenecks? The impact of the small public dataset on generalization across a large client population could be further investigated.
    * **Limited ablation studies:**  While the results are strong, it would be useful to see more ablation studies to understand the specific contribution of each component of AFLORA (e.g., how much improvement comes from decoupling vs. rank awareness vs. public data refinement?).  Which of these contributes the most?

* **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of federated learning for LLMs. The decoupling strategy and dynamic rank adaptation are likely to be adopted and further developed by other researchers. The framework could enable more widespread deployment of federated LLMs in real-world applications where resources are limited and data is heterogeneous. The idea of decoupling the adaptation of general knowledge versus domain-specific information for federation is a valuable concept with potentially broader applicability.

**Score:** 7.5

**Rationale:** The paper presents a novel and well-motivated federated learning framework for LLMs that addresses several key challenges in the field.  The experimental results are strong, demonstrating the effectiveness of AFLORA compared to existing methods. However, the complexity of the framework, potential hyperparameter sensitivity, lack of a thorough scalability analysis, and limited ablation studies prevent it from achieving a higher score.  While a valuable contribution, these weaknesses require further research and refinement before AFLORA can be considered a truly groundbreaking advancement. The paper's novelty and impact are high enough to push it to 7.5, but not to a higher rating because it needs more evidence that its complexities are justified by the performance and scalability trade-offs.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Revisiting Epistemic Markers in Confidence Estimation: Can Markers Accurately Reflect Large Language Models' Uncertainty?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24778v1)
- **Authors**: Jiayu Liu, Qing Zong, Weiqi Wang, Yangqiu Song
- **Abstract**: As large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used in high-stakes domains, accurately assessing their confidence is crucial. Humans typically express confidence through epistemic markers (e.g., "fairly confident") instead of numerical values. However, it remains unclear whether LLMs consistently use these markers to reflect their intrinsic confidence due to the difficulty of quantifying uncertainty associated with various markers. To address this gap, we first define marker confidence as the observed accuracy when a model employs an epistemic marker. We evaluate its stability across multiple question-answering datasets in both in-distribution and out-of-distribution settings for open-source and proprietary LLMs. Our results show that while markers generalize well within the same distribution, their confidence is inconsistent in out-of-distribution scenarios. These findings raise significant concerns about the reliability of epistemic markers for confidence estimation, underscoring the need for improved alignment between marker based confidence and actual model uncertainty. Our code is available at https://github.com/HKUST-KnowComp/MarCon.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Revisiting Epistemic Markers in Confidence Estimation: Can Markers Accurately Reflect Large Language Models' Uncertainty?" investigates whether LLMs use epistemic markers (e.g., "fairly certain") in a stable and consistent way to express their confidence in question-answering tasks. The authors define "marker confidence" as the accuracy of LLM responses when a specific marker is used.  They evaluate this marker confidence across multiple models (open-source and proprietary) and datasets (in-distribution and out-of-distribution), introducing seven metrics to systematically assess the stability and consistency. Their findings show that while LLMs can generalize marker confidence within similar data distributions, this consistency decreases significantly in out-of-distribution scenarios. The paper concludes that the current use of epistemic markers by LLMs is unreliable for accurate confidence estimation, highlighting the need for improved alignment between marker-based confidence and actual model uncertainty.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clearly Defined Problem:**  The paper tackles an important and practical problem: the need for reliable confidence estimation in LLMs, particularly when interacting with humans using natural language.
    *   **Novel Definition of Marker Confidence:** Defining marker confidence based on observed accuracy rather than direct semantic meaning is insightful and enables quantifiable analysis.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation Framework:** The use of multiple datasets, models, and the introduction of seven new metrics provide a thorough evaluation of marker stability.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The experimental results demonstrate that the consistency of epistemic marker usage drops significantly in out-of-distribution scenarios, which is a significant finding.
    *   **Well-Written and Organized:** The paper is clearly written and well-organized, making it easy to follow the methodology and understand the results.
    *   **Reproducible Code:** The availability of code promotes reproducibility and further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Marker Set:** The paper doesn't explicitly define *how* the epistemic markers are initially identified. While filtering helps control for random noise in infrequent markers, this process could potentially introduce bias. The marker sets themselves, and any potential bias from them, are not deeply explored.
    *   **Reliance on Closed-Source QA:** The primary application is within question answering, which may limit the generalizability of the findings to other tasks or modalities. The analysis focuses on QA tasks where a "correct" answer is known, making assessment more straightforward, but less applicable to other LLM outputs.
    *   **Incomplete Solution:** The paper highlights a problem but doesn't propose a concrete solution for improving the reliability of epistemic markers.  The focus is primarily on *diagnosing* the inconsistency rather than *rectifying* it.  Future work directions are suggested, but the paper remains largely observational.
    *   **Numerical Confidence as Baseline:** Directly prompting LLMs for numerical confidence values isn't necessarily the most robust baseline, as it simply translates the challenge to *numerical* confidence assessment. The comparative advantage of *epistemic markers* over numerical ones is not investigated beyond highlighting that humans use them.
    *   **Nuance on Marker Interpretation:** The paper's definition of "marker confidence" avoids semantic interpretations but also misses out on a deeper analysis of *why* these markers are failing out of distribution. Are the LLMs overgeneralizing? Are they using the markers incorrectly? The lack of a qualitative error analysis leaves this open.
    *   **Scope of Models:** The choice of instruction-tuned models is somewhat justified but limits generalizability. The behavior of the base model is not explored further which might have given some insight on the role of instruction fine-tuning.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    The paper's novelty lies in its systematic and quantitative investigation of epistemic marker stability across different distributions, using a novel definition of marker confidence. It provides empirical evidence that challenges the assumption that LLMs use these markers reliably, raising concerns about their applicability in high-stakes scenarios. While previous work has explored human-LLM marker misalignment, this paper contributes by focusing on the *internal consistency* of LLM marker usage. The significance stems from highlighting a critical limitation in LLM confidence estimation that needs to be addressed for safer and more reliable deployment.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **7**. The paper makes a valuable contribution by identifying and rigorously quantifying a significant limitation in LLM confidence estimation – the instability of epistemic marker usage across distributions. The methodology is well-defined and the experimental results are convincing. However, the paper's impact is somewhat limited by the lack of a proposed solution and a deeper qualitative analysis of the underlying reasons for the inconsistency. While it sets the stage for future research, it's primarily a diagnostic study rather than a prescriptive one. The scope of the models considered and tasks considered also constrains the reach.

- **Score**: 7/10

### QGAN-based data augmentation for hybrid quantum-classical neural networks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24780v1)
- **Authors**: Run-Ze He, Jun-Jian Su, Su-Juan Qin, Zheng-Ping Jin, Fei Gao
- **Abstract**: Quantum neural networks converge faster and achieve higher accuracy than classical models. However, data augmentation in quantum machine learning remains underexplored. To tackle data scarcity, we integrate quantum generative adversarial networks (QGANs) with hybrid quantum-classical neural networks (HQCNNs) to develop an augmentation framework. We propose two strategies: a general approach to enhance data processing and classification across HQCNNs, and a customized strategy that dynamically generates samples tailored to the HQCNN's performance on specific data categories, improving its ability to learn from complex datasets. Simulation experiments on the MNIST dataset demonstrate that QGAN outperforms traditional data augmentation methods and classical GANs. Compared to baseline DCGAN, QGAN achieves comparable performance with half the parameters, balancing efficiency and effectiveness. This suggests that QGANs can simplify models and generate high-quality data, enhancing HQCNN accuracy and performance. These findings pave the way for applying quantum data augmentation techniques in machine learning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores data augmentation techniques for hybrid quantum-classical neural networks (HQCNNs) to address data scarcity, a major challenge in quantum machine learning. The authors propose a framework that integrates quantum generative adversarial networks (QGANs) with HQCNNs, developing two augmentation strategies: a general approach for enhancing data processing and classification across various HQCNN architectures, and a customized approach that dynamically generates samples tailored to HQCNN performance on specific data categories. The study presents simulation experiments on the MNIST dataset, comparing the effectiveness of QGANs against traditional data augmentation methods and classical GANs. The results suggest that QGANs can simplify models, generate high-quality data, and improve HQCNN accuracy and performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The integration of QGANs for data augmentation in HQCNNs is a promising direction. While the individual components (HQCNNs and QGANs) are known, their combined application for *this specific* problem is a valuable exploration. The development of two distinct data augmentation strategies (general and customized) provides a relatively novel contribution. The customized approach that targets specific weaknesses of the HQCNN is particularly noteworthy. However, it is important to consider how unique the combination is given existing literature on GAN-based data augmentation in *classical* machine learning. This existing body of work diminishes the novelty somewhat.

*   **Significance:** Data scarcity is a critical bottleneck in quantum machine learning. If QGANs can genuinely alleviate this issue and improve the performance of HQCNNs, the research has significant potential. The comparison of QGANs to classical GANs and traditional augmentation methods on the MNIST dataset provides empirical evidence. The key finding—that QGANs can achieve comparable performance with fewer parameters than DCGANs—is significant if it consistently holds true for other tasks and datasets. This finding would offer substantial advantages in quantum computing, where resources (qubits) are limited.
*   **Strengths:** The paper provides a clear problem statement and a well-defined research approach. The experimental design is sound, utilizing a standard dataset (MNIST) for evaluation and including appropriate baselines. The paper also discusses the theoretical advantages of QGANs (e.g., quantum entanglement for modeling complex data correlations). The inclusion of both a general and a customized data augmentation strategy strengthens the paper.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scalability:**  The simulations are performed on a simplified three-class MNIST problem due to computational limitations. It's uncertain whether the QGAN-HQCNN framework will scale effectively to more complex, real-world datasets and multi-class problems. This is a *major* concern. The paper doesn't address techniques for handling the increased computational cost as the number of qubits or the size of the quantum circuits grows.
    *   **Hardware Constraints:**  The experimental results are based on simulations. The practical implementation of QGANs on noisy intermediate-scale quantum (NISQ) devices faces significant challenges due to limited qubits and high error rates. The paper could benefit from a more in-depth discussion of these challenges and potential mitigation strategies.
    *   **Limited theoretical analysis:** Even with simulation results, the paper does not offer concrete theoretical justification for the observed efficiency of QGAN in this context.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by providing a practical approach to data augmentation in quantum machine learning. The findings could encourage further research into the development of QGAN-based techniques for other quantum machine learning models and applications. The customized data augmentation strategy also serves as inspiration to other researchers in this area.

*   **Score Rationale:** Given the relatively novel approach, the potential for significance, the experimental evidence on MNIST, and the clear exposition, this paper does make a valuable contribution to the research area. However, the *major* limitations related to scalability, reliance on simulations, and limited theoretical justifications considerably diminish the overall impact. Therefore, a score that reflects this is appropriate.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Draw ALL Your Imagine: A Holistic Benchmark and Agent Framework for Complex Instruction-based Image Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24787v1)
- **Authors**: Yucheng Zhou, Jiahao Yuan, Qianning Wang
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in text-to-image (T2I) generation have enabled models to produce high-quality images from textual descriptions. However, these models often struggle with complex instructions involving multiple objects, attributes, and spatial relationships. Existing benchmarks for evaluating T2I models primarily focus on general text-image alignment and fail to capture the nuanced requirements of complex, multi-faceted prompts. Given this gap, we introduce LongBench-T2I, a comprehensive benchmark specifically designed to evaluate T2I models under complex instructions. LongBench-T2I consists of 500 intricately designed prompts spanning nine diverse visual evaluation dimensions, enabling a thorough assessment of a model's ability to follow complex instructions. Beyond benchmarking, we propose an agent framework (Plan2Gen) that facilitates complex instruction-driven image generation without requiring additional model training. This framework integrates seamlessly with existing T2I models, using large language models to interpret and decompose complex prompts, thereby guiding the generation process more effectively. As existing evaluation metrics, such as CLIPScore, fail to adequately capture the nuances of complex instructions, we introduce an evaluation toolkit that automates the quality assessment of generated images using a set of multi-dimensional metrics. The data and code are released at https://github.com/yczhou001/LongBench-T2I.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've reviewed the provided document. Here's a summary and a critical evaluation, including a novelty/significance score with a justification:

**Summary**

The paper "Draw ALL Your Imagine: A Holistic Benchmark and Agent Framework for Complex Instruction-based Image Generation" introduces LongBench-T2I, a new benchmark specifically designed to evaluate text-to-image (T2I) models' ability to handle complex instructions.  LongBench-T2I comprises 500 intricately designed prompts covering nine visual evaluation dimensions.  The authors also propose Plan2Gen, an agent framework that leverages large language models (LLMs) to decompose complex prompts into manageable sub-prompts, guiding the image generation process without requiring model retraining. The paper also includes an evaluation toolkit to automate quality assessment using multi-dimensional metrics.  The authors present experimental results comparing various T2I models on LongBench-T2I and demonstrate the effectiveness of Plan2Gen.

**Critical Evaluation**

**Strengths:**

*   **Focus on Complexity:** The paper addresses a significant gap in existing T2I benchmarks, which often fail to adequately evaluate models' performance on complex, multi-faceted instructions. This is crucial because real-world applications often require models to interpret and execute nuanced instructions.
*   **Comprehensive Benchmark Design:** LongBench-T2I's design, with its nine visual evaluation dimensions, provides a more holistic assessment of T2I models than previous benchmarks that focus on only a few aspects of compositionality.
*   **Agent Framework (Plan2Gen):** The Plan2Gen framework is a valuable contribution. By leveraging LLMs to decompose complex prompts, it offers a practical approach to improve T2I generation without requiring extensive retraining of T2I models. This modularity is beneficial.
*   **Evaluation Toolkit:** The automated evaluation toolkit is a practical contribution that can facilitate future research in this area.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper includes a broad range of T2I models in its evaluation, including both closed-source and open-source options, and both diffusion and autoregressive architectures. This helps provide a solid understanding of the landscape.
*   **Release of Data/Code:** Releasing the dataset and code significantly enhances the reproducibility and impact of the work, allowing other researchers to build upon their contributions.
*   **Consistency Across Evaluators**: The paper notes high consistency in results across Gemini-2.0 and InternVL3-78B evaluators which provides greater confidence in the reproducibility of results.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on LLMs for Evaluation:** While the automated evaluation toolkit is helpful, it depends heavily on LLMs, which can have their own biases and limitations.  The quality of the evaluation is inherently tied to the performance of the LLM evaluator.
*   **Novelty of Plan2Gen is incremental:** Scene decomposition and progressive generation have been explored by earlier work, and this paper combines them with LLM.
*   **Limited Exploration of Refinement Strategies:** While the paper demonstrates the benefits of refinement iterations in Plan2Gen, it does not explore a wide range of refinement strategies. Other refinement techniques might be more effective. The results are shown to degrade slightly after 3 refinement steps; this effect should be examined more deeply.
*   **Human Evaluation Limited:** While the inclusion of a human evaluation is positive, the scale is small. More extensive human evaluation is required to confidently validate automated metrics.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The novelty lies in the specific combination of a new, complex benchmark dataset with a practical agent framework and an evaluation toolkit. While the individual components (LLM-based prompt decomposition, progressive generation) are not entirely new, their integration within LongBench-T2I and Plan2Gen is a novel contribution. The significance stems from the potential to drive research toward T2I models that can truly understand and execute complex, real-world instructions. It also provides a standardized way to evaluate such models.

**Score:**

**7/10**

**Justification:**

A score of 7 reflects the following: The paper provides a valuable contribution by addressing a relevant problem in the T2I field: evaluating and improving performance on complex instructions. The benchmark, agent framework, and evaluation toolkit represent a significant step forward in this area. However, reliance on LLMs for evaluation and incremental improvements over earlier techniques (especially in Plan2Gen) keep the score from climbing further. The release of data and code are strong positives. The analysis could have explored more types of refinement strategies and included a more extensive human evaluation, however. The paper pushes the boundary of long complex instructions in text-to-image generation while providing a systematic benchmark and evaluation strategy.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 0/10

### Guiding Generative Storytelling with Knowledge Graphs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24803v2)
- **Authors**: Zhijun Pan, Antonios Andronis, Eva Hayek, Oscar AP Wilkinson, Ilya Lasy, Annette Parry, Guy Gadney, Tim J. Smith, Mick Grierson
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown great potential in automated story generation, but challenges remain in maintaining long-form coherence and providing users with intuitive and effective control. Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) has proven effective in reducing hallucinations in text generation; however, the use of structured data to support generative storytelling remains underexplored. This paper investigates how knowledge graphs (KGs) can enhance LLM-based storytelling by improving narrative quality and enabling user-driven modifications. We propose a KG-assisted storytelling pipeline and evaluate its effectiveness through a user study with 15 participants. Participants created their own story prompts, generated stories, and edited knowledge graphs to shape their narratives. Through quantitative and qualitative analysis, our findings demonstrate that knowledge graphs significantly enhance story quality in action-oriented and structured narratives within our system settings. Additionally, editing the knowledge graph increases users' sense of control, making storytelling more engaging, interactive, and playful.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the use of knowledge graphs (KGs) to enhance long-form story generation with Large Language Models (LLMs). It addresses two key challenges: maintaining narrative coherence and enabling intuitive user control. The authors propose a KG-assisted storytelling pipeline where the KG acts as a structured repository of narrative elements, dynamically ensuring consistency and factual accuracy. Users can edit the KG to influence the story's content and progression. A user study with 15 participants demonstrates that KGs improve story quality in action-oriented narratives and that editing the KG increases user agency and engagement. The study also reveals that KGs may be less effective for introspective narratives.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The idea of using KGs for story generation is not entirely new, as the paper itself acknowledges prior work. However, the paper's novelty lies in several aspects:
    *   **Focus on Long-Form Narratives:**  Many existing studies concentrate on short text outputs, whereas this paper specifically addresses long-form storytelling, where challenges of coherence and consistency are magnified.
    *   **User-Driven KG Editing:**  The paper emphasizes user interaction through editable KGs, allowing for direct manipulation of story elements. This is a relatively underexplored area compared to purely automated approaches or prompt engineering.
    *   **Empirical Evaluation:**  The paper conducts a user study to assess the impact of KGs on both story quality and user experience. This is a significant strength compared to purely computational evaluations.
    *   **Genre-Specific Findings:** The identification of genre-dependent KG effectiveness (better for action-oriented narratives, less so for introspective ones) is a valuable contribution.

*   **Significance:**
    *   **Addresses Practical Challenges:** The paper tackles real-world challenges in LLM-based storytelling, such as hallucinations and lack of user control.
    *   **Provides a Concrete Pipeline:**  The proposed KG-assisted pipeline provides a practical framework for researchers and developers to build upon.
    *   **Offers Empirical Evidence:** The user study provides empirical support for the effectiveness of KGs in specific contexts, informing future research directions.
    *   **Highlights Limitations:** Acknowledging the limitations of KGs in introspective narratives is crucial, as it directs research towards alternative or complementary approaches.

* **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Small Sample Size:** The user study has a relatively small sample size (15 participants), which limits the generalizability of the findings.
    *   **Model Limitations:**  The use of a smaller 8B LLama 3.1 model affects story quality and constrains conclusions. The study's insights into KG performance would be strengthened with the use of a stronger LLM.
    *   **Simplified Prompts:**  The deliberate use of minimal prompts, while designed to isolate the KG effect, might have limited the overall potential of the system.
    *   **Limited UI Exploration:**  The focus was on the effect of KG editing, not the usability of a KG editing interface.

*   **Potential Influence:**
    *   The paper's findings can inform the design of future storytelling tools that integrate KGs, tailoring their use to specific genres and user preferences.
    *   The proposed pipeline can serve as a starting point for developing more sophisticated KG-assisted storytelling systems.
    *   The paper's emphasis on user control and agency can inspire research into more intuitive and engaging AI-assisted creative tools.

**Justification of Score:**

While the paper doesn't revolutionize the field, it makes several solid contributions. The combination of KG integration, user-driven editing, empirical evaluation, and genre-specific insights warrants a favorable assessment. However, the limitations of the sample size and model capabilities prevent a higher score. Given the solid foundation, practical framework, and nuanced findings, I assign it a score of 7.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### RealDrive: Retrieval-Augmented Driving with Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24808v1)
- **Authors**: Wenhao Ding, Sushant Veer, Yuxiao Chen, Yulong Cao, Chaowei Xiao, Marco Pavone
- **Abstract**: Learning-based planners generate natural human-like driving behaviors by learning to reason about nuanced interactions from data, overcoming the rigid behaviors that arise from rule-based planners. Nonetheless, data-driven approaches often struggle with rare, safety-critical scenarios and offer limited controllability over the generated trajectories. To address these challenges, we propose RealDrive, a Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) framework that initializes a diffusion-based planning policy by retrieving the most relevant expert demonstrations from the training dataset. By interpolating between current observations and retrieved examples through a denoising process, our approach enables fine-grained control and safe behavior across diverse scenarios, leveraging the strong prior provided by the retrieved scenario. Another key insight we produce is that a task-relevant retrieval model trained with planning-based objectives results in superior planning performance in our framework compared to a task-agnostic retriever. Experimental results demonstrate improved generalization to long-tail events and enhanced trajectory diversity compared to standard learning-based planners -- we observe a 40% reduction in collision rate on the Waymo Open Motion dataset with RAG.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces RealDrive, a Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) framework for improving learning-based autonomous driving planners. RealDrive enhances diffusion-based planning by retrieving similar expert driving demonstrations from a training dataset and using these retrievals to initialize and guide the planning process. The key ideas include a task-specific embedding model for effective retrieval of relevant scenarios, a Retrieval Interpolation Module (RIM) to seamlessly integrate retrieved knowledge while adapting to the current context, and the use of diffusion models to maintain fine-grained control and safety. The paper demonstrates that RealDrive improves generalization to long-tail events, enhances trajectory diversity, and reduces collision rates compared to standard learning-based planners.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using RAG to enhance learning-based driving planners is not entirely new, as the paper itself acknowledges existing works like RAG-Driver and similar approaches that leverage LLMs for driving scenarios. The core novelty lies in the specific combination of techniques: (1) using a **task-specific embedding model** (trained with planning objectives) for retrieval (2) using **diffusion models** rather than LLMs to incorporate retrieved examples at a lower level and in the denoising process (3) a **Retrieval Interpolation Module (RIM)**, which integrates observations and actions from both current and retrieved trajectories, addressing a limitation in prior work. The RIM and the task-specific embedding are potentially significant improvements, but the incremental improvement over existing RAG frameworks needs to be further demonstrated in the experiments (see below).

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work lies in its potential to address two key limitations of learning-based driving planners: poor generalization to rare, safety-critical scenarios and limited controllability. By incorporating expert demonstrations from similar situations, RealDrive shows promise in improving safety and allowing for more nuanced control over planned trajectories. The reported 40% reduction in collision rate on the Waymo Open Motion dataset is a compelling result. However, the long-term impact will depend on whether the demonstrated improvements generalize to diverse and complex real-world driving scenarios and whether the system can be scaled for practical deployment.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The proposed RIM module is a strong contribution, as it goes beyond simply using retrieved actions and incorporates the observations as well, allowing for a more context-aware planning.
    *   The use of diffusion models allows for a more continuous and controlled integration of retrieved knowledge during the denoising process.
    *   The paper provides a comprehensive experimental evaluation on standard driving datasets, comparing RealDrive to several baselines and analyzing the influence of various design choices.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper could benefit from a more thorough comparison with prior RAG-based driving approaches. While the introduction mentions limitations of using LLMs, a more direct comparison (in terms of performance and computational cost) would strengthen the claims of superiority.
    *   The "oracle" experiments, while demonstrating a potential upper bound, might be somewhat unrealistic. A more practical evaluation of retrieval accuracy and its influence on planning performance would be valuable.
    *   The computational cost associated with the retrieval process should be clearly quantified and compared against alternative approaches. The paper acknowledges increased latency but could provide more detailed profiling.
    *   Figure 4 is difficult to interpret without further explanation in the text. What is collision rate and minADE plotted against? Also, the difference between lines is visually very small and hard to see.

*   **Potential Influence:** The work has the potential to influence future research in learning-based autonomous driving by demonstrating the benefits of retrieval-augmented planning and highlighting the importance of task-specific embedding models and context-aware knowledge integration. The RIM module and the use of diffusion models provide valuable insights for improving the safety and controllability of driving planners.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the incremental nature of the novelty (building upon existing RAG approaches), some weaknesses in the experiments, and the lack of direct comparative analysis with existing RAG frameworks, a score of 6 is appropriate. The RIM is a novel concept and does improve results. The idea of task-specific training is also solid, and could be further elaborated. The 40% collision rate reduction is an exciting finding, but the paper is still short on details that would demonstrate the framework's effectiveness in more diverse real-world driving settings. More details on the failure modes of the models, and explanations of why RAG helps in those cases, would strengthen the impact of the paper.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 6/10

### PhySense: Principle-Based Physics Reasoning Benchmarking for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24823v1)
- **Authors**: Yinggan Xu, Yue Liu, Zhiqiang Gao, Changnan Peng, Di Luo
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have rapidly advanced and are increasingly capable of tackling complex scientific problems, including those in physics. Despite this progress, current LLMs often fail to emulate the concise, principle-based reasoning characteristic of human experts, instead generating lengthy and opaque solutions. This discrepancy highlights a crucial gap in their ability to apply core physical principles for efficient and interpretable problem solving. To systematically investigate this limitation, we introduce PhySense, a novel principle-based physics reasoning benchmark designed to be easily solvable by experts using guiding principles, yet deceptively difficult for LLMs without principle-first reasoning. Our evaluation across multiple state-of-the-art LLMs and prompt types reveals a consistent failure to align with expert-like reasoning paths, providing insights for developing AI systems with efficient, robust and interpretable principle-based scientific reasoning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the PhySense paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces *PhySense*, a new benchmark designed to assess the principle-based physics reasoning abilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). *PhySense* contains 380 problems spanning various areas of physics (electricity & magnetism, quantum mechanics, etc.) and crafted to be easily solvable by human experts using core physics principles like symmetry, dimensional analysis, and conservation laws. The key premise is that while current LLMs can tackle complex scientific problems, they often fail to emulate the concise, principle-based reasoning employed by human physicists, resorting instead to lengthy and opaque computational approaches. The paper evaluates several state-of-the-art LLMs using zero-shot, hint-based, and no-computation prompts, revealing a consistent failure to align with expert-like reasoning paths, indicating a gap in their ability to apply fundamental principles efficiently and interpretable. The paper advocates for developing AI systems with more robust, efficient, and transparent principle-based scientific reasoning capabilities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the explicit focus on *principle-based reasoning* in physics.  While other physics benchmarks exist, they often focus on factual knowledge, computational skills, or domain-specific knowledge. *PhySense* attempts to isolate and evaluate the ability of LLMs to apply simplifying principles, which is a subtle but crucial aspect of expert-level physics problem-solving. The dataset is crafted based on the physics principle it tests, unlike the common practice of curating/repurposing real-world exam questions. The annotation and design criteria (difficulty rating, solution constraints, conciseness) also demonstrate a careful approach. However, the specific types of problems chosen, while diverse, might not cover the entirety of what is considered "principle-based reasoning" across all areas of physics.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the identification and quantification of a crucial limitation in current LLMs: their tendency to miss simple, elegant solutions in favor of more computationally intensive methods. If LLMs are to become truly useful tools for scientific discovery, they need to be able to reason like experts, not just perform calculations. Highlighting this gap with a dedicated benchmark is a significant step. Furthermore, the different prompting strategies ("Hint", "No-Comp") provide insights into *why* LLMs struggle (e.g., a failure to prioritize principle-driven approaches even when computation is discouraged). The demonstration of human experts' token usage is an excellent comparison point, as well, highlighting the gap that still exists in reasoning efficiency. However, the paper acknowledges limitations, primarily that *PhySense* is text-based, and therefore doesn't assess multi-modal reasoning. Finally, the paper provides clear guidelines, directions and future work to address the current performance gap, enhancing the novelty and significance of this work.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear definition of principle-based reasoning and its importance in physics.
    *   Well-designed benchmark dataset with diverse problem types and difficulty levels.
    *   Systematic evaluation of LLMs using different prompting strategies.
    *   Quantifiable metrics for accuracy and token efficiency (reasoning complexity).
    *   Insightful analysis of the LLMs' limitations and potential directions for improvement.
    *   The creation and thorough testing of different prompting techniques.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The benchmark is currently limited to text-based problems, excluding potentially important aspects of physics reasoning that involve diagrams, visualisations, or real-world experiments (multi-modality).
    *   The specific choice of physical principles, while representative, might not be exhaustive. There could be other fundamental reasoning patterns that are not explicitly addressed.
    *   The paper does not provide a detailed analysis of the specific types of errors LLMs make when applying principles. A deeper dive into the error patterns could provide more targeted guidance for future research.
    *   It does not explicitly provide details on how *PhySense* distinguishes from already existing related works such as FEABench, CURIE, TheoremQA and OlympicBench.

**Justification of the Score:**

*PhySense* is a valuable contribution to the growing field of LLMs for scientific discovery. It identifies and quantifies a key limitation – the lack of principle-based reasoning – that needs to be addressed for LLMs to become truly effective tools in physics. While it acknowledges the limitation of only testing principle-based reasoning and future expansion to additional methods, it provides clear and concise guidance with the different prompting techniques and reasoning skills it tests in *PhySense*. The paper is well-written, the methodology is sound, and the results are insightful. While acknowledging the limitations and opportunities for future work, this paper has provided critical contributions and is assigned a score of:

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### LegalEval-Q: A New Benchmark for The Quality Evaluation of LLM-Generated Legal Text
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24826v1)
- **Authors**: Li yunhan, Wu gengshen
- **Abstract**: As large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used in legal applications, current evaluation benchmarks tend to focus mainly on factual accuracy while largely neglecting important linguistic quality aspects such as clarity, coherence, and terminology. To address this gap, we propose three steps: First, we develop a regression model to evaluate the quality of legal texts based on clarity, coherence, and terminology. Second, we create a specialized set of legal questions. Third, we analyze 49 LLMs using this evaluation framework. Our analysis identifies three key findings: First, model quality levels off at 14 billion parameters, with only a marginal improvement of $2.7\%$ noted at 72 billion parameters. Second, engineering choices such as quantization and context length have a negligible impact, as indicated by statistical significance thresholds above 0.016. Third, reasoning models consistently outperform base architectures. A significant outcome of our research is the release of a ranking list and Pareto analysis, which highlight the Qwen3 series as the optimal choice for cost-performance tradeoffs. This work not only establishes standardized evaluation protocols for legal LLMs but also uncovers fundamental limitations in current training data refinement approaches. Code and models are available at: https://github.com/lyxx3rd/LegalEval-Q.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "LegalEval-Q: A New Benchmark for The Quality Evaluation of LLM-Generated Legal Text" addresses the gap in existing LLM evaluation benchmarks, which primarily focus on factual accuracy and reasoning while neglecting textual quality aspects like clarity, coherence, and terminology, especially in the legal domain. The authors propose a new benchmark consisting of a regression model for evaluating legal text quality, a specialized set of legal questions across various subdomains, and an analysis of 49 LLMs using this framework.  The study identifies a performance plateau beyond 14 billion parameters, limited impact from quantization and context length engineering choices, and consistent outperformance of reasoning models over base architectures. They release a ranking list and Pareto analysis, highlighting the Qwen3 series as a cost-effective option. The authors aim to establish standardized evaluation protocols for legal LLMs and uncover limitations in current training data refinement approaches.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its specific focus on *textual quality* within the legal domain for LLM evaluation. Existing benchmarks concentrate heavily on factual accuracy and reasoning. The creation of a benchmark centered on clarity, coherence, and legal terminology is a valuable contribution. The investigation into factors beyond sheer scale is also a positive point.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the increasing deployment of LLMs in legal applications. Assessing the linguistic quality of generated legal text is paramount, as ambiguity or incoherence can have serious consequences. The paper provides a practical tool and actionable insights for practitioners selecting LLMs for legal tasks. The Pareto analysis offers a cost-benefit perspective, which is especially useful for resource-constrained deployments.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies a gap in existing evaluation methodologies.
    *   **Comprehensive Methodology:** The three-step approach (regression model, specialized questions, LLM analysis) is well-structured and rigorous.
    *   **Empirical Analysis:** The analysis of 49 LLMs provides substantial empirical evidence.
    *   **Actionable Insights:** The findings offer practical guidance for model selection and deployment in legal contexts.
    *   **Resource Release:** Making the code, models, and ranking lists publicly available enhances the impact of the work.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:** While the focus on legal text is a strength, it also limits the generalizability of the findings to other specialized domains. The degree of domain-specific refinement needed is not explicitly discussed, nor how to generalise the approach.
    *   **Subjectivity in Quality Assessment:**  Evaluating "clarity" and "coherence" can be subjective, even with a defined rubric. The process of generating the comments, conclusions, and scores requires careful quality control and inter-annotator agreement to ensure reliability. The degree of inter-annotator agreement is not explicitly discussed, introducing an element of ambiguity. The dependency on LLMs to generate ground truth is concerning and can also induce a bias.

*   **Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in LLM evaluation, particularly in specialized domains. It could encourage the development of more nuanced evaluation metrics beyond factual accuracy. Practitioners might adopt the LegalEval-Q framework for selecting and fine-tuning LLMs for legal applications.

*   **Justification for Score:** The paper addresses an important problem and offers a well-executed methodology with practical insights. However, the subjective nature of text quality assessment and the legal-specific focus introduce limitations. There are no baselines provided or a comparison with current similar works that evaluate legal datasets. Furthermore, there is limited contribution towards explainability of the generated texts. Therefore, while the work is valuable, its impact is likely to be localized and incremental rather than transformative.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Improving Reliability and Explainability of Medical Question Answering through Atomic Fact Checking in Retrieval-Augmented LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24830v1)
- **Authors**: Juraj Vladika, Annika Domres, Mai Nguyen, Rebecca Moser, Jana Nano, Felix Busch, Lisa C. Adams, Keno K. Bressem, Denise Bernhardt, Stephanie E. Combs, Kai J. Borm, Florian Matthes, Jan C. Peeken
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) exhibit extensive medical knowledge but are prone to hallucinations and inaccurate citations, which pose a challenge to their clinical adoption and regulatory compliance. Current methods, such as Retrieval Augmented Generation, partially address these issues by grounding answers in source documents, but hallucinations and low fact-level explainability persist. In this work, we introduce a novel atomic fact-checking framework designed to enhance the reliability and explainability of LLMs used in medical long-form question answering. This method decomposes LLM-generated responses into discrete, verifiable units called atomic facts, each of which is independently verified against an authoritative knowledge base of medical guidelines. This approach enables targeted correction of errors and direct tracing to source literature, thereby improving the factual accuracy and explainability of medical Q&A. Extensive evaluation using multi-reader assessments by medical experts and an automated open Q&A benchmark demonstrated significant improvements in factual accuracy and explainability. Our framework achieved up to a 40% overall answer improvement and a 50% hallucination detection rate. The ability to trace each atomic fact back to the most relevant chunks from the database provides a granular, transparent explanation of the generated responses, addressing a major gap in current medical AI applications. This work represents a crucial step towards more trustworthy and reliable clinical applications of LLMs, addressing key prerequisites for clinical application and fostering greater confidence in AI-assisted healthcare.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel framework for improving the reliability and explainability of medical question answering (Q&A) systems that use retrieval-augmented large language models (LLMs). This framework, called "Atomic Fact Checking," decomposes LLM-generated responses into individual atomic facts, verifies these facts against an authoritative vector database of medical knowledge, corrects inaccurate facts, and then rewrites the complete response. The authors evaluate their framework through multi-reader medical expert assessments and automated benchmarks. The results demonstrate significant improvements in factuality, overall answer quality, and explainability compared to baseline RAG-based LLM systems.  Importantly, the authors find the method particularly beneficial for smaller LLMs. The framework also enhances explainability by tracing each fact back to its source material.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The concept of atomic fact-checking within a RAG framework has some precedence in general knowledge domains (as referenced in the paper). However, its application to the specific and critical domain of medical Q&A, with the explicit goal of enhancing both reliability and explainability, contributes to its novelty. The framework's design is also novel: a pipeline to generate answers from the LLM, identify atomic facts, find correct data chunks and correct the original answer. The decomposition and iterative verification process provide a significant step forward. The focus on creating "explainable" answers by explicitly linking each fact back to its source is also a strong point of novelty, which is a required aspect for medical application of LLMs.

* **Significance:** The significance stems from the pressing need for reliable and trustworthy medical information. The current generation of LLMs, while powerful, are prone to hallucinations, which pose a significant risk in healthcare settings. The paper addresses this risk directly and provides a method to mitigate it, while improving explainability and transparency. The finding that the framework is especially beneficial for smaller LLMs is significant, as it could enable the deployment of trustworthy medical AI on resource-constrained platforms and in settings where data privacy concerns restrict access to larger models. The framework's modularity (atomic fact decomposition, individual verification, and re-writing) makes it adaptable to various medical knowledge bases and question types.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of unreliable medical advice from LLMs and the need for explainable solutions.
    * **Well-Defined Framework:** The atomic fact-checking framework is well-defined and clearly explained.
    * **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The combination of medical expert evaluation and automated benchmarks provides a robust assessment of the framework's performance. Using the AMEGA benchmark is particularly good, as it is purpose-built for medical LLMs. Using real world medical questions (from a tumor board) increases the significance of the work.
    * **Focus on Explainability:** The explicit focus on linking facts to their sources contributes significantly to the trustworthiness and acceptance of the system by medical professionals.
    * **Positive Results:** The improvements in factuality and overall answer quality are statistically significant, demonstrating the effectiveness of the framework.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Dependency on LLM Generation:** The framework's performance is contingent on the quality of the initial LLM-generated response. If the initial response is fundamentally flawed or contains significant knowledge gaps, the fact-checking process may not be able to fully correct it. This may introduce biases in the fact checking.
    * **Potential for Information Loss:** The process of decomposing the response into atomic facts and rewriting it could lead to the loss of nuance or context, particularly in complex medical scenarios. The potential for data loss (or altered meaning) needs to be investigated more deeply.
    * **Limited Scope:** The evaluation is focused on specific types of cancer questions. While the results are promising, further evaluation across a broader range of medical specialties and question types is necessary to establish the generalizability of the framework.
    * **Scalability:** The paper doesn't explicitly address the computational cost and scalability of the atomic fact-checking process, particularly for large-scale deployments with a high volume of user queries.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the development of reliable and trustworthy medical AI systems. The framework provides a practical approach to mitigating the risks associated with LLM hallucinations and improving the transparency and explainability of their responses. The focus on smaller LLMs could facilitate broader adoption of medical AI in resource-constrained settings.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of medical AI. While the concept of fact-checking isn't entirely new, its application to medical Q&A, combined with the focus on explainability and the finding that the method benefits smaller models, makes it valuable. The paper is well-written, the framework is clearly defined, and the evaluation is comprehensive. However, the paper has limitations regarding dependency on LLM generation, potential information loss, limited scope, and scalability. These weaknesses prevent it from achieving a higher score. Even so, the potential influence of this work is substantial.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### VideoCAD: A Large-Scale Video Dataset for Learning UI Interactions and 3D Reasoning from CAD Software
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24838v1)
- **Authors**: Brandon Man, Ghadi Nehme, Md Ferdous Alam, Faez Ahmed
- **Abstract**: Computer-Aided Design (CAD) is a time-consuming and complex process, requiring precise, long-horizon user interactions with intricate 3D interfaces. While recent advances in AI-driven user interface (UI) agents show promise, most existing datasets and methods focus on short, low-complexity tasks in mobile or web applications, failing to capture the demands of professional engineering tools. In this work, we introduce VideoCAD, the first attempt at engineering UI interaction learning for precision tasks. Specifically, VideoCAD is a large-scale synthetic dataset consisting of over 41K annotated video recordings of CAD operations, generated using an automated framework for collecting high-fidelity UI action data from human-made CAD designs. Compared to existing datasets, VideoCAD offers an order of magnitude higher complexity in UI interaction learning for real-world engineering tasks, having up to a 20x longer time horizon than other datasets. We show two important downstream applications of VideoCAD: learning UI interactions from professional precision 3D CAD tools and a visual question-answering (VQA) benchmark designed to evaluate multimodal large language models' (LLM) spatial reasoning and video understanding abilities. To learn the UI interactions, we propose VideoCADFormer - a state-of-the-art model in learning CAD interactions directly from video, which outperforms multiple behavior cloning baselines. Both VideoCADFormer and the VQA benchmark derived from VideoCAD reveal key challenges in the current state of video-based UI understanding, including the need for precise action grounding, multi-modal and spatial reasoning, and long-horizon dependencies.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the VideoCAD paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces VideoCAD, a large-scale synthetic dataset of CAD software interactions. It contains over 41,000 annotated video recordings of CAD operations generated from human-made CAD designs using Onshape. The dataset captures long-horizon UI interactions and 3D reasoning, addressing a gap in existing UI interaction datasets that primarily focus on simpler web and mobile applications. The authors demonstrate two downstream applications of VideoCAD: (1) training a novel transformer-based model called VideoCADFormer for UI interaction learning in CAD software, which outperforms existing baselines, and (2) introducing a VQA benchmark, VideoCADQA, to evaluate the spatial reasoning and video understanding abilities of multimodal LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Novelty:** VideoCAD addresses a significant gap in the field by providing a large-scale, high-fidelity dataset specifically designed for CAD software interactions. This is a significant departure from existing datasets which primarily focus on web and mobile interfaces or lack procedural information. This makes the contribution substantial within the field of learning user interfaces from CAD software.
*   **Significance:** The dataset has the potential to advance research in several areas, including AI-driven UI navigation, software automation, CAD generation, and the evaluation of LLMs for spatial reasoning. It could provide a valuable resource for developing more intelligent and user-friendly CAD tools and automated workflow, or as training data for automation strategies.
*   **Methodology:** The automated framework for generating the dataset from existing CAD designs and mapping them to UI actions is well-described and robust. The implementation of quality control metrics and the curation effort ensure that the dataset is more than just a large collection of data, but that is is high quality and accurate.
*   **Downstream Applications:** Demonstrating the utility of the dataset through VideoCADFormer and VideoCADQA strengthens the paper's impact. VideoCADFormer's state-of-the-art performance on CAD UI interaction learning and the VQA benchmark highlights the challenges in current LLM capabilities for spatial and temporal reasoning.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Synthetic Data:** The synthetic nature of the dataset is a significant limitation. While the authors attempt to introduce human-like heuristics into the data generation process, it may not fully capture the complexities and nuances of real-world user interactions, such as errors or unexpected strategies. As a result, models trained on VideoCAD might not generalize perfectly to human users.
*   **Limited Scope:** The dataset focuses primarily on sketch-extrude workflows within Onshape, which, while common, do not represent the full range of operations available in CAD software. It also omits advanced operations like fillets, sweeps, and lofts, which could limit the dataset's applicability to more complex modeling tasks. There is an inherent limitation of relying on a single platform and its particular tool sets.
*   **Validation:** The performance of the models are limited by relying on benchmarks created by the dataset's creators. Further validation using external benchmarks would solidify the work.

**Overall Impact and Score:**

VideoCAD makes a valuable contribution by providing the first large-scale dataset specifically designed for CAD software UI interaction learning. The dataset's unique characteristics, such as long-horizon tasks and 3D reasoning requirements, address a gap in existing resources and open up new avenues for research. However, the synthetic nature of the data and the limited scope of CAD operations represent significant limitations that could hinder the generalization of models trained on VideoCAD to real-world scenarios.

Despite these limitations, VideoCAD's potential impact on the field justifies a high score. It addresses a relevant problem and provides a valuable resource for the community.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Vision LLMs Are Bad at Hierarchical Visual Understanding, and LLMs Are the Bottleneck
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24840v1)
- **Authors**: Yuwen Tan, Yuan Qing, Boqing Gong
- **Abstract**: This paper reveals that many state-of-the-art large language models (LLMs) lack hierarchical knowledge about our visual world, unaware of even well-established biology taxonomies. This shortcoming makes LLMs a bottleneck for vision LLMs' hierarchical visual understanding (e.g., recognizing Anemone Fish but not Vertebrate). We arrive at these findings using about one million four-choice visual question answering (VQA) tasks constructed from six taxonomies and four image datasets. Interestingly, finetuning a vision LLM using our VQA tasks reaffirms LLMs' bottleneck effect to some extent because the VQA tasks improve the LLM's hierarchical consistency more than the vision LLM's. We conjecture that one cannot make vision LLMs understand visual concepts fully hierarchical until LLMs possess corresponding taxonomy knowledge.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the ability of Vision Large Language Models (VLLMs) to perform hierarchical visual understanding, specifically focusing on their consistency in classifying images within taxonomic hierarchies. The authors create a large dataset of visual question answering (VQA) tasks based on six taxonomies and four image datasets. Their experiments reveal that current VLLMs struggle with hierarchical consistency, often failing to correctly classify images along the entire path from root to leaf nodes within a taxonomy. Through probing experiments, they identify the Large Language Model (LLM) component of VLLMs as the bottleneck, lacking sufficient taxonomic knowledge about the visual world. Finetuning the VLLM using their VQA tasks improves the LLM's text-based consistency more than the VLLM's visual consistency, further solidifying this conclusion. The authors conjecture that truly hierarchical visual understanding requires LLMs to possess corresponding taxonomic knowledge.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's primary contribution is in identifying the LLM component as a significant bottleneck in hierarchical visual understanding within VLLMs. While prior works have observed limitations in VLLMs for fine-grained image classification and inconsistencies in taxonomic classification, this paper offers a focused analysis on hierarchical consistency, providing compelling evidence for the LLM knowledge gap.  The construction of a large VQA dataset specifically designed to evaluate hierarchical consistency is also a valuable contribution.

* **Significance:** The findings are significant because they highlight a fundamental limitation in current VLLM architectures. This limitation has implications for applications that require reasoning across different levels of granularity, such as biodiversity monitoring, medical image analysis, and robotics. By identifying the LLM as the bottleneck, the paper points to a clear direction for future research: improving the taxonomic knowledge encoded within LLMs or exploring alternative architectural approaches that better integrate visual and linguistic information. The study's well-defined experimental setup, clear metrics, and ablation studies further strengthen its significance. The results suggest that simply scaling up model size may not address this issue and that a more targeted approach to knowledge integration is needed. The demonstration that finetuning improves the LLM's text component consistency more than the VLLM's visual consistency is compelling evidence.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of hierarchical visual understanding and the importance of hierarchical consistency.
    * **Well-Designed Experiments:** The VQA task design is suitable for evaluating hierarchical understanding in a closed-set setting.  The probing experiments are insightful in isolating the LLM bottleneck.
    * **Comprehensive Analysis:**  The paper explores various potential causes for the poor performance and provides evidence to support its conclusion about the LLM bottleneck.
    * **Large-Scale Evaluation:** The use of a large dataset and evaluation across multiple models and datasets provides robustness to the findings.
    * **Clear writing and organization.**

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Scope of Taxonomies:** While the paper uses six taxonomies, there could be other types of hierarchical organization that are not explored. It would be interesting to see how these models behave with other types of hierarchies outside of biology and general image understanding.
    * **Closed-Set VQA:** The closed-set VQA task, while helpful for isolating the problem, might not fully reflect real-world scenarios where the answer space is open. It would be helpful to extend these insights to open-ended generation settings to fully assess how this impacts models outside the lab.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in VLLMs by:
    * Shifting focus towards improving the knowledge representation and reasoning capabilities of LLMs within VLLMs.
    * Encouraging the development of new architectures or training techniques that better integrate visual and linguistic information hierarchically.
    * Motivating the creation of new datasets and benchmarks specifically designed to evaluate hierarchical visual understanding.
    * Informing the design of more robust and reliable AI systems for applications that require reasoning across different levels of abstraction.

**Score: 8**

**Justification:** This paper makes a strong contribution by identifying the LLM component as the main bottleneck for hierarchical visual understanding in VLLMs. The experimental design is thorough and provides convincing evidence. The findings are significant and have the potential to influence future research directions within the field. While there are some limitations regarding the specific types of hierarchies and reliance on closed-set VQA tasks, the paper is well-written, well-executed, and offers valuable insights. It could be a '9' if the contribution opened up and explored a new path of exploration and this paper only points to the issue.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Chameleon: A Flexible Data-mixing Framework for Language Model Pretraining and Finetuning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24844v1)
- **Authors**: Wanyun Xie, Francesco Tonin, Volkan Cevher
- **Abstract**: Training data mixtures greatly impact the generalization performance of large language models. Existing domain reweighting methods often rely on costly weight computations and require retraining when new data is introduced. To this end, we introduce a flexible and efficient data mixing framework, Chameleon, that employs leverage scores to quantify domain importance within a learned embedding space. We first construct a domain affinity matrix over domain embeddings. The induced leverage scores determine a mixture that upweights domains sharing common representations in embedding space. This formulation allows direct transfer to new data by computing the new domain embeddings. In experiments, we demonstrate improvements over three key scenarios: (i) our computed weights improve performance on pretraining domains with a fraction of the compute of existing methods; (ii) Chameleon can adapt to data changes without proxy retraining, boosting few-shot reasoning accuracies when transferred to new data; (iii) our method enables efficient domain reweighting in finetuning, consistently improving test perplexity on all finetuning domains over uniform mixture. Our code is available at https://github.com/LIONS-EPFL/Chameleon.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CHAMELEON, a novel and efficient data-mixing framework for language model pretraining and finetuning. CHAMELEON utilizes kernel ridge leverage scores (KRLS) computed from domain embeddings to quantify the importance of each domain within the training data. The framework constructs a domain affinity matrix and uses leverage scores to upweight domains with distinct representations during finetuning and leverages the inverse KRLS for pretraining to promote a general understanding of the data. CHAMELEON aims to address the limitations of existing domain reweighting methods, such as their high computational cost and inflexibility in adapting to domain changes. The paper demonstrates through experiments that CHAMELEON improves performance on pretraining and finetuning tasks, adapts to new data efficiently, and offers a more accessible solution for researchers with limited resources.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the application of KRLS to domain reweighting for LLMs, especially across both pretraining and finetuning stages. Existing methods often rely on proxy model training or manual selection. CHAMELEON's data-centric approach, using KRLS on learned embeddings, offers a unique perspective. The idea of using inverse KRLS for pretraining to emphasize general knowledge is also a novel twist. *However*, the use of KRLS itself isn't entirely new in machine learning, and the novelty is more in its application to the specific problem of data mixing in LLMs.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work lies in its potential to reduce the computational burden of data mixing, making it more accessible. The method adapts well to domain changes without proxy retraining, which is crucial in real-world dynamic training environments.
